# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Number of papers in this paper set: 305

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Cressman, R., Song, J. W., Zhang, B. Y., & Tao, Y. (2012). Cooperation and evolutionary dynamics in the public goods game with institutional incentives. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that institutional incentives (reward, punishment, or both) can fundamentally alter the Nash equilibrium and evolutionary outcomes of the public goods game. Without incentives, the only stable outcome is full defection (zero contribution, zero efficiency). With sufficient institutional reward, partial cooperation emerges and group efficiency increases, but full cooperation is not stable. With sufficient punishment, full cooperation can be maintained, but it cannot invade a population of defectors. The combination of reward and punishment leads to full cooperation and maximal efficiency as the unique globally stable outcome. Theoretical results are supported by simulations of replicator dynamics, showing that the mean contribution (and thus efficiency) increases with the strength of incentives, and that the combined scheme is most robust.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling institutional punishment (and/or reward) in a standard public goods game increases group efficiency relative to the no-incentive baseline. The effect size and stability depend on the magnitude of the incentive, the group size, and the specific incentive scheme. For the canonical parameter set (n=4, r=1.6, E=20, A=20), institutional punishment alone can maintain full cooperation if established, but cannot invade defection; reward alone increases cooperation but not to full efficiency; the combination achieves full efficiency. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar design dimensions, especially for continuous contribution, no chat, known group size, and institutional (not peer) incentives. The findings are less directly applicable to repeated games or peer-sanctioning settings.

- source: Perc, M., Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., Wang, Z., Boccaletti, S., & Szolnoki, A. (2017). Statistical physics of human cooperation. *Physics Reports-review Section of Physics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment in public goods games can increase group efficiency and sustain cooperation, especially when punishment is not too costly and is effective at deterring defectors. However, the effect is not universally positive: high punishment costs, the presence of antisocial punishment, or poorly tuned parameters can lead to lower efficiency or even the collapse of cooperation. Adaptive and probabilistic punishment mechanisms can improve efficiency by reducing unnecessary costs. The spatial structure of interactions (e.g., lattices, networks) is critical: it allows for clustering of cooperators and punishers, which can support higher efficiency at lower multiplication factors (mpcr) than in well-mixed populations. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing the regions where punishment increases, decreases, or has no effect on efficiency, depending on the interplay of punishment cost, effectiveness, group size, and synergy factor.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly sensitive to game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, mpcr, punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control only when punishment is not too costly and is sufficiently effective, and when the spatial structure allows for clustering of cooperators/punishers. If punishment is costly or antisocial punishment is possible, efficiency gains may be lost or reversed. The paper's phase diagrams and model descriptions can be used to map specific game designs to expected efficiency outcomes, given the control efficiency. However, the results are based on theoretical models and simulations, not direct empirical measurement, so predictions should be interpreted with caution and in the context of the model's assumptions.

- source: Fehr, E., & Gintis, H. (2007). Human motivation and social cooperation: Experimental and analytical foundations. *Annual Review of Sociology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, cooperation and group efficiency decline over time when punishment is not available, as self-regarding individuals free ride and conditional cooperators reduce their contributions in response. When a costly punishment mechanism is introduced, cooperation and group efficiency increase dramatically, approaching full cooperation and maximum group earnings. This effect is robust even when punishment is costly and when group composition changes to prevent repeated interactions. The review also highlights that a minority of strong reciprocators can sustain high efficiency when punishment is possible, but even a majority cannot do so without punishment. The findings are supported by experimental evidence from Fehr & Gächter (2000, 2002) and others.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly punishment in public goods games substantially increases group efficiency, even when punishment is costly to the punisher and when group composition is randomized. The effect is robust across different group sizes and settings. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency from low or declining levels (in the control) to near-maximal levels (in the treatment), provided that punishment is sufficiently effective and visible. The review also suggests that the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are important moderators, but the main qualitative result is that punishment enables high efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2013). Correlation of Positive and Negative Reciprocity Fails to Confer an Evolutionary Advantage: Phase Transitions to Elementary Strategies. *Physical Review X*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game, punishment is generally the most effective strategy for promoting cooperation and group efficiency, outperforming both reward and the combined punishment+reward strategy except in very narrow parameter regions. The combined strategy (B) can survive and even outperform elementary strategies only when the cost of punishment/reward is extremely low relative to their impact, and/or when the synergy factor is low (i.e., cooperation is otherwise hard to sustain). However, these conditions are considered unrealistic for most practical or experimental settings. The results suggest that adding reward to punishment does not generally increase group efficiency beyond what punishment alone achieves, and that the evolutionary advantage of combining positive and negative reciprocity is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, enabling punishment (with or without reward) generally increases group efficiency compared to no punishment, especially when the cost of punishment is not prohibitively high. However, adding reward to punishment does not further increase efficiency except in very narrow, low-cost parameter regions. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with reasonable cost and effectiveness) should be expected to increase efficiency, but the marginal benefit of also enabling reward is likely negligible except under harsh conditions (low MPCR, low cost). The results are most directly applicable to spatial (networked) PGGs with group size 5, continuous contributions, and symmetric cost/impact for punishment and reward.

- source: Duong, M. H., & Han, T. A. (2021). Cost efficiency of institutional incentives for promoting cooperation in finite populations. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in finite-population PGGs, the expected total cost to an institution of achieving a minimum cooperation level via punishment or reward can be exactly calculated and optimized. The cost efficiency of punishment versus reward depends on the required cooperation threshold: for low cooperation targets, reward is more cost-efficient; for high targets, punishment is more cost-efficient, with the threshold typically around 50% cooperation. The cost functions exhibit phase transitions as the intensity of selection changes. The analysis provides explicit formulas and algorithms for determining the optimal incentive size and minimal institutional cost, given the game parameters, population size, and selection intensity. The results are validated by numerical simulations and apply to both the PGG and the donation game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, theoretically grounded formulas for predicting the efficiency (cost-effectiveness) of institutional punishment (and reward) in finite-population PGGs, as a function of player count, group size, marginal per-capita return, selection intensity, and the required cooperation level. It shows that the efficiency of punishment depends critically on the required cooperation threshold and the selection intensity. For prediction tasks, the paper enables calculation of the minimal institutional cost (and thus efficiency) for any specified game design, provided the relevant parameters are known. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the institution can set the punishment/reward level to achieve a target cooperation rate, and where the main outcome of interest is the total cost to the institution (i.e., efficiency in terms of budget spent per unit of cooperation achieved). The findings are less directly applicable to settings where individual payoffs or group welfare (rather than institutional cost) are the main outcome, but the cost formulas can be mapped to group efficiency if the institutional cost is subtracted from total group payoffs.

- source: Chen, X. J., Sasaki, T., Brännström, & Dieckmann, U. (2015). First carrot, then stick: how the adaptive hybridization of incentives promotes cooperation. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional incentives, an adaptive hybrid policy that rewards when cooperation is rare and punishes when cooperation is common ('first carrot, then stick') minimizes the cost of establishing and maintaining cooperation. This policy outperforms pure reward or pure punishment in both effectiveness (range of conditions for full cooperation) and efficiency (cumulative cost to reach/restore cooperation). The findings hold for both well-mixed and spatial populations, and are robust to changes in group size, incentive leverage, spatial structure, and other model details. The hybrid policy is especially efficient at converting a population of defectors to cooperators, requiring less total incentive expenditure than either reward or punishment alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, for a given public goods game structure, the efficiency gain from enabling punishment depends on how punishment is combined with reward. Pure punishment is effective at recovering cooperation but costly for establishing it, while pure reward is the opposite. The adaptive hybrid policy achieves both at lower cost. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is maximized when punishment is used adaptively after cooperation is established, and that the efficiency benefit is greater when the punishment technology is strong relative to reward. The results are robust across a range of game design dimensions, including player count, group size, and spatial structure. However, the results are theoretical and assume institutional (not peer-to-peer) sanctioning, with rational agents and no implementation errors.

- source: Sasaki, T., Brännström, Dieckmann, U., & Sigmund, K. (2012). The take-it-or-leave-it option allows small penalties to overcome social dilemmas. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional punishment, allowing optional participation (the 'take-it-or-leave-it' option) enables even small penalties to establish a globally stable cooperative norm, maximizing group welfare. In contrast, compulsory participation requires much larger penalties to achieve the same effect, and can leave the group trapped in full defection if initial cooperation is low. Rewarding also promotes cooperation, but requires much higher costs to achieve full cooperation and does not eliminate the possibility of stable coexistence between cooperators and defectors. The efficiency gains from punishment with optional participation are robust across several model variants, including user-pays and capped punishment. The key outcome is that punishment, when combined with voluntary participation, is a highly efficient mechanism for overcoming social dilemmas in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public goods games, enabling optional participation (voluntary entry) dramatically increases the efficiency of institutional punishment: much smaller penalties suffice to achieve full cooperation and maximize group welfare. The model is directly relevant to settings where the game design allows players to opt out, and where punishment is administered by an institution rather than peers. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (without punishment), introducing institutional punishment with optional participation will yield a much larger increase in efficiency than with compulsory participation, and that the cost required to achieve this is much lower than for reward-based incentives. The results are robust to variations in group size, cost structure, and punishment allocation, making them broadly applicable to prediction tasks involving these design dimensions.

- source: Gintis, H. (2000). Strong reciprocity and human sociality. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity—cooperation combined with costly punishment of defectors—can sustain high levels of cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games, even when repeated interaction is unlikely. Theoretical results show that the ability to inflict punishment at low cost (high punishment effectiveness) and the presence of a sufficient fraction of strong reciprocators can induce cooperation among self-interested agents, raising group fitness. The model predicts that punishment increases group efficiency, especially when the cost to punishers is low and the impact on defectors is high. Laboratory evidence is reviewed to support these claims, showing that allowing punishment in public goods experiments prevents the decay of cooperation and increases average contributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency, especially when punishment is effective (high impact per cost) and not too costly for punishers. The model identifies key moderators: group size, punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the probability of future interaction. The reviewed experimental evidence (e.g., Fehr & Gächter 2000) shows that punishment prevents the decline of cooperation and can even increase it to near full cooperation, implying a positive effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment will have a more positive effect on efficiency when the punishment technology is strong and the cost is low, and that group size and the structure of repeated interaction also matter.

- source: Wolitzky, A. (2013). Cooperation with Network Monitoring. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games with network monitoring, the maximum sustainable level of cooperation (and thus efficiency) is achieved by grim trigger (punishment) strategies. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation depends on the monitoring network's structure (who observes whom), group size, and the type of public good. For pure public goods, efficiency is typically increasing in group size; for divisible public goods, it is typically decreasing. More connected and less uncertain monitoring networks support higher efficiency. The paper provides explicit formulas for the maximum sustainable efficiency as a function of these design dimensions. Rewards are not necessary for sustaining maximum cooperation; punishment alone suffices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of repeated public goods games with punishment, as a function of game design dimensions. It shows that, with punishment enabled (grim trigger strategies), the maximum sustainable efficiency is determined by the network structure, group size, monitoring technology, and the marginal per-capita return (mpcr). The results are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design dimensions: enabling punishment (grim trigger) increases efficiency up to the robust maximum, which is explicitly characterized. The paper also provides comparative statics for how efficiency changes with group size, network connectivity, and monitoring certainty, all of which are key design dimensions.

- source: Sasaki, T., Uchida, S., & Chen, X. J. (2015). Voluntary rewards mediate the evolution of pool punishment for maintaining public goods in large populations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary public goods games with large populations, pool punishment cannot emerge from rare initial conditions due to the second-order free rider problem. However, if a voluntary reward fund is available and especially if it rewards those who contribute to pool punishment, then pool punishment can emerge and become stable, leading to high group efficiency (i.e., the population converges to all-punisher state). The reward mechanism acts as a mediator, facilitating the transition from free riding to stable punishment-supported cooperation. Once established, pool punishment is self-sustaining and the reward fund is no longer needed. The findings are robust to variations in group size, benefit multipliers, and cost parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of a reward mechanism, especially one that rewards contributors to punishment, is a key moderator for the emergence and stability of efficient outcomes in public goods games with punishment. In the absence of such a reward, punishment alone cannot overcome the second-order free rider problem in large populations, and efficiency remains low. When predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, the presence or absence of a reward mechanism (and its design) is critical. Other dimensions such as group size, MPCR, and cost parameters are explored and found not to qualitatively change the main result: without reward, punishment does not increase efficiency from a free-rider baseline; with reward, punishment can emerge and efficiency increases dramatically.

- source: Rand, D. G., Armao, J. J., Nakamaru, M., & Ohtsuki, H. (2010). Anti-social punishment can prevent the co-evolution of punishment and cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations, allowing anti-social punishment (punishment of cooperators) prevents the evolution of cooperation in public goods games with costly punishment. In models where only pro-social punishment is possible, cooperation and punishment can co-evolve, leading to high group efficiency. However, when anti-social punishment is included, defecting strategies that punish others become dominant, and cooperation is not stable. This result holds across a wide range of parameter values and is robust to small behavioral errors. The findings highlight that the efficiency gains from punishment depend critically on the absence of anti-social punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly contingent on the type of punishment allowed. If anti-social punishment is possible, enabling punishment can reduce or eliminate efficiency gains, as cooperation is not stable and defectors who punish others dominate. In contrast, if only pro-social punishment is possible, enabling punishment can increase efficiency by supporting cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of anti-social punishment as a design dimension is critical: if anti-social punishment is allowed, do not expect efficiency gains from punishment; if it is not allowed, efficiency gains are possible under the right parameter regimes.

- source: Henrich, J., & Boyd, R. (2001). Why people punish defectors - Weak conformist transmission can stabilize costly enforcement of norms in cooperative dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods dilemmas with costly punishment, the combination of payoff-biased and conformist social learning can stabilize both punishment and cooperation, even when punishment is costly and higher-order free-riding is possible. The model demonstrates that, for a finite number of punishment stages, an arbitrarily small amount of conformist transmission is sufficient to stabilize cooperation and punishment, leading to higher group payoffs. The expected payoff of individuals in cooperative groups is strictly higher than in non-cooperative groups, and these high-payoff equilibria can spread through cultural group selection. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation and punishment are stable and when prosocial genetic traits may invade.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling costly punishment in public goods games can increase group efficiency, provided that some mechanism (such as conformist transmission) supports the stability of punishment. The model clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on the costs of cooperation and punishment, the error rate, and the strength of conformist transmission. It also highlights that, in the absence of punishment, cooperation is not stable and efficiency is low. Thus, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, the presence of social learning mechanisms and the cost structure are critical. The model is most informative for settings where group size is large, punishment is costly but effective, and social learning is plausible.

- source: MACY, M. W. (1993). BACKWARD-LOOKING SOCIAL-CONTROL. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulations show that the introduction of social control (punishment/sanctions) in a public goods environment can move a group from a low-efficiency, noncooperative equilibrium to a high-efficiency, cooperative equilibrium. Material sanctions (with moderate cost and linear production) are most effective at achieving and sustaining high efficiency, while moral sanctions are vulnerable to start-up problems unless the cost structure is highly favorable. Internalized (self-imposed) fairness norms can lead to even higher efficiency but risk overcooperation (wasteful excess contribution). The model demonstrates that sanctioning systems can be self-limiting: once high cooperation is achieved, the need for enforcement diminishes. The efficiency gains from punishment depend on the sanctioning regime's cost, production function, and the group's learning rate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in a public goods game can increase group efficiency, especially when the punishment system is not too costly and is effective at inducing compliance. The effect is moderated by the type of sanction (material vs. moral vs. internalized), the cost structure of enforcement, and the learning dynamics of the group. For prediction, the findings suggest that, all else equal, adding a reasonably efficient punishment mechanism to a PGG-like environment will increase efficiency, but the magnitude and sustainability of the effect depend on the details of the punishment technology and cost. The model also highlights the risk of overcooperation with internalized norms and the importance of self-limiting enforcement. The results are most directly applicable to binary (all-or-nothing) contribution games with group sizes around 100, moderate jointness of supply, and no communication.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schmidt, K. M. (1999). A theory of fairness, competition, and cooperation. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that in standard public goods games without punishment, the equilibrium is typically full defection and low efficiency, matching empirical data. When a punishment stage is added (costly to the punisher, but more costly to the punished), the threat of punishment by inequity-averse players can sustain full cooperation and thus high efficiency, even if most players are selfish. The model provides explicit conditions under which this occurs, and shows that the presence of even a single sufficiently inequity-averse 'enforcer' can be enough to sustain efficient cooperation. The theory also predicts that the effect of punishment on efficiency is stronger when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is higher, and when the cost of punishment is lower.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public goods game will increase efficiency, often dramatically, provided there is at least a small fraction of inequity-averse players. The model gives explicit conditions relating group size, mpcr, and punishment cost to the likelihood of efficient cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that, for typical lab PGG designs, adding punishment will shift efficiency from low (near zero) to high (near one), unless punishment is extremely costly or the group is very large and inequity aversion is rare. The model also suggests that the marginal effect of punishment is moderated by mpcr and punishment cost.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2017). Second-Order Free-Riding on Antisocial Punishment Restores the Effectiveness of Prosocial Punishment. *Physical Review X*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games with both prosocial and antisocial punishment, the negative effects of antisocial punishment on cooperation and efficiency are largely neutralized by second-order free-riding among defectors. When the synergy factor (multiplication factor) is high, cooperation is sustained by network reciprocity alone, and antisocial punishment is negligible. When the synergy factor is low, prosocial punishment can still restore cooperation and efficiency, because nonpunishing defectors (second-order free-riders) undermine antisocial punishers, allowing punishing cooperators to dominate. The result is that, across a wide range of parameters, the introduction of punishment increases group efficiency, even in the presence of antisocial punishment. The paper also identifies parameter regions with stable coexistence of strategies, but the main result is that prosocial punishment is robustly effective in structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in spatially structured public goods games, enabling punishment (even when antisocial punishment is possible) will increase group efficiency compared to the control (no punishment) condition, except in cases where punishment is extremely costly or the synergy factor is very low. The results suggest that the presence of antisocial punishment does not undermine the efficiency gains from prosocial punishment in structured populations, due to the protective effect of second-order free-riding. The findings are most directly applicable to games with local interactions, fixed group size (G=5), and continuous contribution choices. The mapping from strategy composition to efficiency is direct, so the phase diagrams and stationary states can be used to predict efficiency outcomes under different parameter settings (e.g., player count, mpcr, punishment cost, punishment effectiveness). The paper does not address chat, communication, or information dimensions, so predictions for those features are not informed.

- source: Perc, M. (2012). Sustainable institutionalized punishment requires elimination of second-order free-riders. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game with pool-punishment, sustainable institutionalized punishment (i.e., a non-negative punishment pool balance and thus maintained group payoff) is only possible if second-order free-riders are eliminated. This elimination occurs via a discontinuous phase transition as the punishment fine increases. If second-order free-riders are not punished sufficiently, the punishment institution cannot be sustained, and efficiency remains low. Notably, even with a significant fraction of defectors, efficiency can be maintained if second-order free-riders are absent. The critical parameters for this transition are the punishment fine (γ), the relative punishment of second-order free-riders (δ), and the synergy factor (r). The model provides phase diagrams showing regions of full, conditional, and unsustainable punishment, directly mapping to efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games with institutionalized punishment is highly sensitive to whether second-order free-riders are sanctioned. If the game design allows for second-order free-riders and does not punish them sufficiently, enabling punishment will not improve efficiency and may even be unsustainable. If the design ensures that second-order free-riders are eliminated (e.g., by punishing them at a sufficient rate), enabling punishment can lead to a discontinuous jump in efficiency, even with a nontrivial fraction of defectors. Thus, for prediction, the presence and treatment of second-order free-riders, the punishment fine, and the synergy factor are critical moderators. The model is most directly relevant to spatial, structured populations with pool-punishment, but the qualitative insight about the necessity of eliminating second-order free-riders for sustainable efficiency is likely to generalize to other institutional punishment settings.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2011). The evolution of antisocial punishment in optional public goods games. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that in optional public goods games, allowing all forms of punishment (including antisocial punishment) leads to the breakdown of the positive effect of punishment on cooperation and group efficiency. Punishment does not increase average payoffs or cooperation rates; instead, it is used by all strategy types to protect against invasion, resulting in a 'tragedy of the commons' where costly punishment is widespread and destructive. Only when punishment is weak (low impact per cost) and the public good is highly efficient (high multiplier) can high cooperation and efficiency be sustained. Theoretical results are supported by large-scale simulations and are robust to parameter variation. Preliminary behavioral experiments are consistent with the model's predictions, but the main results are theoretical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the set of available punishment strategies. If only pro-social punishment (cooperators punishing defectors) is allowed, punishment can greatly increase efficiency. However, if antisocial punishment is possible, punishment does not improve efficiency and can even reduce it. The effect is robust across player count, group size, and a wide range of parameters. The efficiency impact of punishment is more positive when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is high and punishment is weak (low punishment impact per cost). For prediction, the presence of antisocial punishment options and the effectiveness of punishment are key moderators.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Nowak, M. A., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Punishment does not promote cooperation under exploration dynamics when anti-social punishment is possible. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both compulsory and voluntary public goods games, when all forms of punishment are allowed (including anti-social punishment), the introduction of punishment does not increase the average payoff or efficiency of the group under high mutation rates. Analytical results and agent-based simulations show that the average payoff of cooperators is not increased by punishment when anti-social punishment is possible; in fact, the outcome is equivalent to the no-punishment baseline. Only when punishment is restricted to pro-social forms (punishing defectors only) does punishment increase efficiency. These findings are robust to changes in mutation rate and selection intensity.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that in PGG-like environments with high exploration/mutation and the possibility of anti-social punishment, enabling punishment will not increase group efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect of punishment on efficiency is neutral or negative when anti-social punishment is possible, regardless of other game parameters such as player count, rounds, or selection intensity. If the game design allows anti-social punishment, do not expect efficiency gains from enabling punishment.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Mercenary punishment in structured populations. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that mercenary punishment (punishers who do not contribute but punish defectors) can promote cooperation and increase group efficiency in structured populations, even without an additional tax, provided the punishment cost is not too high and the fine is sufficiently large. Introducing a tax to support punishers expands the parameter space where defectors are eliminated and efficiency is high, but only if the punishment cost is significant. If the fine is too low, the tax can actually harm cooperators and allow defectors to coexist with punishers, reducing efficiency. There is an optimal punishment cost that maximizes average payoff. The model also reveals regions of cyclic dominance among strategies, leading to complex spatial dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence on how punishment cost, punishment effectiveness (fine), and the presence of a tax to support punishment interact to determine group efficiency in a spatial public goods game. For prediction, it suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment) if the punishment cost is moderate and the fine is high, especially if a tax is used to support punishers when costs are high. However, if punishment is too costly or the fine is too low, efficiency may not improve or may even decrease. The findings are most applicable to spatially structured games with three strategies (C, D, P), and the results are sensitive to the exact parameter values of punishment cost, fine, and tax. The paper does not provide empirical data but offers detailed phase diagrams and payoff analyses that can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment under varying game design dimensions.

- source: Gintis, H. (2003). Solving the puzzle of prosociality. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, the introduction of costly punishment by strong reciprocators can sustain high levels of cooperation and group efficiency, even when cooperation would otherwise unravel due to self-interest. Theoretical results show that with sufficient oblique cultural transmission and a non-trivial fraction of punishers, full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) can be a stable equilibrium. The paper also reviews experimental evidence showing that when punishment is allowed, group payoffs and cooperation rates are significantly higher than when punishment is not allowed. The model further predicts that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining efficiency depends on the cost and impact of punishment, the fraction of punishers, and the strength of cultural transmission.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and empirical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency relative to control games without punishment, provided that punishment is not prohibitively costly and that a sufficient fraction of players are willing to punish defectors. The model and supporting evidence suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is robust across a range of game parameters, including group size and number of rounds, as long as punishment is effective and not too costly. The paper also highlights the importance of cultural and institutional context (e.g., norm internalization, oblique transmission) in sustaining prosocial behavior and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when punishment is not too costly and is effective at deterring defection.

- source: Boyd, R., Gintis, H., & Bowles, S. (2010). Coordinated Punishment of Defectors Sustains Cooperation and Can Proliferate When Rare. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that coordinated punishment in public goods games can evolve and persist, and when it does, it increases group-average payoffs (efficiency) compared to groups without punishment. The model shows that the cost of punishing a defector decreases as more punishers participate, making punishment more effective and less costly at higher frequencies. The equilibrium with coordinated punishment achieves higher average fitness than the nonpunishing equilibrium, and even modest levels of genetic assortment allow punishment to invade when rare. The model predicts that only some individuals will engage in punishment, but this is sufficient to achieve a large fraction of the maximum possible gains from cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling coordinated punishment in a public goods game will increase group efficiency (payoff) relative to a control without punishment, provided that punishment is coordinated and costs are shared among punishers. The model highlights the importance of coordination, increasing returns to scale in punishment, and the presence of a signaling stage. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that if the game design allows for coordinated punishment (e.g., communication, quorum requirements, or cost-sharing), one should expect a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency. The model also suggests that the effect is robust to group size and persists under plausible levels of assortment, but is sensitive to the cost structure of punishment and the degree of coordination.

- source: Barrett, S. (2016). Coordination vs. voluntarism and enforcement in sustaining international environmental cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods and prisoners' dilemma games, voluntary cooperation and self-enforcing treaties generally fail to achieve efficient outcomes, especially as the number of players increases. Costly punishment can increase contributions but does not necessarily improve group efficiency or payoff, and may even reduce it if punishment is inefficient or leads to counter-punishment. Coordination mechanisms, such as trade restrictions or weakest-link structures, are more effective at achieving efficient outcomes. The paper also notes that the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) affects cooperation rates in experiments, even though theory predicts it should not. Theoretical models are supported by references to experimental findings from other studies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the cost and effectiveness of punishment. If punishment is costly and not highly effective, it may increase contributions but reduce or fail to improve efficiency due to the cost imposed on both punishers and punished. Coordination mechanisms or institutional changes that alter incentives (e.g., trade bans, weakest-link structures) are more likely to improve efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment should not be assumed to increase efficiency unless punishment is highly effective and not subject to costly retaliation. The marginal per-capita return (mpcr) and group size are also important moderators.

- source: Sigmund, K., De Silva, H., Traulsen, A., & Hauert, C. (2010). Social learning promotes institutions for governing the commons. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both peer- and pool-punishment can emerge in voluntary public goods games, but their efficiency and stability differ. Peer-punishment is more efficient (higher average income) when second-order punishment is absent, but is less stable due to vulnerability to second-order free-riders. Pool-punishment, especially with second-order punishment, is more stable but incurs higher costs, leading to lower average group payoff. The model predicts that the introduction of punishment can increase cooperation but often reduces overall efficiency due to the costs of sanctioning, particularly for institutionalized (pool) punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus mixed: it can stabilize cooperation but at a cost to total group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the type of punishment (peer vs. pool), the presence of second-order punishment, and whether participation is voluntary. Punishment can increase cooperation but often reduces efficiency due to its costs, especially for pool-punishment. When predicting treatment efficiency from game design, one should expect that enabling punishment may not always increase efficiency, and may even reduce it if punishment is costly and/or institutionalized. The presence of second-order punishment and voluntary participation are key moderators. The findings are directly relevant for mapping design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment tech, player count, voluntary participation) to expected efficiency outcomes.

- source: Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in a population with probabilistic corrupt enforcers and violators. *Mathematical Models & Methods in Applied Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with pool punishment and probabilistic corruption (bribery), the evolutionary outcome for cooperation and group efficiency depends on the balance between the expected loss to defectors from punishment and the expected bribe received by punishers. If the expected loss from punishment is less than the bribe, a stable coexistence of cooperators, defectors, and punishers is possible, maintaining cooperation and higher efficiency. If the expected loss equals the bribe, the system exhibits neutral cycles (limit cycles) with periodic oscillations in cooperation and efficiency. If the expected loss exceeds the bribe, the system can either converge to all-defection (low efficiency) or to a heteroclinic cycle with cyclic dominance, which may still allow for periods of cooperation. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive when punishment is strong and corruption is weak, but can be neutral or negative when corruption undermines punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is moderated by the effectiveness of punishment relative to the possibility and attractiveness of corruption (bribery). For prediction, if the game design ensures that punishment is costly and effective (high fine, low bribe, low probability of successful bribery), enabling punishment will likely increase efficiency. If the design allows for easy or attractive bribery (high bribe, high probability of bribery, low punishment cost), enabling punishment may not improve and can even reduce efficiency. The model is most relevant for infinite, well-mixed populations and pool punishment settings. The findings are directly informative for mapping game design parameters (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, bribe size, probability of corruption) to expected efficiency outcomes.

- source: Liu, L. J., Wang, S. X., Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2018). Evolutionary dynamics in the public goods games with switching between punishment and exclusion. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a well-mixed infinite population PGG, both peer-based and pool-based punishment and exclusion mechanisms can promote cooperation and increase average group payoff (efficiency) compared to defection. When individuals use a switching strategy—punishing defectors when they are few and excluding them when they are many—there is a stable coexistence of cooperators, defectors, and sanctioning strategists, leading to higher average payoffs. The efficiency gains are maximized at intermediate switching thresholds. Pure punishment (high threshold) leads to dominance of defectors and low efficiency, while pure exclusion (low threshold) can lead to oscillatory coexistence but still supports higher efficiency than no sanctions. The model explicitly tracks average payoff as the key outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that enabling punishment (and/or exclusion) in a standard PGG increases group efficiency (average payoff) compared to the control (no sanctions), especially when the sanctioning mechanism is flexible (switching between punishment and exclusion based on the number of defectors). The effect is sensitive to the threshold for switching, the costs and effectiveness of punishment/exclusion, and the marginal per-capita return (mpcr). The model is fully specified for N=5, r=3, c=1, and various punishment/exclusion costs, and can be used to predict efficiency under different game design dimensions. The findings are highly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design parameters in PGG-like environments.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2009). When does optional participation allow the evolution of cooperation?. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that optional participation only allows punishment to invade and stabilize cooperation (and thus increase efficiency) under specific conditions: (1) loners must have higher payoffs than defectors, (2) two cooperators must have higher per capita payoffs than loners, and (3) the public good must have constant or increasing returns to scale. If these conditions are not met, the system does not support oscillations that allow punishment to invade, and the long-run outcome is either all defectors or all loners, both of which are inefficient. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the game's payoff structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games with optional participation is not universal, but depends on specific game design dimensions: the relative payoffs of loners, defectors, and cooperators, and the returns to scale in public goods production. For prediction, this means that knowing the control efficiency is not sufficient; one must also know whether the game's payoff structure meets the three key conditions identified. If so, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency; if not, punishment may have little or no effect. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions but offers clear qualitative moderators.

- source: Eldakar, O. T., & Wilson, D. S. (2008). Selfishness as second-order altruism. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game, punishment (even when carried out by selfish individuals) can stabilize cooperation and maintain higher group efficiency. Altruistic punishers are vulnerable to being outcompeted by altruistic nonpunishers (second-order free riders), but selfish punishers—who do not contribute but do punish non-contributors—can coexist with altruistic nonpunishers in a stable equilibrium. This equilibrium maintains a higher level of cooperation and group payoff than would be possible without punishment. The stability and efficiency of this equilibrium depend on parameters such as group size, punishment cost, and the marginal per-capita return. As punishment cost increases or group size increases, the frequency of punishers decreases, but the system can still maintain cooperation if selfish nonpunishers are rare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in a public goods game increases group efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is not prohibitively costly and group size is not too large. The model is directly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in games with punishment enabled, as it analytically derives the conditions under which punishment stabilizes cooperation and increases group payoff. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by punishment cost, group size, and the marginal per-capita return. The model also highlights the importance of considering the types of punishers present (altruistic vs. selfish) and the potential for second-order free riding. For prediction, this paper supports a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency, with the caveat that high punishment costs or large group sizes can reduce this benefit.

- source: Hauert, C., Traulsen, A., Brandt, H., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2007). Via freedom to coercion: The emergence of costly punishment. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can choose whether to participate in the public goods game, costly punishment can emerge and become the dominant strategy, leading to high group efficiency and payoffs. In compulsory participation settings, defectors dominate and group efficiency is low, even if punishment is possible. The model predicts that voluntary participation is essential for the evolutionary stability and emergence of punishment as a mechanism for cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameter values for group size, multiplication factor, and baseline income.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling voluntary participation in public goods games with punishment will result in higher group efficiency compared to compulsory participation. The presence of costly punishment alone is not sufficient to ensure high efficiency; the institutional context (voluntary vs. compulsory participation) is a critical moderator. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether players can abstain from participation. Other design dimensions such as punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and MPCR are discussed as parameters, but the main qualitative result is robust to their variation.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Uchida, S. (2013). The evolution of cooperation by social exclusion. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that social exclusion is a more effective and robust mechanism than costly punishment for sustaining cooperation and high efficiency in public goods games. When excluders can effectively and cheaply exclude defectors, the group can reach and maintain the efficient outcome where all contribute and defectors are eliminated. Costly punishment, even when effective, is vulnerable to invasion by non-punishing cooperators (second-order freeriders), leading to a decline in punishers and eventual dominance by defectors, which collapses efficiency. The model demonstrates that social exclusion can solve both the emergence and maintenance problems of cooperation, provided the cost of exclusion is not too high. The results are robust to changes in group size and to stochastic effects. The paper also notes that if monitoring costs are high, or if exclusion is not self-serving, the advantage of social exclusion disappears.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public goods games, enabling a punishment mechanism that takes the form of social exclusion (where defectors are excluded from benefit sharing and the cost of exclusion is not too high) will lead to a large increase in group efficiency compared to the control (no punishment) condition. The effect is robust across group sizes and does not require repeated interaction or reputation. In contrast, enabling costly punishment (where punishers pay to reduce defectors' payoffs) is less likely to increase efficiency, especially in the long run, due to vulnerability to second-order freeriders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a social exclusion mechanism with low cost and high effectiveness should be treated as a strong positive moderator of the efficiency effect of punishment. The specific model parameters (group size, multiplication factor, cost of exclusion/punishment, effectiveness) are all explicitly analyzed, allowing for quantitative predictions if mapped to empirical settings.

- source: Brandt, H., Hauert, C., & Sigmund, K. (2006). Punishing and abstaining for public goods. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a public goods game with the option to abstain (be a loner) and with costly punishment, evolutionary dynamics can lead to either a high-efficiency equilibrium (with cooperators and punishers) or a low-efficiency oscillatory state (with only cooperators, defectors, and loners). The high-efficiency outcome is only reached if punishers are present in sufficient numbers initially; otherwise, the system can become trapped in the low-efficiency state. The model contrasts with previous work that predicted inevitable dominance of punishers. The key finding is that punishment can sustain high efficiency, but its success is contingent and not automatic.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games with abstention (loner option) is context-dependent and can be bistable. If the initial conditions or parameters favor the emergence of punishers, efficiency can be high; otherwise, the system may remain at low efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency unless the game design and initial state support the stability of punishers. The model also clarifies the importance of group size, punishment cost, and the structure of participation (abstention option) in moderating the effect of punishment.

- source: Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2017). Competitions between prosocial exclusions and punishments in finite populations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in finite-population public goods games, exclusion strategies (both peer and pool) consistently outperform punishment strategies (peer and pool) in terms of evolutionary advantage, which is measured by stationary frequencies and average payoffs. Peer exclusion dominates in the absence of second-order sanctioning, while pool exclusion dominates when second-order sanctioning is present. The results hold for both optional and compulsory PGGs and are robust to variations in group size and punishment fine (unless the fine is extremely high). The findings imply that, for a given control efficiency, enabling exclusion (especially peer exclusion) will yield higher group efficiency than enabling punishment, and that the presence of second-order sanctioning shifts the advantage to pool exclusion.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in standard PGGs, enabling exclusion (especially peer exclusion) will increase group efficiency more than enabling punishment, across a wide range of parameter settings. The effect is robust to group size, number of rounds (since the model is evolutionary and not round-based), and the relative costs of punishment and exclusion (as long as they are not extremely imbalanced). The presence of second-order sanctioning is a key moderator: without it, peer exclusion is optimal; with it, pool exclusion is optimal. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment (vs. exclusion), one should expect a positive but smaller efficiency gain than with exclusion, and that the details of sanctioning (peer vs pool, second-order) are critical.

- source: Adami, C., Schossau, J., & Hintze, A. (2016). Evolutionary game theory using agent-based methods. *Physics of Life Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of the public goods game, the introduction of punishment (where cooperators can punish defectors at a cost) shifts the critical synergy parameter (r) required for cooperation to be stable, thus expanding the parameter region where cooperation (and thus higher group efficiency) is observed. The effect is quantitatively predicted by mean-field theory and confirmed by simulations: higher probability or density of punishment lowers the threshold for cooperation. However, because punishment is costly, it creates a second-order dilemma, and the evolutionary dynamics can lead to meta-stable states and hysteresis. When punishment is allowed to evolve, its effect is strongest near the critical point, and in highly cooperative or highly defective regimes, punishment is not maintained by selection. The findings are robust across different group sizes and mutation rates, and the paper provides explicit equations and simulation results for the effect of punishment on group payoff and cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public goods games, enabling punishment increases group efficiency (payoff) in the dilemma region by shifting the critical point for cooperation to lower values of the synergy parameter (r). The effect size and direction depend on the punishment parameters (cost, effectiveness), group size, and whether punishment is fixed or can evolve. For the downstream prediction task, the paper supports the prediction that, controlling for game design dimensions and baseline efficiency, enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency, especially near the cooperation/defection threshold. The paper also highlights that the effect of punishment is context-dependent: it is most effective near the critical point, and less so when cooperation or defection is already dominant. The explicit equations and simulation results can inform quantitative predictions.

- source: Schoenmakers, S., Hilbe, C., Blasius, B., & Traulsen, A. (2014). Sanctions as honest signals - The evolution of pool punishment by public sanctioning institutions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that public sanctioning institutions (pool punishment) can evolve and sustain high levels of cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games, provided their existence is publicly visible. The signaling effect of such institutions deters free-riding and enables opportunistic cooperation, leading to a stable equilibrium with a mix of pool punishers and opportunists. This equilibrium achieves higher average payoffs than regimes without visible punishment or without the option to abstain. Diminishing taxes (cost-sharing among tax payers) further increase efficiency, while second-order punishment eliminates tax evasion but does not increase average payoffs beyond the baseline with only first-order punishment. The efficiency of the punishment technology and the level of public information about the institution are critical for the emergence and stability of efficient cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and public visibility of a punishment institution in a PGG-like environment increases group efficiency (average payoff) compared to control conditions without punishment. The model allows direct mapping to game design dimensions such as player count, punishment cost, punishment technology, and information structure. It predicts that enabling punishment (with public visibility) will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is sufficiently deterrent (fine > cost of cooperation + tax), and when the institution is visible to most or all players. Diminishing the per-person cost of punishment (e.g., via cost-sharing) further increases efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, given knowledge of the control efficiency and the game design parameters.

- source: Archetti, M., Scheuring, I., Hoffman, M., Frederickson, M. E., Pierce, N. E., & Yu, D. W. (2011). Economic game theory for mutualism and cooperation. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in nonlinear public goods games (which are more biologically realistic than linear ones), a mixed equilibrium with both cooperators and defectors can exist, and group payoff (efficiency) can be substantial and close to maximum even without assortment or repeated interactions. The effect of punishment or sanctions (host sanctions) is formally distinguished from partner fidelity feedback, with different evolutionary implications. The models show that the shape of the public good function (nonlinearity) is a key moderator of efficiency and cooperation, and that punishment/sanctions can maintain cooperation, but the resulting equilibrium is generally not fully efficient. The paper does not present new empirical data but provides a theoretical framework for interpreting efficiency and punishment effects in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of game design dimensions, especially the shape of the public good function (linearity vs. nonlinearity), group size, and cost/benefit ratio. It clarifies that punishment/sanctions (host sanctions) can support cooperation and increase group payoff, but the efficiency gain depends on whether the mechanism is true punishment (requiring commitment) or partner fidelity feedback (self-serving host response). The models suggest that in nonlinear public goods games, efficiency can be high even without punishment, and the marginal effect of punishment may be limited by the structure of the game. The paper is highly relevant for interpreting the likely efficiency effects of punishment in both linear and nonlinear public goods games, but does not provide direct empirical estimates.

- source: Fowler, J. H. (2005). Altruistic punishment and the origin of cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper presents a formal evolutionary model of a voluntary public goods game with contributors, defectors, nonparticipants, and altruistic punishers. Without punishment, the population cycles between cooperation, defection, and nonparticipation, and cooperation is not stable. When altruistic punishment is introduced, punishers can invade and eventually dominate the population, leading to a stable equilibrium with high cooperation and higher group payoffs. The model shows that punishment can only enforce strategies that are payoff-improving relative to nonparticipation, and that the presence of punishment eliminates the previously observed cycles and leads to persistent cooperation and higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in voluntary public goods games, enabling punishment leads to higher group efficiency and welfare, provided that the punishment is sufficiently strong and not excessively costly. The model predicts that punishment will eliminate cycles of cooperation and defection, stabilize cooperation, and increase average payoffs relative to the baseline without punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a voluntary PGG-like environment should increase efficiency, but only if the parameters (punishment impact, cost, and net benefit of cooperation) allow for a payoff advantage over nonparticipation. The model also highlights that punishment cannot enforce inefficient or welfare-reducing strategies.

- source: Guala, F. (2012). Reciprocity: Weak or strong? What punishment experiments do (and do not) demonstrate. *Behavioral and Brain Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that laboratory experiments with public goods games and punishment show that while punishment can increase cooperation rates, it often reduces group efficiency due to the destruction of resources. Efficiency gains from punishment are only observed under specific conditions, such as very long repeated interactions with the same group, and even then, the need for punishment diminishes as cooperation stabilizes. In field and ethnographic studies, there is little evidence for the use of costly, uncoordinated punishment to sustain cooperation; instead, low- or no-cost sanctions (gossip, ostracism, coordinated punishment) are prevalent and effective. The paper argues that the external validity of laboratory punishment experiments is limited, and that real-world institutions that sustain cooperation do so by reducing the cost of punishment, extending the time horizon, and improving information flow.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that enabling costly, uncoordinated punishment in public goods games is unlikely to increase efficiency except under narrow conditions (e.g., long repeated games with stable groups). In most laboratory settings, punishment increases cooperation but reduces efficiency due to resource destruction. In real-world or field-like settings, cooperation is more effectively sustained by low-cost, coordinated, or symbolic sanctions, not by costly punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that the presence of costly punishment (especially if uncoordinated and not paired with communication or institutional support) should not be expected to reliably increase efficiency over the control condition, and may even reduce it. Game design dimensions that reduce the cost of punishment, allow communication, or enable coordination are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Kurzban, R., Burton-Chellew, M. N., & West, S. A. (2015). The Evolution of Altruism in Humans. *Annual Review of Psychology, Vol 66*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: This review synthesizes theory and empirical evidence on the evolution of altruism, with a focus on public goods games and the role of punishment. It highlights that punishment mechanisms increase contributions to the public good, but typically reduce overall efficiency or group payoffs because the costs of punishing and being punished are not fully offset by increased cooperation. Exceptions exist, such as when punishment is cheap or the game is played for many rounds, but these are not the norm. The review also notes the existence of 'perverse' punishment (punishing high contributors) and the importance of contextual factors (e.g., possibility of counter-punishment, cultural variation).
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this review suggests that, in most standard lab PGGs, enabling punishment increases cooperation but reduces efficiency due to the direct costs of punishment. However, if punishment is relatively cheap or the game is played for many rounds, efficiency losses may be mitigated or reversed. The review provides strong theoretical and empirical support for the importance of punishment cost, number of rounds, and the structure of punishment opportunities as moderators of the efficiency effect. It also cautions that the presence of counter-punishment or 'perverse' punishment can further reduce efficiency.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2014). Optimal distribution of incentives for public cooperation in heterogeneous interaction environments. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
