  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games on heterogeneous (scale-free) networks, the optimal way to distribute limited institutional punishment (and reward) depends on the network structure, the enhancement factor (r), and whether payoffs are absolute or degree-normalized. For low enhancement factors, distributing punishment/reward equally is optimal for maximizing cooperation. For high enhancement factors, targeting high-degree nodes (punishing or rewarding them more) is optimal, but the direction depends on whether payoffs are absolute or normalized. The main outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, especially in heterogeneous network settings. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) depends on the distribution of punishment, the enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR), and the network structure. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to inferring that higher cooperation rates (under optimal punishment schemes) may translate to higher efficiency, especially when the enhancement factor is high. The findings are most relevant for settings with network heterogeneity and institutional (not peer) punishment.

- source: Wang, S. X., Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Tax-based pure punishment and reward in the public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both decentralized punishment and reward can drive a networked public goods game to high cooperation, but the minimal cumulative cost required depends on the initial cooperation level. If the initial fraction of cooperators is low, reward is more cost-effective; if it is high, punishment is more cost-effective. The study provides analytical and simulation results for the optimal incentive protocols and the conditions under which full cooperation is stable. The main outcome is the cumulative cost of incentives needed to reach a target cooperation level, not group efficiency or total payoff per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in networked public goods games. While it does not report group efficiency directly, it analyzes the cost (incentives spent) required to achieve high cooperation, which is closely related to efficiency when incentive costs are subtracted from group payoffs. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of punishment (in terms of cost to achieve cooperation) depends on initial conditions and the structure of the game (network, group size, synergy factor). For prediction tasks, the results imply that punishment is not always the most efficient intervention; its relative efficiency compared to reward depends on the starting state of the group. The detailed modeling of incentive cost and cooperation dynamics can inform parameterizations of efficiency in similar networked PGG environments.

- source: Perc, M. (2016). Phase transitions in models of human cooperation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment into spatial public goods games can enable the survival and dominance of cooperative strategies under harsher conditions (lower multiplication factors) than in the absence of punishment. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the fine and cost parameters, with phase transitions (sometimes discontinuous) between different strategy compositions. The addition of reward and the interplay of positive and negative reciprocity further enrich the dynamics, leading to complex patterns such as cyclic dominance. However, the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of cooperation and the conditions under which cooperation can persist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that punishment can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when the punishment fine is sufficiently large and the cost is not prohibitive. The models specify how game design parameters (e.g., group size, multiplication factor, punishment fine and cost) affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, because the main outcomes are strategy frequencies and phase transitions rather than explicit efficiency or group payoff, the paper offers only indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. It is most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of punishment-related parameters and for mapping the conditions under which cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) is likely to emerge, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data for direct prediction.

- source: Janssen, M. A., & Bushman, C. (2008). Evolution of cooperation and altruistic punishment when retaliation is possible. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that when agents in a public goods game can retaliate against those who punish them, the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation is greatly reduced. In the absence of retaliation, punishment can support high levels of cooperation (and thus higher group payoffs/efficiency). When retaliation is possible, cooperation and punishment both decline, especially in larger groups or when the cost of retaliation is low relative to the cost of punishment. The visibility of the punisher (i.e., whether punished agents can identify who punished them) is also important: if punishers are less visible, retaliation is less likely and cooperation can be higher. The model also shows that spiteful punishment (defectors punishing others unconditionally) does not undermine the main result: retaliation is the key factor that reduces cooperation and, by implication, efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling punishment in public goods games are highly contingent on whether retaliation is possible and on the relative costs of punishment and retaliation. In environments where punished agents can easily identify and retaliate against punishers, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be much smaller or even negative, especially in larger groups. The model also highlights the importance of punisher anonymity: making it harder to identify punishers can help preserve the efficiency benefits of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of retaliation and punisher identifiability are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Sigmund, K. (2007). Punish or perish? Retaliation and collaboration among humans. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of punishment in public goods games increases cooperation and contributions, but the cost of punishment can reduce average group income, at least initially. Over time, as cooperation becomes the norm and punishment is less frequently used, the group can approach efficient outcomes. However, the efficiency gains depend on the cost-effectiveness of punishment and the dynamics of the group. The review also notes that rewards alone are less effective than punishment in boosting cooperation, but combining indirect reciprocity (reputation-based rewards) with punishment can increase efficiency. Voluntary participation in public goods games with punishment can also enhance cooperation and efficiency compared to compulsory participation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and review-based support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in public goods games, but its effect on efficiency is mixed and context-dependent. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment is likely to increase contributions, but whether this translates into higher efficiency depends on the cost of punishment, the number of rounds, and whether cooperation stabilizes. If punishment is costly and used frequently, efficiency may decrease; if it is used mainly as a threat and actual punishment becomes rare, efficiency can increase. The review also suggests that combining punishment with reputation-based rewards or allowing voluntary participation can improve efficiency. Thus, when predicting efficiency with punishment enabled, consider not only the presence of punishment but also its cost, the game structure, and whether mechanisms exist to reduce the frequency or cost of punishment over time.

- source: Brandt, H., Hauert, C., & Sigmund, K. (2003). Punishment and reputation in spatial public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games with three players, the introduction of punishment opportunities transforms the system from one where cooperators and defectors can coexist (with cooperation only possible above a relatively high threshold r_c ≈ 2.07) to one where the threshold for stable cooperation is much lower (r_c ≈ 1.35 with punishment, and r_c ≈ 1.25 with both punishment and reputation). This means that punishment enables cooperative, group-beneficial outcomes under a wider range of game parameters. The presence of punishment eliminates coexistence and leads to bistability: either a fully cooperative or a fully defecting state, depending on initial conditions. Reputation further enhances cooperation and fairness by making the cooperative state more robust and reducing the prevalence of non-punishing cooperators. The findings are based on evolutionary game-theoretic modeling and simulations, not on laboratory or field experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in spatial public goods games increases the likelihood of efficient, cooperative outcomes, as measured by the range of parameters (specifically, the multiplication factor r) under which cooperation is stable. The reduction in the threshold r_c for cooperation is a clear signal that punishment makes high-efficiency outcomes possible in settings where they would otherwise not occur. While the paper does not report efficiency as a direct ratio of actual to maximal group payoff, the shift in r_c is a strong proxy for increased group welfare. The findings are most applicable to spatial, all-or-nothing, three-player, repeated games with binary contribution and punishment choices, and may be less directly transferable to well-mixed or continuous-contribution settings.

- source: Helbing, D., Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabo, G. (2010). Evolutionary Establishment of Moral and Double Moral Standards through Spatial Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment in spatial public goods games enables the spread and dominance of moralists (cooperators who punish), eliminating both defectors and non-punishing cooperators over time. This resolves the second-order free-rider problem and leads to higher levels of cooperation than would be expected in well-mixed populations, where defectors dominate. The effect depends on the punishment fine-to-cost ratio and the synergy factor (MPCR analog), with higher synergy and/or higher fine-to-cost ratios favoring the spread of moralists. The presence of a small number of defectors can accelerate the spread of moralists. The model shows that spatial structure is crucial: in well-mixed populations, punishment does not prevent the tragedy of the commons, but in spatial settings, punishment can establish and maintain cooperation. While the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, the dominance of moralists and elimination of defectors implies a more efficient outcome than the baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in spatially structured public goods games can substantially increase group efficiency by promoting the dominance of punishing cooperators (moralists) and eliminating defectors and non-punishing cooperators. The effect is sensitive to the punishment fine-to-cost ratio and the synergy factor (MPCR), but is robust to group size and network structure as long as spatial clustering exists. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with sufficient effectiveness and not too high cost) in a spatial or networked setting should be expected to increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when the control is dominated by defectors. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so predictions should be qualitative or based on the expected dominance of cooperative strategies.

- source: Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2014). Probabilistic sharing solves the problem of costly punishment. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that probabilistic sharing of punishment duties in a public goods game can solve the problem of costly punishment and promote cooperation, even when punishment is expensive. In well-mixed populations, probabilistic sanctioning transforms the game into a coordination game with two stable equilibria (full cooperation and full defection). In structured populations, sharing punishment duties leads to high cooperation rates, but only if the severity and frequency of punishment are carefully balanced; both too little and too much punishment can reduce cooperation. The main reported outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. Theoretical payoff expressions are provided, and the analysis explains why intermediate punishment is optimal for cooperation, but efficiency is not directly measured or reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the way punishment is implemented (shared probabilistically or periodically) can have a major impact on cooperation rates in public goods games. While the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency, the derived payoff expressions and analysis of invasion dynamics suggest that intermediate levels of punishment severity and frequency can maximize group welfare by supporting high cooperation at lower cost. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that both the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the structure of the population and the sharing of punishment duties, are important moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, because efficiency is not directly reported, quantitative predictions about efficiency must be inferred indirectly from the cooperation rates and payoff formulas.

- source: Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2012). Self-organization of punishment in structured populations. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Adaptive punishment in spatial public goods games enables the spontaneous emergence of rare, localized punishers who respond to the spread of defection. This mechanism maintains smooth interfaces between cooperators and defectors, invigorates spatial reciprocity, and prevents the emergence of cyclic dominance that would otherwise allow defectors to persist. As a result, adaptive punishment can achieve full cooperation (pure C phase) in parameter regions where, without punishment, only defectors would survive. The cost of punishment, when adaptively deployed, is globally negligible, making the mechanism highly efficient. Compared to steady (non-adaptive) punishment, adaptive punishment is more effective at eliminating defectors and does so with lower cost.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that adaptive, context-sensitive punishment mechanisms in spatially structured public goods games can substantially increase the likelihood of full cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, even when punishment is costly. The effect is especially pronounced when punishment is inexpensive, but remains positive even for costly punishment. The findings suggest that models or interventions that allow for adaptive, rather than fixed, punishment are likely to yield higher efficiency than those with no punishment or with steady punishment. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, so predictions should be based on the qualitative pattern that adaptive punishment expands the parameter space for high efficiency.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2013). Effectiveness of conditional punishment for the evolution of public cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that conditional punishment, where the severity of punishment is proportional to the number of punishers in a group, is more effective than unconditional punishment in promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when punishment is costly. Conditional punishers can maintain cooperation at lower fines and costs, and are more efficient in terms of cooperation per unit cost. Unconditional punishers only outperform conditional punishers when punishment is very cheap. The results are robust across different values of the multiplication factor (mpcr), and the findings are explained through the mechanism of indirect territorial competition and pattern formation on the spatial grid.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, the design of the punishment mechanism (conditional vs. unconditional) and the cost-to-fine ratio are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on cooperation and cost-effectiveness. When predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, the results suggest that conditional punishment is more likely to yield higher cooperation per unit cost, especially when punishment is not cheap. However, the paper does not directly report group efficiency as a fraction of the maximum possible payoff, so predictions about absolute efficiency should be made with caution. The findings are most applicable to spatially structured games with local interactions and no communication.

- source: BOYD, R., & RICHERSON, P. J. (1992). PUNISHMENT ALLOWS THE EVOLUTION OF COOPERATION (OR ANYTHING ELSE) IN SIZABLE GROUPS. *Ethology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in n-person public goods games, the introduction of targeted punishment (retribution) allows for the stable evolution of cooperation in larger groups than is possible with reciprocity alone. When the benefits to a punisher from induced cooperation exceed the costs of punishing, cooperation and punishment can coexist at equilibrium, leading to higher average payoffs (i.e., greater efficiency) than in the absence of punishment. The model also shows that 'moralistic' strategies—where individuals punish both noncooperators and those who fail to punish—can stabilize cooperation (and any other costly behavior) even when the group benefit is not sufficient to compensate individual punishers. Increasing group size makes cooperation harder to sustain, but the negative effect is less severe with punishment than with reciprocity. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide empirical data, but it directly models payoff flows and evolutionary stability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency (payoff) relative to the no-punishment baseline, especially in larger groups and with repeated interactions. The model specifies how the costs and effectiveness of punishment, group size, and the expected number of rounds interact to determine whether cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) can be sustained. It also warns that if punishment is too costly or group size is too large, cooperation may not evolve. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, given knowledge of group size, punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the structure of repeated interactions. However, the results are theoretical and assume evolutionary dynamics rather than short-run behavioral responses.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Vasconcelos, V. V., & Santos, F. C. (2014). Climate change governance, cooperation and self-organization. *Physics of Life Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing local (group-level) sanctioning institutions (punishment) in threshold public goods games with risk of collective loss significantly increases the fraction of groups that successfully achieve the public good, especially under low risk perception and high contribution thresholds. Global (population-wide) sanctioning institutions provide only marginal improvement. Smaller group sizes and higher risk perception also promote higher group achievement. Threshold uncertainty (uncertainty about the required contribution level) can sharply reduce group achievement. The model suggests that decentralized, polycentric punishment is more effective than centralized punishment for promoting cooperation and group success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment (specifically, local/group-level sanctioning institutions) in threshold public goods games with risk of collective loss will increase group achievement (a close proxy for efficiency), especially when group size is small, risk perception is high, and contribution thresholds are stringent. The effect of punishment is much stronger when implemented locally rather than globally. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local punishment should be expected to yield a more positive effect on efficiency-related outcomes than global punishment or no punishment, particularly in small groups and under high risk. However, the main outcome is group achievement, not exact efficiency, so predictions should be interpreted accordingly.

- source: Parks, C. D., Joireman, J., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Cooperation, Trust, and Antagonism: How Public Goods Are Promoted. *Psychological Science in the Public Interest*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward mechanisms are effective at promoting cooperation in public goods games and similar social dilemmas, with meta-analytic evidence indicating that their effects on cooperation are roughly equal and of medium size. The presence of punishment opportunities leads to higher contributions to public goods over time, and thus to higher group efficiency and collective welfare. The effectiveness of punishment is moderated by factors such as trust (more effective in high-trust societies), group size (less effective in large groups), and the iterated nature of the game (more effective in repeated interactions). The review also notes potential downsides of punishment, such as undermining intrinsic motivation or evoking retaliation, but concludes that, overall, structural solutions like punishment are robust tools for increasing cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper should inform prediction by reinforcing that, across a wide range of public goods game designs, enabling punishment (or reward) mechanisms generally increases group efficiency and collective welfare compared to control conditions without such mechanisms. The effect is robust across many studies and is supported by meta-analytic evidence. However, the magnitude of the effect may depend on moderators such as group size, trust, and the iterated nature of the game. The review provides indirect evidence for the predictive value of design dimensions such as punishment cost, identifiability, communication, and group size. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, especially in high-trust, small-group, iterated settings.

- source: Kraak, S. B. M. (2011). Exploring the 'public goods game' model to overcome the Tragedy of the Commons in fisheries management. *Fish and Fisheries*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods game experiments, the option for costly punishment increases cooperation and, after initial costs, leads to higher average payoffs and group efficiency. When punishment is combined with mechanisms for reputation-building (e.g., non-anonymity, indirect reciprocity), cooperation is further enhanced and the use of punishment becomes more targeted and less frequent, reducing its destructive impact. The author argues that these findings are directly relevant for designing fisheries management institutions: enabling peer monitoring, punishment, and communication can foster cooperation and improve collective outcomes. However, the paper also cautions that externally imposed sanctions may undermine intrinsic motivation to cooperate, and that institutional legitimacy and stakeholder involvement are crucial.
  prediction_guidance: This paper should inform predictions that enabling punishment in public goods game-like environments (including fisheries) generally increases efficiency and group payoff, especially when combined with mechanisms for reputation and communication. The evidence is based on a synthesis of experimental studies, not new data, but the translation to fisheries is conceptually strong. The paper highlights that the effect of punishment is most positive when it is peer-driven, combined with transparency and communication, and not imposed externally. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment, reputation, and communication mechanisms should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control games without these features.

- source: Milinski, M. (2016). Reputation, a universal currency for human social interactions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that punishment in public goods games can maintain high levels of cooperation, but often at a net cost due to the expenses of punishing and being punished. Reputation mechanisms, such as indirect reciprocity, can also sustain cooperation and do so more efficiently by avoiding these costs. When both punishment and reputation mechanisms are available, punishment is used less frequently and more selectively, and overall group efficiency is increased. The review also notes that reward mechanisms are generally less effective than punishment in sustaining cooperation. The findings are based on a synthesis of multiple experimental studies, with particular emphasis on the interaction between punishment and reputation in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games generally increases cooperation and can improve group efficiency, especially when combined with reputation mechanisms. However, punishment alone may not always increase efficiency due to its costs. The review provides indirect evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the presence of reputation systems and the ability to observe or communicate about others' actions. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and is maximized when reputation mechanisms are also present. The review does not provide new quantitative estimates but synthesizes qualitative and comparative findings from the literature.

- source: Fang, Y. H., Benko, T. P., Perc, M., Xu, H. Y., & Tan, Q. M. (2019). Synergistic third-party rewarding and punishment in the public goods game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment and reward, when applied together, strongly promote cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The synergy between punishment and reward is more effective than either alone, especially at lower cost to the third party. Lower thresholds for intervention (greater tolerance for defection) and higher intensity of intervention both increase cooperation. More frequent intervention (shorter time intervals) also promotes cooperation. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the combination of punishment and reward can robustly increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, and that the effect is sensitive to the threshold, intensity, and frequency of intervention. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its findings are most useful for understanding how design dimensions like intervention intensity, threshold, and frequency affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings, but this is not demonstrated here.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Lun, J. (2015). Societal threat and cultural variation in the strength of social norms: An evolutionary basis. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of the PGG with punishment, higher societal threat leads to higher rates of cooperation and a greater proportion of norm-enforcing punishers. Under low threat, cooperation and norm enforcement are weaker. The presence of punishment is necessary to achieve high cooperation under threat; spatial structure alone is insufficient. The model also shows that norm strength (adherence and enforcement) increases rapidly in response to increased threat and declines gradually when threat is removed. While payoffs are modeled, the main reported outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement in PGG-like environments, especially under higher threat. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment enabled/disabled, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The model parameters (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR) are specified, and the model is robust to parameter changes, but the main outcomes are behavioral. The paper supports the qualitative prediction that enabling punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement, which is likely to increase efficiency, but does not provide direct efficiency data for prediction tasks.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Rong, Z. H. (2015). Mutual punishment promotes cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study uses agent-based simulations of a spatial public goods game on a square lattice, where each player participates in five overlapping groups. The key manipulation is the introduction of a symmetric, mutual punishment: whenever two neighbors have different strategies, both pay a punishment fine. The main result is that higher punishment fines lead to higher steady-state cooperation rates, and for sufficiently high fines, the population becomes fully cooperative. The mechanism is robust to changes in system size and noise. The paper provides a formula for the payoff difference between cooperators and defectors at the interface between clusters, showing that this difference increases with the punishment fine, favoring the spread of cooperation. However, the paper does not report group-level efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mutual punishment increases cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially as the punishment fine increases. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not efficiency. The model is theoretical and does not include communication, reward, or other institutional features. The punishment is symmetric and always applied when strategies differ, which is a specific variant of punishment not always present in experimental or real-world settings.

- source: Carpenter, J., Matthews, P., & Ong'ong'a, O. (2004). Why Punish? Social reciprocity and the enforcement of prosocial norms. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment in public goods games is best explained by social reciprocity: individuals are motivated to punish norm violators (free riders) even when doing so is costly and does not improve their own material payoff, and even when the violator is outside their immediate group. Experimental and survey evidence shows that punishment is not solely driven by the desire to reduce payoff differentials, and that people continue to punish even when the cost of punishment exceeds the harm inflicted. The demand for punishment is relatively inelastic to price, and motivations are primarily normative. While the models present payoff calculations and show that punishment can stabilize cooperation under certain initial conditions, the empirical focus is on behavioral motivations and patterns rather than direct measures of group efficiency or total earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment mechanisms in public goods games can sustain higher levels of cooperation, but it does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff outcomes. The theoretical models show that, under certain conditions, populations with punishers can achieve higher average payoffs than those dominated by free riders, but these are model-based results rather than empirical efficiency measurements. The experimental and survey data are primarily about who punishes, why, and under what conditions, rather than about the quantitative effect of punishment on group efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that punishment can support cooperation even when it is costly, and that motivations are primarily normative, but it does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, ability to punish outgroup) are discussed in the context of behavioral patterns, not efficiency outcomes.

- source: Lv, S. J., & Song, F. F. (2022). Particle swarm intelligence and the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game with punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of punishment on cooperation in a spatial public goods game depends on both the punishment intensity and the learning rule (PSO weighting coefficient). For low punishment intensity, low susceptibility to neighbors (low omega) promotes cooperation; for high punishment intensity, high omega is more beneficial. Intermediate omega values increase punishment input but can reduce cooperation. The enhancement factor (r) also moderates these effects. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are about cooperation rates and investment allocation, not direct welfare or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and learning rules affect cooperation rates and investment allocation in spatial PGGs with continuous strategies. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment. For the prediction task, the main value is in understanding that the effect of punishment on cooperation is highly sensitive to punishment intensity and the learning rule, and that the allocation of resources between cooperation and punishment can affect the expansion of cooperative clusters. However, since efficiency is not reported, the paper's findings should be used only as indirect, mechanistic context rather than as direct quantitative evidence for efficiency prediction.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Szabó, G., & Czakó, L. (2011). Competition of individual and institutional punishments in spatial public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games with both peer and pool punishment, the prevailing strategy depends sensitively on the synergy factor (MPCR analog), punishment cost, and fine. Peer punishers often dominate and are more effective at eliminating defectors, especially at lower synergy factors where cooperation would otherwise collapse. However, when both punishment types are present, their competition can sometimes undermine the effectiveness of punishment, leading to the reemergence of defectors. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams mapping out the conditions under which each strategy or combination of strategies prevails. The findings are based on the frequencies of strategies and do not directly quantify group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how the presence and type of punishment (peer vs pool), as well as the synergy factor (MPCR), punishment cost, and fine, affect the prevalence of cooperation and defection in spatial PGGs. However, because the outcomes are reported as strategy frequencies and not as group efficiency or payoff, the results must be interpreted as indirect evidence for efficiency. The elimination of defectors and the dominance of cooperators or punishers can be taken as a proxy for higher efficiency, but the paper does not provide quantitative efficiency values. The detailed mapping of parameter regimes is useful for predicting when punishment will be effective, but direct prediction of efficiency outcomes requires caution.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Szabó, G., & Perc, M. (2011). Phase diagrams for the spatial public goods game with pool punishment. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that pool punishment in spatial public goods games can support the coexistence of cooperation and punishment, but only within limited parameter regions (low synergy factor, low punishment cost, and intermediate fines). For high fines, the system often evolves into a cyclic dominance phase where defectors, cooperators, and pool punishers coexist in a rock-paper-scissors dynamic. Pool punishment is less effective than peer punishment at eliminating defectors, and cooperators can invade pool punishers' territories due to the cost of punishment. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams for different values of the synergy factor, showing the boundaries between homogeneous, coexistence, and cyclic phases. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so its findings are most directly relevant to predicting the prevalence of cooperation and punishment strategies rather than efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on how the cost and effectiveness of pool punishment, as well as the synergy factor (mpcr), affect the prevalence of cooperation and punishment in spatial public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the phase diagrams and strategy frequencies can inform predictions about when punishment is likely to increase or decrease efficiency, especially in structured populations with spatial interactions. The findings suggest that pool punishment is only effective in a narrow range of parameters and can lead to complex coexistence rather than full cooperation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper's results are only indirectly informative, as they do not quantify group payoff or efficiency directly.

- source: Helbing, D., Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2010). Defector-accelerated cooperativeness and punishment in public goods games with mutations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games, costly punishment (when fines are sufficiently high relative to costs) allows punishing cooperators to outcompete defectors and non-punishing cooperators, solving the second-order free-rider problem in structured populations. This effect is robust to small and moderate mutation rates, but high mutation rates (which mimic well-mixed conditions) restore the tragedy of the commons by enabling defectors to dominate. The presence of even tiny mutation rates can dramatically accelerate the spread of punishing cooperators by temporarily introducing defectors, which punishing cooperators can then outcompete. The main outcomes are the stationary fractions of each strategy, not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatially structured public goods games, enabling punishment (with sufficiently high fine-to-cost ratio) can lead to the dominance of punishing cooperators and the elimination of defectors and non-punishing cooperators, especially at moderate mutation rates. While efficiency is not directly measured, the dominance of punishing cooperators is likely associated with higher group payoff than defector-dominated states. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the structure of interactions (spatial vs. well-mixed) and to mutation rates. For prediction tasks, the presence of spatial structure, the fine-to-cost ratio, and mutation rate are critical moderators of punishment's effect on group outcomes.

- source: Helbing, D., Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2010). Punish, but not too hard: how costly punishment spreads in the spatial public goods game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games, the introduction of punishing cooperators (PC) or punishing defectors (PD) can promote the spread of cooperation, but through different mechanisms. For punishing cooperators, increasing the punishment fine monotonically increases the cooperation level up to a threshold, after which non-punishing cooperators and defectors are eliminated. For punishing defectors, there is a non-monotonic (reentrant) effect: increasing the fine initially increases cooperation, but too high a fine reduces the fraction of punishing defectors, causing cooperation to collapse and the system to revert to defection. The results are presented as phase diagrams and time-evolution of strategy fractions, not as direct efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and type of punishment mechanism, as well as the punishment cost and fine, can have large effects on the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the outcomes are reported as fractions of strategies and cooperation level, not as group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that enabling punishment (especially by punishing cooperators) can increase cooperation and likely efficiency, but the relationship is mediated by the spatial structure and the specific punishment parameters. The non-monotonic effect for punishing defectors highlights the importance of tuning punishment severity. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment can improve efficiency in structured populations, but the mapping to efficiency must be inferred from cooperation levels.

- source: Liu, J. Z., Meng, H. R., Wang, W., Li, T., & Yu, Y. (2018). Synergy punishment promotes cooperation in spatial public good game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment into a spatial public goods game increases the fraction of cooperators, and that further reducing the cost of punishment through a synergistic effect (i.e., when multiple punishers share the cost) leads to even higher cooperation rates. The effect of synergy is most pronounced when the punishment fine is low; at high fines, the additional benefit of synergy is minimal. The main outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The paper qualitatively links higher cooperation to higher group payoffs, but does not report efficiency or payoff data directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both punishment and reduced punishment cost (via synergy) increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting how design dimensions like punishment cost and punishment effectiveness affect cooperation rates, and by extension may suggest (but not demonstrate) positive effects on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect support for the idea that lower punishment cost and higher punishment effectiveness (via synergy) should increase efficiency, but this is inferred from cooperation rates, not measured payoffs.

- source: Zhu, P. C., Guo, H., Zhang, H. L., Han, Y., Wang, Z., & Chu, C. (2020). The role of punishment in the spatial public goods game. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that peer punishment leads to higher levels of cooperation than pool punishment in spatial public goods games. When the threshold for switching to pool punishment is high (i.e., more peer punishment), cooperation is more prevalent. The coexistence of both punishment types is explored, and the transition between regimes is mapped. The main findings are based on the frequencies of cooperators, defectors, and punishers, not on efficiency or group payoff. The ratio of fine to cost is higher for peer punishment, which is suggested as a reason for its greater effectiveness in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that peer punishment is more effective than pool punishment in increasing the prevalence of cooperation in spatial PGGs. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model and parameterization are clearly described, so the paper can inform mechanistic expectations about how punishment type and its implementation affect cooperation, but not directly about efficiency or welfare outcomes.

- source: Boyd, R., Gintis, H., Bowles, S., & Richerson, P. J. (2003). The evolution of altruistic punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding punishment to a public goods game model with group selection allows for the maintenance of high levels of cooperation in much larger groups than group selection alone. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation depends on the cost structure of punishment (punishment must be more costly to the punished than to the punisher, and costs to punishers must decline as defectors become rare). The model is robust to various parameter changes, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it focuses on the frequency of cooperation and punishment as the main outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment can increase and sustain cooperation in public goods game-like environments, especially in larger groups, under certain cost structures. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is indirect. It is most useful for understanding the mechanisms and conditions under which punishment is likely to have a positive effect on cooperation, which is often correlated with higher efficiency, but this link is not explicitly modeled or measured in terms of payoffs.

- source: Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2015). Competition and cooperation among different punishing strategies in the spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game with diverse punishing strategies, the highest level of public cooperation is attainable through a selection mechanism among punishers. The evolutionary success of a punishing strategy depends on its invasion velocity relative to defectors and other punishers. At low multiplication factors (difficult environments), cooperation among punishing strategies can allow otherwise weak punishers to survive. At higher multiplication factors, competition among punishers can lead to a single dominant strategy. The impact of punishment fine is nonmonotonic: too high fines can be detrimental due to excessive costs. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so its findings are most relevant for predicting the prevalence of cooperation and the dynamics of punishment strategies, not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure and diversity of punishment strategies, as well as the multiplication factor and punishment fine, are critical moderators of cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it informs the prediction task by highlighting that the relationship between punishment and cooperation is nonmonotonic and context-dependent, and that the presence of multiple punishing strategies can alter the evolutionary outcome. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment may depend on the costliness of punishment and the synergy factor, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Traulsen, A., Hauert, C., De Silva, H., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2009). Exploration dynamics in evolutionary games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in finite-population public goods games with punishment, the rate of random exploration (mutation) crucially shapes the evolutionary outcome. For low mutation rates, defectors dominate in compulsory games, and cooperation/punishment only emerge with voluntary participation. For high mutation rates, cooperators can dominate even in compulsory games, with punishers playing a pivotal but not dominant role. The analysis is based on theoretical models and simulations, reporting the average abundance of each strategy type. The paper does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but the prevalence of cooperation and punishment is interpreted as beneficial for group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation in public goods games is highly sensitive to the rate of random exploration (mutation) in the population. It suggests that punishment alone does not guarantee high cooperation or efficiency unless exploration rates are high or participation is voluntary. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect: it supports the idea that punishment's effect on efficiency is contingent on the broader evolutionary or behavioral context, especially the rate of exploration/mutation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but less so for quantifying efficiency changes.

- source: Wang, J., Wu, B., Ho, D. W. C., & Wang, L. (2011). Evolution of cooperation in multilevel public goods games with community structures. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that in multilevel PGGs with community structure, the prevalence of cooperation and punishment depends on the strengths of imitation within and between communities. When within-community imitation is weak or between-community imitation is strong, cooperation and punishment are more abundant than defection. If both imitation strengths are strong, defection dominates. The model also finds that, under moderate within-community imitation and small between-community imitation, punishers can become the most abundant strategy while cooperators nearly disappear. The analysis is based on the stationary distribution of homogeneous states (all-cooperator, all-defector, all-punisher) and fixation probabilities, not on direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment and cooperation can be sustained in multilevel PGGs with community structure, depending on imitation dynamics. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff with and without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that punishment can increase the prevalence of cooperation under certain imitation regimes, which may imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly modeled or quantified. The results are most useful for understanding the conditions under which punishment and cooperation are likely to be sustained, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Chen, X. J., Sasaki, T., & Perc, M. (2015). Evolution of public cooperation in a monitored society with implicated punishment and within-group enforcement. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of implicated punishment (where all group members are fined if a defector is detected) can transform the public goods game into a coordination game, making both full cooperation and full defection stable equilibria. Increasing the monitoring probability, the implicated punishment fine, or the within-group enforcement probability all enlarge the basin of attraction for full cooperation, thus promoting higher cooperation rates. Group size has a nonlinear effect: intermediate group sizes are least conducive to cooperation, while both small and large group sizes favor cooperation. The addition of within-group enforcement (peer punishment) further relaxes the conditions for cooperation to thrive. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but higher cooperation rates in standard PGGs are typically associated with higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that implicated punishment and within-group enforcement can substantially increase cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when monitoring probability and punishment fines are sufficiently high. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the close relationship between cooperation rate and efficiency in standard PGGs suggests that these mechanisms would also increase efficiency. The nonlinear effect of group size is important: intermediate group sizes may be less effective for promoting cooperation than either small or large groups. For prediction tasks, the presence of implicated punishment and within-group enforcement, as well as the values of monitoring probability, punishment fine, and group size, should be considered as key moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: García, J., & Traulsen, A. (2012). Leaving the loners alone: Evolution of cooperation in the presence of antisocial punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary success of cooperation and punishment in public goods games with voluntary participation depends critically on whether loners (abstainers) can be punished or can punish others. If loners are truly isolated (neither punish nor are punished), cooperators who punish defectors become dominant in the population, even when antisocial punishment is allowed. If loners can be punished, cooperation and punishment do not evolve, and the population is split among cooperators, defectors, and loners, with no strategy dominating. The results are robust across different strategy sets and selection intensities. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the abundance of strategies as the main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the ability to punish or be punished, especially for abstainers (loners), is a critical moderator of whether punishment can support cooperation in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that if the game design allows abstainers to be punished, punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency or cooperation. If abstainers are truly isolated, punishment can support the dominance of cooperative strategies. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results are most useful for understanding which game design features (especially the treatment of abstainers) are likely to enable or undermine the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation.

- source: van Dijk, E., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2021). Experimental Games and Social Decision Making. *Annual Review of Psychology, Vol 72*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The review highlights that the inclusion of punishment and reward mechanisms in repeated public goods games and other social dilemmas enables partner control, which can promote cooperation and the development of group norms. It notes that punishment is typically costly to the punisher but more costly to the target, and that such mechanisms can shape future cooperation through reputation and indirect reciprocity. The review also discusses moderators such as group heterogeneity, intergroup competition, and cross-cultural variation in norm enforcement. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative synthesis on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in public goods games and related environments, especially in repeated interactions. It identifies key design features (e.g., repeated play, sanctioning options, partner selection) that are relevant to predicting the impact of punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to calibrate the magnitude of punishment's effect on efficiency. Its value for prediction is mainly in identifying which game dimensions are theoretically important moderators.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schurtenberger, I. (2018). Normative foundations of human cooperation. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that social norms, particularly conditional cooperation norms, are central to explaining cooperation in public goods games. Punishment opportunities increase cooperation rates, but the welfare (efficiency) effects are often limited or negative due to the costs of punishment and the occurrence of antisocial punishment. Normative constraints on punishment—such as those enabled by institutional or group-level norm formation—can reduce antisocial punishment and make punishment more effective, leading to higher cooperation with less collateral cost. The review emphasizes that the presence of punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the structure and normative context of punishment matter greatly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and integrative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates, but does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff unless punishment is normatively constrained to reduce antisocial punishment and collateral costs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of normative or institutional constraints, the prevalence of antisocial punishment, and the cultural context. Game design dimensions that affect the cost, targeting, and normative context of punishment are likely to be critical moderators of efficiency outcomes. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency changes, but it cautions against assuming that increased cooperation from punishment always translates into higher efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that the opportunity to punish norm violators in public goods games leads to a dramatic increase in cooperation rates, often approaching full cooperation. Punishment is frequently administered by group members or even third parties, and is motivated by non-selfish concerns for norm enforcement rather than self-interest. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency and strength, but punishment remains prevalent even when it is costly. The presence of punishment opportunities transforms the dynamics of cooperation, preventing the typical decline in contributions over time seen in standard public goods games without punishment. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games leads to much higher and more stable cooperation rates, and that the effect is robust across various experimental designs. For the downstream prediction task, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment will increase efficiency, provided that efficiency is closely linked to contribution rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly, its value is primarily in establishing the behavioral mechanism and the robustness of the punishment effect on cooperation. It also highlights that the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as group size and information structure, are important moderators, but does not provide quantitative estimates for these effects on efficiency.

  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and empirical literature to argue that human cooperation and the willingness to punish defectors in public goods games are products of gene-culture coevolution. It reviews evidence that, in public goods games, cooperation typically decays over time unless costly punishment is available, in which case cooperation is sustained or even increases. The paper references Fehr & Gächter's experiments showing that allowing punishment prevents the decay of cooperation, but does not report efficiency or group payoff data. The main findings are about the prevalence and rationality of prosocial and punitive behaviors, not about their impact on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates, and references classic experiments that show this effect. However, it does not provide quantitative evidence or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes, which limits its direct utility for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions. The referenced experiments (e.g., Fehr & Gächter) are more directly relevant for such predictions, but this paper itself does not supply the necessary outcome data.

- source: Sylwester, K., Hellmann, B., & Bryson, J. J. (2013). Homo Homini Lupus? Explaining Antisocial Punishment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews and interprets evidence that antisocial punishment—punishing cooperators at a cost to oneself—is a widespread phenomenon in public goods games, especially in certain cultural and ecological contexts. It argues that antisocial punishment is often driven by status competition, aggression, or negative reciprocity, and is more prevalent where the cost of punishment is low, legal institutions are weak, and social competition is high. The review notes that antisocial punishment can undermine the cooperation-enhancing effects of punishment, and that its prevalence varies widely across cultures. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment; instead, it focuses on behavioral mechanisms and cross-cultural patterns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical and contextual background for understanding when and why punishment may fail to increase efficiency in public goods games, especially due to antisocial punishment. It highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be negative in environments with high antisocial punishment. However, because the paper is a review and does not report new or meta-analyzed efficiency or payoff data, it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. It is most useful for identifying moderators (e.g., punishment cost, anonymity, cultural context) that should be considered when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, and for warning that punishment can sometimes reduce, rather than increase, group welfare.

The authors argue that punishment can induce and stabilize cooperation, and discuss the evolutionary puzzle of why individuals punish at a cost to themselves. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the expectation that punishment increases cooperation, which is often associated with higher efficiency, but does not provide data or analysis to calibrate this relationship or its dependence on design features.

- source: Perc, M., Gómez-Gardeñes, J., Szolnoki, A., Floría, L. M., & Moreno, Y. (2013). Evolutionary dynamics of group interactions on structured populations: a review. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.

  findings: The review summarizes that structured populations (lattices, networks, bipartite graphs) and coevolutionary rules can strongly affect the evolution of cooperation in public goods games. Punishment and reward mechanisms, as well as strategic complexity (e.g., conditional cooperation, pool/peer punishment, rewarding), can promote cooperation and solve second-order free-rider problems in structured settings. The review highlights that the effectiveness and stability of punishment and reward depend on the interplay between game parameters (e.g., group size, benefit functions, network structure) and evolutionary dynamics. However, the main outcomes discussed are cooperation rates and the survival/extinction of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.

It is useful for understanding which game design dimensions (e.g., group size, network structure, strategic complexity) are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report control vs. punishment-enabled efficiency rates. For the downstream prediction task, this review can inform which dimensions are likely to be important moderators, but cannot be used for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Fehr, E., & Rockenbach, B. (2004). Human altruism: economic, neural, and evolutionary perspectives. *Current Opinion in Neurobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that the availability of costly punishment in public goods games leads to much higher and more stable cooperation levels compared to games without punishment. Voluntary selection into punishment regimes further increases cooperation compared to exogenous assignment. The review also highlights that punishment is only effective at promoting cooperation when it is perceived as fair or socially justified. Neuroeconomic studies show that both cooperation and the act of punishing defectors activate reward-related brain circuits, suggesting a neural basis for strong reciprocity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative results on efficiency or group payoff, instead focusing on behavioral outcomes and neural mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation and contributions, especially when punishment is voluntary and perceived as justified. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this review supports the expectation that punishment will increase efficiency when it increases cooperation, but the magnitude and conditions for this effect must be inferred from other sources or from the primary studies it cites.

- source: Singer, T., & Steinbeis, N. (2009). Differential Roles of Fairness- and Compassion-Based Motivations for Cooperation, Defection, and Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
