  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that fairness-based motivation leads to punishment of norm violations, which can sustain cooperation in public goods games, as shown in referenced empirical studies. Compassion-based motivation, in contrast, can prevent the breakdown of cooperation in noisy environments by promoting forgiveness, but may not be effective in the long term against persistent defectors. The emotional and neural mechanisms underlying these motivations are distinct. The paper does not provide new empirical results or direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, but summarizes that punishment options in public goods games are associated with higher cooperation rates and norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and literature-based support for the idea that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates and helps sustain cooperation, referencing empirical studies that show this effect. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that punishment increases cooperation (and thus likely efficiency), but does not allow for fine-grained prediction based on game design dimensions. It is most useful for understanding the motivational and emotional mechanisms behind punishment and cooperation, and for contextualizing why punishment may be effective in sustaining cooperation.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Human cooperation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and experimental literature on the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in humans, with a focus on public goods games and related social dilemmas. It finds that: (1) Repetition and targeted reciprocity (including punishment and reward) can promote cooperation in public goods games; (2) Both punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but reward is generally more efficient (i.e., produces higher group payoffs) than punishment, which can destroy value and provoke retaliation or antisocial punishment; (3) The effectiveness of punishment and reward depends on their implementation and the broader social context; (4) Cooperation in one-shot anonymous games is explained as an overgeneralization of strategies learned in repeated or reputation-based settings. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and qualitative evidence that adding punishment to public goods games can increase cooperation, but may not always increase efficiency or group payoff, especially if punishment is costly, provokes retaliation, or is used antisocially. Reward mechanisms are generally more efficient than punishment. The review does not provide quantitative data or direct predictions about efficiency changes, so it should be used for qualitative guidance: expect that punishment can promote cooperation, but its effect on efficiency is context-dependent and may be negative if punishment is misapplied. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the design of punishment (cost, effectiveness, possibility of antisocial use) and the presence of reward options are important moderators.

- source: Henrich, J., & Muthukrishna, M. (2021). The Origins and Psychology of Human Cooperation. *Annual Review of Psychology, Vol 72*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The review argues that mechanisms such as punishment, reputation, and signaling are crucial for sustaining cooperation in large-scale human societies, especially where kinship and direct reciprocity are insufficient. It highlights that the willingness to punish norm violations (including in public goods and related games) varies dramatically across societies and is associated with community size and institutional context. The review synthesizes evidence that punishment can sustain cooperation, but also that the form and prevalence of punishment are culturally variable. It also notes that mechanisms supporting cooperation can stabilize both prosocial and maladaptive costly norms. However, the review does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can sustain cooperation in public goods game-like environments, especially in large, anonymous societies. It also highlights that the effectiveness and prevalence of punishment depend on cultural, institutional, and demographic factors such as community size. However, because the review does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in identifying which dimensions (e.g., community size, presence of punishment, cultural context) are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation, and in providing context for interpreting empirical findings from other studies.

- source: Baumard, N., André, J. B., & Sperber, D. (2013). A mutualistic approach to morality: The evolution of fairness by partner choice. *Behavioral and Brain Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation and punishment behaviors in economic games, including public goods games, are guided by evolved fairness norms shaped by partner choice. It reviews evidence that people punish both low and high contributors in PGGs, often to restore fairness rather than to maximize group efficiency. The mutualistic approach predicts that individuals seek proportionality between contributions and rewards, and that punishment is used to rebalance unfair distributions. The paper notes that in real-world small-scale societies, exclusion and partner switching are more common than costly punishment. It also highlights that punishment in PGGs is sometimes antisocial (targeting high contributors) and is strongly shaped by local fairness norms. However, the paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes as a function of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and interpretive context for understanding why and how punishment may affect cooperation and fairness in PGG-like environments, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether punishment is perceived as fair and proportional, and that exclusion or partner choice may be more effective than costly punishment in sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., fairness norms, entitlement, partner choice), but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

  findings: The paper argues that punishment, including altruistic and third-party forms, is a key mechanism for maintaining cooperation in humans, especially in public goods game contexts. It reviews evidence from the literature that allowing punishment in public goods games increases cooperation, but it does not provide new quantitative results on efficiency or group payoff. The paper also discusses the psychological and evolutionary underpinnings of punishment and spite, suggesting that negative other-regarding preferences (spite) may be as important as positive ones (empathy) in shaping human cooperation. The review highlights that while punishment can deter free-riding and enforce norms, it can also be motivated by antisocial preferences, and the net effect on group welfare or efficiency is not always clear from the literature.

However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. - source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2012). Evolutionary advantages of adaptive rewarding. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Adaptive rewarding in spatial public goods games can eliminate defectors and promote high levels of cooperation, outperforming steady (non-adaptive) rewarding. The effectiveness of adaptive rewarding depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost of rewarding (alpha), and the strength of the reward (Delta). When rewards are strong and not too costly, adaptive rewarding can lead to defector-free states, interpreted as maximal efficiency. However, adaptive punishment is even more effective: for the same cost, punishment achieves defector elimination at lower required intensity. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing under what parameter regimes each strategy dominates, coexists, or is eliminated. The main efficiency outcome is the prevalence of defector-free states, which is used as a proxy for group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, adaptive punishment is more effective than adaptive rewarding at achieving maximal efficiency (defector-free states), given the same cost-to-impact ratio. The results are highly informative for predicting the effect of introducing punishment (or reward) in spatial PGGs: punishment is more likely to yield higher efficiency than reward, especially when the cost of sanctioning is not prohibitive. The findings are most directly applicable to spatial, lattice-based PGGs with local interactions, and may be less directly transferable to well-mixed or non-spatial settings. The paper's phase diagrams and minimum required cost-to-impact ratios for defector elimination can inform quantitative predictions about efficiency under different game design parameters.

- source: Gerber, A., & Wichardt, P. C. (2009). Providing public goods in the absence of strong institutions. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a voluntary deposit-commitment mechanism in public goods games can fully implement efficient contribution profiles as equilibrium outcomes, even in the absence of strong external enforcement or punishment institutions. The mechanism works by making non-contribution costly (loss of deposit) only if all players commit, thus aligning individual incentives with group welfare. Theoretical results show that, under this mechanism, the group can achieve payoffs and efficiency levels that strictly Pareto-dominate the standard Nash equilibrium of zero contribution. The mechanism is robust to various extensions, including repeated games and alternative uses of forfeited deposits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that self-commitment mechanisms (such as refundable deposits) can substitute for traditional punishment in public goods games, leading to higher efficiency and group payoffs. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, in environments where such a deposit-commitment mechanism is available and properly designed, the efficiency with 'punishment' (here, self-sanctioning via deposit) can approach the social optimum, even if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the deposit mechanism is feasible and all players can observe and verify commitments. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or parameterized predictions, but the qualitative direction is clear: enabling credible self-sanctioning or commitment mechanisms can dramatically improve efficiency in public goods games.

- source: dos Santos, M. (2015). The evolution of anti-social rewarding and its countermeasures in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with pool reward funds, allowing defectors to form their own reward funds (anti-social rewarding) causes a breakdown of cooperation and group efficiency, even when mechanisms to exclude non-rewarders (second-order sanctions) are present. Theoretical analysis shows that anti-social rewarding leads to stable equilibria with low or no cooperation, unless additional countermeasures are implemented. These include making pro-social reward funds more productive than anti-social ones, or making reward funds contingent on the actual production of the public good. Only under these conditions, and with sufficiently strong exclusion of non-rewarders, can high cooperation and efficiency be restored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of anti-social rewarding mechanisms in public goods games with institutional rewards can dramatically reduce the efficiency gains from positive incentives, even when second-order punishment or exclusion is available. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling anti-social rewarding (or failing to prevent it) will likely result in much lower efficiency in the punishment/reward-enabled condition compared to control, unless the game design includes mechanisms that make pro-social rewards more effective or contingent on public good production. The findings are directly relevant for settings with institutional rewards, but less so for standard punishment-only designs. The results highlight the importance of considering the possibility of anti-social incentives and the structure of reward and punishment technologies when predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gintis, H., Smith, E. A., & Bowles, S. (2001). Costly signaling and cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a multi-player public goods game, honest signaling of quality by providing a public good (or by punishing defectors) can be an evolutionarily stable strategy, provided that the cost of signaling is lower for high-quality individuals and that the benefits of alliances or partnerships are sufficiently high. The signaling equilibrium yields higher average group payoffs than the non-signaling equilibrium. The model also shows that punishment can function as a costly signal, and that under certain conditions, prosocial signals (including punishment) are more likely to evolve than neutral or antisocial signals. The model provides explicit conditions for when signaling equilibria exist and are stable, and analyzes the evolutionary dynamics leading to these equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling costly signaling (including punishment) in public goods games can increase group efficiency and average payoff, even in the absence of repeated interactions or reciprocity. The model is directly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where punishment is interpreted as a costly signal of quality. The findings suggest that, when the cost structure and benefit parameters are appropriate, introducing punishment (as a form of signaling) should increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment. The paper also highlights the importance of the cost differential between high- and low-quality individuals, the size of the group, and the benefits of alliances in determining the stability and efficiency of the signaling equilibrium. However, as a theoretical model, it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct experimental evidence.

- source: Li, K., Cong, R., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2015). Social exclusion in finite populations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that social exclusion mechanisms (peer and pool) can allow excluders to dominate over defectors in finite-population public goods games, depending on exclusion cost, group size, and exclusion effectiveness. Peer exclusion is more effective at low costs, while pool exclusion is more robust at high costs and larger group sizes. Both mechanisms can sustain cooperation and prevent the dominance of defectors, which would otherwise reduce group payoff. The analysis is based on evolutionary game theory, using payoff functions and fixation probabilities, rather than direct simulation of group efficiency. The results suggest that enabling exclusion mechanisms can improve group outcomes compared to no-punishment baselines, especially under the right parameter conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support that enabling exclusion-based punishment in public goods games can increase the prevalence of cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, especially when exclusion is effective and/or costs are not too high. The results are parameterized by group size, exclusion cost, and exclusion probability, and show that pool exclusion is more robust in large groups or at high costs. While the paper does not report efficiency as a direct outcome, the dominance of excluders and cooperators over defectors implies higher group payoff. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive when exclusion is effective, costs are moderate, and group size is not too large for peer exclusion (but pool exclusion can handle larger groups). However, the lack of direct efficiency reporting and the theoretical nature of the results mean that quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Quan, J., Li, X., & Wang, X. J. (2019). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game with conditional peer exclusion. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of conditional peer exclusion in a spatial public goods game model can greatly promote the emergence and stability of cooperation, especially when the tolerance for defectors is low (i.e., excluders act against defectors even when there are few of them), the cost of exclusion is low, and the probability of exclusion success is high. Even with moderate or low exclusion success probability, conditional exclusion can still promote cooperation if the synergy factor (equivalent to MPCR) is not too low. The main reported outcome is the cooperation level, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include a direct control (no-exclusion) efficiency comparison, but does reference the threshold for cooperation in the standard SPGG. The findings suggest that exclusion-based punishment can be a powerful mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional exclusion (a form of punishment) can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially under low tolerance, low cost, and high effectiveness. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting the effect of punishment on cooperation rates, and only indirectly suggest that efficiency may increase when cooperation is high. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency from design dimensions plus control efficiency), this paper provides only indirect, mechanistic support, not quantitative or direct evidence.

- source: Kitts, J. A. (2006). Collective action, rival incentives, and the emergence of antisocial norms. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective action and group efficiency in public-goods-like environments are shaped by a three-way interaction between the value of selective incentives (rewards), the rivalness of those incentives, and the strength of peer influence (cohesiveness). When incentives are nonrival, increasing their value or increasing peer influence both increase participation and group welfare. When incentives are rival, however, strong peer influence can lead to the enforcement of antisocial norms that discourage contribution, reducing group efficiency. The model demonstrates that under high rivalness and high incentive value, increasing peer influence or incentive value can actually decrease collective action and group welfare. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are most applicable to small groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, rival selective incentives and norm enforcement) on group efficiency is highly contingent on the design dimensions of the incentive system and the strength of peer influence. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling strong peer enforcement or increasing the value of selective incentives will not always increase efficiency—if the incentives are rival, these interventions can backfire and reduce group welfare. The model is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with reward-based incentives, especially when those rewards are rival. While the paper does not model punishment per se, the logic and results are highly informative for any system where sanctions or rewards are rival and peer enforcement is possible.

  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that group effort and efficiency in public goods games and group contests depend strongly on group size, within-group heterogeneity, and the nonlinearity of cost and benefit functions. In linear models, group effort typically decreases with group size and increases with heterogeneity, while in nonlinear models, these patterns can reverse. The largest contributors are those with the highest stake or lowest cost, but under some conditions, these individuals may end up with lower net payoffs than free-riders. The review also summarizes experimental findings, noting that punishment can increase contributions but may not always increase total earnings or efficiency, especially in heterogeneous groups. Theoretical models predict that group selection, between-group competition, and synergicity can all increase group effort and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and synthetic evidence on how game design dimensions such as player count, group heterogeneity, cost/benefit structure, and group selection affect efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency, as punishment is only discussed in the context of prior literature and experiments. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is mainly useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the moderating role of group structure, but not for estimating the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2015). Antisocial pool rewarding does not deter public cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the introduction of both antisocial and prosocial pool rewarding does not deter the evolution of cooperation or reduce group efficiency. Instead, prosocial rewarding continues to promote cooperation, and the aggregation required for rewards to be effective leads to higher group payoff and efficiency. The critical threshold for cooperation to dominate is lowered as the effectiveness of rewarding increases, even when defectors can also reward each other. In well-mixed populations, antisocial rewarding can prevent cooperation unless cooperators are initially predominant, but in structured populations, local aggregation allows cooperation to thrive. The results are robust to increased randomness in the interaction network.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public goods games with pool rewarding, the presence of antisocial rewarding does not reduce the efficiency gains from prosocial rewarding in structured populations. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling reward mechanisms (even if both cooperators and defectors can use them) will generally increase efficiency compared to control, provided the population structure allows for aggregation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings with spatial or network structure, group size of 5, all-or-nothing contributions, and no punishment. The results are less applicable to well-mixed populations or to games with punishment rather than reward.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Unemi, T. (2011). Replicator dynamics in public goods games with reward funds. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a compulsory public goods game with a pool-rewarding mechanism, cooperation and high efficiency can be maintained if the net benefit from the reward fund exceeds the cost of contributing to the public good. Specifically, if c2*(r2-1) > sigma (where sigma is the net cost of contributing to the public good), the population can converge to all rewarders (full efficiency) or exhibit stable cycles with high average payoff. If this condition is not met, defectors dominate and efficiency collapses. The model also shows that introducing second-order sanctions (reducing rewards to second-order free riders) can stabilize full cooperation. The findings are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGG-like games with reward mechanisms, but only indirectly inform about punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with reward funds, the efficiency outcome depends critically on the relative effectiveness and cost of the reward mechanism compared to the cost of cooperation. The explicit condition c2*(r2-1) > sigma can be used to predict when enabling a reward mechanism will increase efficiency relative to the control (no reward) case. However, the paper does not analyze punishment, so its direct value for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency is limited. The model structure and results may be informative by analogy for understanding how the cost and effectiveness of sanctions (punishment or reward) interact with game parameters (group size, MPCR, etc.) to determine efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hauert, C. (2010). Replicator dynamics of reward & reputation in public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, the opportunity to reward contributors (with or without reputation) can encourage the emergence of cooperation if rewards are sufficiently appealing and visible (via reputation). However, reward alone cannot stabilize cooperation, as second-order free-riders (who cooperate but do not reward) undermine the system, leading to cycles of cooperation and defection. The analysis is based on replicator dynamics and explicit payoff calculations for different strategy types. The paper contrasts these findings with the literature on punishment, noting that punishment can stabilize cooperation but is less effective at establishing it. Efficiency (average population payoff) is analyzed as a function of the strategic composition of the population, but the paper does not provide direct results for punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the mechanisms by which reward and reputation affect efficiency and cooperation in public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but its discussion of the contrasting roles of reward and punishment can inform predictions: reward can increase cooperation and thus efficiency in the short term, but cannot maintain high efficiency due to second-order free-riding. Punishment, as discussed from prior literature, can stabilize high efficiency once cooperation is established. The explicit payoff equations and analysis of group size, reward cost/benefit, and reputation can inform parameterization of models, but direct quantitative predictions for punishment effects are not provided.

- source: Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2019). Evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in the public goods game with pool exclusion strategies. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper shows that introducing prosocial pool exclusion into a PGG leads to higher average payoffs and can stabilize cooperation through periodic oscillations or fixation to full cooperation, depending on whether second-order exclusion is present. Antisocial exclusion can destabilize cooperation but does not fully prevent the effectiveness of prosocial exclusion. Compared to punishment, prosocial pool exclusion is a more robust and effective mechanism for sustaining high efficiency in the population, even in the presence of antisocial strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in infinite well-mixed populations, enabling a prosocial pool exclusion mechanism (where excluders pay a fixed cost to exclude defectors from the public good) increases group efficiency relative to the standard PGG without exclusion. The effect is robust to the presence of antisocial exclusion, and even more pronounced when second-order exclusion is allowed. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a pool exclusion mechanism should be expected to yield higher efficiency than punishment, especially when exclusion is institutionalized and second-order exclusion is possible. However, the results are derived from deterministic replicator dynamics and may not directly translate to finite or experimental settings.

- source: Bergstrom, T. C. (2002). Evolution of social behavior: Individual and group selection. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in linear public goods games (multiplayer prisoners' dilemmas), cooperation cannot be sustained in equilibrium under nonassortative (random) matching and individual selection; only defectors survive, leading to low group efficiency. However, cooperation and higher group payoffs can be sustained if (a) matching is assortative (e.g., kin selection, spatial structure, partner choice), (b) the game is not a prisoners' dilemma (e.g., stag hunt), or (c) the game is repeated and social norms (including punishment) are enforced. The paper discusses the theoretical role of punishment in repeated games as a means to sustain cooperation and efficient outcomes, but does not provide direct analysis or predictions about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical context for the importance of game structure (e.g., matching, repetition, group size, payoff parameters) in determining whether cooperation and high efficiency can be sustained in public goods games. It highlights that punishment can, in theory, support efficient cooperation in repeated games, but does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about the magnitude or direction of punishment's effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of repeated interaction and enforceable punishment norms are key moderators, but does not provide parameter-level guidance for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in specific game designs.

- source: Suzuki, S., & Akiyama, E. (2007). Evolution of indirect reciprocity in groups of various sizes and comparison with direct reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in n-person public goods games with indirect reciprocity, both cooperative (discriminating) and defective strategies can be evolutionarily stable, but the threshold for cooperation increases sharply with group size. As group size increases, it becomes much harder for cooperation to evolve and persist, leading to lower expected group efficiency/payoff. The reputation mechanism (standing vs. image scoring) is a strong moderator: standing allows for cooperation to be stable under a wider range of parameters. The presence of implementation errors (failures to cooperate) further undermines the stability of cooperation. When unconditional cooperators are present, cooperation is only stable for group sizes greater than two and under certain parameter ranges. The results are derived analytically from payoff and replicator dynamics equations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing group size in public goods games with indirect reciprocity (and no explicit punishment mechanism) makes it much harder for cooperation and high efficiency to evolve. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in large groups, the baseline efficiency is likely to be low, and the marginal effect of punishment may depend on whether it can overcome the group size barrier. The paper also highlights the importance of the reputation mechanism: if the punishment or sanctioning system is more like 'standing' (justified punishment does not harm reputation), cooperation is more likely to be stable. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, error rates, and reputation mechanisms, but do not directly model explicit costly punishment as a game option.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2016). Competition of tolerant strategies in the spatial public goods game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing diverse tolerant strategies into the spatial public goods game can significantly increase group efficiency, sometimes leading to defector-free, fully cooperative states. The effect depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost of monitoring (gamma), and the system size. In structured populations, coexistence of multiple tolerant strategies can create synergistic effects that outperform single-strategy or uniform-tolerance scenarios. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing which strategy combinations are stable under different parameter regimes. The main efficiency gains occur when tolerant strategies with different thresholds form alliances, crowding out defectors and maximizing group payoff. The results highlight the importance of diversity in tolerance and the structure of interactions for achieving high efficiency in public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial public goods games, the introduction of conditional strategies (tolerant players) that abstain from cooperation when defection is high can substantially increase efficiency, especially when multiple tolerance thresholds are present. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of such conditional strategies (even in the absence of explicit costly punishment) can be as or more effective than standard punishment in raising efficiency, particularly in structured populations. The results are most relevant for games with spatial structure, optional participation, and where monitoring costs are not prohibitive. The paper does not directly manipulate or analyze explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, so its direct applicability to punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled prediction is limited, but it provides a strong adjacent signal about the power of conditional sanctioning and abstention. For prediction, the key moderators are the synergy factor (mpcr), the cost of monitoring (analogous to punishment cost), and the structure of the population.

- source: Hauert, C., De Monte, S., Hofbauer, J., & Sigmund, K. (2002). Volunteering as Red Queen mechanism for cooperation in public goods games. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, allowing voluntary participation (the option to become a 'loner' and receive a fixed outside payoff) leads to persistent coexistence and oscillations among cooperators, defectors, and loners. This mechanism can sustain cooperation even in large groups and without repeated interactions, memory, or identification of defectors. The average population payoff can, depending on parameters, be higher or lower than the loner's payoff. The paper contrasts this with punishment and reward mechanisms, which require identification of defectors. The findings are robust across well-mixed and spatially structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it focuses on the alternative mechanism of voluntary participation. However, it provides important context: it notes that punishment and reward can establish cooperation if defectors can be identified, but that voluntary participation can also sustain cooperation without identification or repeated interaction. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a voluntary participation (loner) option is a strong moderator of efficiency and cooperation, and that the absence of punishment does not necessarily doom cooperation if such an option exists. The paper does not provide parameterized predictions about punishment effects, so its direct value for predicting efficiency with/without punishment is limited.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). Evolution of commitment and level of participation in public goods games. *Autonomous Agents and Multi-agent Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a commitment mechanism—where players can propose binding agreements before the PGG, with compensation for defection—substantially increases both cooperation and average payoffs, provided the cost of arranging commitment is not too high. The effect is robust across group sizes and compensation levels, as long as compensation is above a threshold. The optimal participation threshold (number of committers required) depends on the severity of the dilemma and the cost of commitment. The model shows that commitment mechanisms can transform the efficiency of the PGG, making group payoffs much closer to the fully cooperative optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling credible commitment mechanisms in PGG-like environments can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of arranging commitment is low relative to the benefit of cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a commitment mechanism (even if not a classic punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency, with the effect size moderated by the cost of commitment, the mpcr, and the participation threshold. The results are robust across group sizes and compensation levels (above a threshold). However, the model does not directly analyze classic punishment (costly peer punishment), so direct transfer to punishment-enabled PGGs should be done with caution.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2015). Benefits of tolerance in public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The presence of tolerant players, who monitor and respond to defection by switching to a loner strategy only when defection exceeds a threshold, can substantially increase the average payoff of the group in public goods games. This effect is strongest at intermediate levels of tolerance and is robust across both well-mixed and spatially structured populations. The model shows that neither unconditional cooperation nor immediate withdrawal (loner) is optimal; instead, a moderate tolerance threshold allows the group to maintain high efficiency, even under conditions where cooperation would otherwise be unsustainable. The paper does not include punishment or reward as explicit mechanisms, but positions tolerance as an alternative to these approaches.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games, the introduction of a tolerance-based strategy (where players cooperate unless defection exceeds a threshold) can dramatically improve group efficiency compared to standard models with only cooperators, defectors, and loners. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms allowing conditional withdrawal (tolerance) can substitute for or outperform punishment in raising efficiency, especially when the cost of monitoring is not too high and the tolerance threshold is moderate. However, because the paper does not include explicit punishment or reward treatments, its direct applicability to predicting the effect of punishment is limited. Instead, it provides a benchmark for the potential of alternative mechanisms to improve efficiency.

- source: Gao, J., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2010). Diversity of contribution promotes cooperation in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to preferentially contribute more to groups with higher cooperation levels (high-quality groups) significantly promotes the evolution of cooperation in public goods games, both on regular lattices and scale-free networks. The mechanism works by enabling cooperators to cluster and avoid exploitation by defectors, and by increasing the wealth/payoff of cooperators for a given cooperation level. The effect is stronger as the preference parameter (alpha) increases. The paper interprets the reduction of contributions to low-quality groups as a form of indirect, costless group-level punishment, but does not model or analyze explicit costly punishment. Group efficiency or total group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding how endogenous contribution patterns (where cooperators can direct more resources to cooperative groups) affect cooperation and the distribution of payoffs in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of explicit, costly punishment on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that indirect, costless group-level punishment (via reduced contributions to low-quality groups) can promote cooperation and increase the payoffs of cooperators, but the implications for overall group efficiency are not quantified. For prediction tasks focused on explicit punishment interventions, this paper provides only adjacent evidence, mainly relevant for understanding alternative mechanisms to punishment.

- source: Arenas, A., Camacho, J., Cuesta, J. A., & Requejo, R. J. (2011). The joker effect: Cooperation driven by destructive agents. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing destructive agents ('jokers') into a public goods game leads to robust cycles in which cooperation periodically surges. The mechanism is that jokers harm defectors more than cooperators, creating a dynamic where cooperators can invade jokers, jokers invade defectors, and defectors invade cooperators. The cycles are robust to mutation and population size. The model provides explicit payoff expressions for each strategy, but the main focus is on the evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding how destructive threats can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions per se. Instead, it analyzes the effect of introducing a destructive agent type that harms all non-jokers. The model is a theoretical extension of the PGG and provides insight into how external threats or destructive forces can promote cooperation through population dynamics. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze standard punishment mechanisms. Its main value is in highlighting that destructive forces can, under certain conditions, increase cooperation, but this is not equivalent to the effect of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2010). Reward and cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing costly reward in a spatial public goods game can promote cooperation, particularly when the synergy factor (MPCR) is low. However, the effect is non-monotonic: moderate rewards can be more effective than high rewards due to the emergence of cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and rewarding cooperators. At high synergy factors, network reciprocity alone can sustain cooperation, and reward mainly determines which cooperative strategy prevails. The paper notes that, compared to punishment, reward is less efficient at promoting cooperation, especially because rewarding cooperators always pay a cost, making them vulnerable to second-order free-riders. The main reported outcomes are the stationary frequencies of each strategy and the phase diagrams of their coexistence, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reward (not punishment) on cooperation in spatial public goods games. It does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it suggests that costly reward can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but is generally less effective than punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the role of reward mechanisms and the importance of spatial structure, synergy factor (MPCR), and cost-benefit ratios in shaping cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only adjacent: it does not provide direct evidence on punishment or efficiency, but may inform expectations about the relative effectiveness of reward versus punishment and the importance of game parameters.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2017). Alliance formation with exclusion in the spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games, the introduction of an excluder strategy (which monitors and excludes defectors at a cost) can lead to the elimination of defectors when excluders form alliances with other cooperative strategies (cooperators or punishers). This effect is robust even when the cost of exclusion is extremely high, provided the population structure allows for the formation of sufficiently large clusters of excluders and cooperators/punishers. The main outcomes are the extinction probabilities of defectors and the conditions for the survival of excluders, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusion-based sanctions (a form of punishment) can be highly effective in structured populations, especially when combined with other cooperative strategies. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it suggests that under certain spatial and strategic conditions, sanctioning mechanisms can eliminate defection, which would likely increase efficiency, but this is not quantified. The findings are most relevant for understanding the qualitative impact of exclusion and alliances in spatially structured games, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wang, Z., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2014). Rewarding evolutionary fitness with links between populations promotes cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that rewarding players whose utility exceeds a threshold by granting them external links to another population can significantly promote cooperation in both public goods and prisoner's dilemma games on structured networks. The effect is maximized at intermediate threshold values, where rewarded players are rare but able to percolate, acting as catalysts for cooperation. If the threshold is too low or too high, the effect diminishes. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. The results are robust across network types and game details. Punishment is not modeled, only discussed as background.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a reward mechanism (external links for high-fitness players) and analyzes its effect on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or payoff. While it discusses the relative merits of reward and punishment in the introduction, all results pertain to reward. Therefore, it is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, except as context for how reward mechanisms can promote cooperation under certain network and threshold conditions.

  findings: The study finds that, in public goods games with constant group size on networks, increasing group size generally promotes higher cooperation rates, especially when individuals can select partners based on reputation. It is relevant for understanding how group size and partner selection (especially reputation-based exclusion) affect cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff. The reputation-based exclusion mechanism is a form of costless, indirect punishment, which differs from the costly punishment typically studied in experimental PGGs. Therefore, the findings should not be directly transferred to predictions about the efficiency effects of enabling costly punishment. The paper may be useful for understanding the role of group size and partner selection in cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

  findings: The study finds that in evolutionary games on networks (including PGG), introducing a survival threshold (death/bankruptcy) leads to catastrophic cascades when defection is present. After such cascades, only cooperators survive, regardless of network topology or game type. Defectors may gain higher payoffs temporarily but are ultimately eliminated. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcomes are the fraction of dead agents, the survival of cooperators, and the conditions for extinction or survival, not efficiency or group payoff.
