It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The model's 'sanction' is the endogenous removal of agents with low payoffs, not a punishment technology as in standard PGG experiments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency. It may provide some adjacent theoretical context about the consequences of defection and the importance of cooperation for system survival, but not about the efficiency impact of punishment interventions.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Coevolutionary dynamics of population and institutional rewards in public goods games. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional rewards, if the reward strength is adaptively increased when cooperation is low and decreased when cooperation is high, the system can reach stable equilibria with sustained cooperation and group welfare. The model shows that: (1) a stable coexistence of cooperation and reward strength is possible; (2) full cooperation can be achieved with minimal reward intensity if the per capita incentive is above a threshold; (3) moderate cooperation can be sustained with either low or high reward intensity, depending on parameter values; (4) in some cases, even high reward intensity cannot prevent full defection if incentives are too low. The model provides explicit conditions for these outcomes in terms of group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with institutional rewards, but not for games with punishment. It provides theoretical predictions for how adaptive reward mechanisms affect group payoff and cooperation, given game parameters such as group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward strength. However, it does not address punishment, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding the efficiency impact of reward-based interventions, especially when reward strength is allowed to adapt to cooperation levels.

- source: Alvarez-Rodriguez, U., Battiston, F., de Arruda, G. F., Moreno, Y., Perc, M., & Latora, V. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of higher-order interactions in social networks. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in PGGs with higher-order interactions (hypergraphs), the critical synergy factor required for cooperation decreases as group size increases in uniform random hypergraphs, meaning larger groups can foster cooperation more easily (at a given synergy factor). Heterogeneity in group membership (hyperdegree) and group size can either facilitate or hinder cooperation, depending on the structure: scale-free (hub-dominated) hypergraphs inhibit cooperation, while uniform or less heterogeneous structures do not. The framework is validated with empirical data from scientific collaborations, showing that the synergy factor (group benefit) is a non-monotonic function of group size, with an optimal group size for maximum benefit. The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency and payoff outcomes in PGG-like environments with varying group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions. It is directly relevant for understanding how group size and network heterogeneity affect the critical conditions for cooperation and group payoff, but cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. Use this paper to inform baseline efficiency predictions in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but not for treatment effects of punishment.

- source: Archetti, M., & Scheuring, I. (2011). COEXISTENCE OF COOPERATION AND DEFECTION IN PUBLIC GOODS GAMES. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the public goods function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold) fundamentally changes the equilibrium outcome. In nonlinear/threshold (Volunteer's Dilemma-like) games, cooperation and defection can coexist at a stable mixed equilibrium, and the public good is produced with positive probability, even in one-shot, well-mixed populations without relatedness or repeated play. In linear (NPD) games, only defection is stable in the absence of assortment. The paper provides analytical and numerical results for the equilibrium frequency of cooperation and the probability of public good provision as a function of group size, cost/benefit ratio, and the threshold parameter. No punishment, reward, or external enforcement mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the public good production function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold), group size, and cost/benefit ratio, but it does not address the effects of punishment or sanctions. It provides direct theoretical predictions for efficiency and group payoff in the absence of punishment, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. For downstream prediction tasks focused on the effect of punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is valuable for understanding baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, especially in games with nonlinear or threshold public goods functions.

- source: Henrich, J., Chudek, M., & Boyd, R. (2015). The Big Man Mechanism: how prestige fosters cooperation and creates prosocial leaders. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that prestige-based leadership, via cultural transmission, can generate and sustain cooperation in public goods game environments, leading to higher group efficiency and payoffs. The effect depends on the probability that followers copy the leader (prestige effect, p), the stickiness of acquired behaviors (s), and group size (n). Cooperation is more likely to evolve and persist in small groups, with high prestige effects and high stickiness. The model predicts that prestigious leaders will be more prosocial in small groups, but this effect diminishes in large groups. The paper does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in its models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games without punishment, the presence of prestige-biased cultural transmission and prosocial leaders can increase group efficiency, especially in small groups and when followers are likely to copy and retain the leader's cooperative behavior. However, because the models do not include punishment or sanctions, the findings do not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The results are most relevant for understanding baseline efficiency in games with prestige-based leadership and social learning, not for predicting the marginal effect of punishment. If a game design includes prestige or leading-by-example mechanisms, this paper suggests that efficiency may already be elevated, and the incremental effect of punishment may be smaller or interact with these mechanisms.

- source: Killingback, T., Bieri, J., & Flatt, T. (2006). Evolution in group-structured populations can resolve the tragedy of the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game with continuous investment, cooperation and high group efficiency do not evolve in well-mixed populations, but can evolve and be stably maintained in group-structured populations with limited dispersal. The mechanism relies on group structure and population dynamics, not on punishment, reputation, or optional participation. The model demonstrates that for a range of dispersal rates, high levels of cooperative investment (and thus high group payoffs) can be sustained. However, the model does not include any form of punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is explicitly excluded from the model. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment and for considering the effects of group structure and dispersal, but it does not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is relevant only as a control or baseline for environments without punishment.

- source: Hilbe, C., Wu, B., Traulsen, A., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Evolutionary performance of zero-determinant strategies in multiplayer games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that repeated interactions (direct reciprocity) can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in small groups, but not in large groups. As group size increases, the evolutionary dynamics favor defectors and extortioners, leading to a sharp drop in average group payoff. The results are robust across both restricted (ZD strategies only) and general (all memory-one strategies) strategy spaces. The paper provides explicit formulas for payoffs and demonstrates, both analytically and via simulation, that group size is a critical moderator of efficiency in repeated public goods games without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, increasing group size in repeated public goods games leads to lower efficiency, even when direct reciprocity is possible. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that the baseline efficiency in control games without punishment will be high for small groups but will decline as group size increases. The paper does not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency change when punishment is enabled. However, it does highlight that, without additional mechanisms (like punishment), large groups are unlikely to sustain cooperation or high efficiency.

- source: Boza, G., & Számadó, S. (2010). Beneficial laggards: multilevel selection, cooperative polymorphism and division of labour in Threshold Public Good Games. *Bmc Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public good games, stable polymorphism (coexistence of cooperators and defectors) can be maintained by multilevel selection, even in the absence of punishment or spatial structure. Group efficiency (measured as group payoff or average fitness) is maximized when the number of cooperators matches the threshold required for success, not when all members cooperate. Increasing group size or cost of cooperation reduces the range of parameters where cooperation is stable. Division of labour can evolve, with individuals specializing in different collective actions. Spatial structure further promotes cooperation. The paper does not address punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in public good games. It is highly relevant for understanding baseline efficiency and the evolution of cooperation in threshold PGGs without punishment, and for predicting the efficiency of control games (punishment disabled) as a function of group size, cost, and threshold. However, it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled, nor does it provide evidence on any moderators of punishment effects. It is useful for modeling baseline efficiency in threshold PGGs, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Hauert, C., Holmes, M., & Doebeli, M. (2006). Evolutionary games and population dynamics: maintenance of cooperation in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, if population density is allowed to vary as a function of average payoff, cooperation can be maintained through eco-evolutionary feedback. When defection leads to low payoffs, population density drops, which in turn reduces group size and can make cooperation advantageous. This can result in stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors at intermediate frequencies, with population density and average payoff stabilizing at levels where neither strategy dominates. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is not modeled. However, it is highly informative about how ecological feedback (variable group size as a function of payoff) can stabilize cooperation and improve group payoffs in the absence of punishment. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it provides a theoretical baseline for understanding efficiency in public goods games without punishment or reward.

- source: Rong, Z. H., & Wu, Z. X. (2009). Effect of the degree correlation in public goods game on scale-free networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in scale-free networks, assortative degree mixing (where hubs are connected to other hubs) inhibits the emergence and sustainment of cooperation in the PGG, as the core-periphery structure weakens the ability of cooperative hubs to influence peripheral nodes. In disassortative networks (where hubs connect to low-degree nodes), cooperation can persist even at low multiplication factors, as hubs maintain their initial strategies and can influence their leaf neighbors. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, with some analysis of average payoff per degree. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It is relevant for understanding how network structure (degree correlation) affects cooperation and individual payoffs in PGGs without punishment. The findings may be useful for contextualizing baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in networked PGGs, but do not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., Wang, W. X., Lai, Y. C., & Wang, B. H. (2011). Impact of link deletions on public cooperation in scale-free networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in multi-group PGGs on scale-free networks, deleting links connected to hubs (network attack) predominantly reduces the fraction of cooperators, especially at low multiplication factors. There is a narrow region where the effect is strictly negative, but outside this region, an intermediate number of link deletions can minimize cooperation. Wealth distributions become less heterogeneous as links are deleted, which is associated with lower cooperation. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but does analyze total payoffs (wealth) at the individual level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how network structure and heterogeneity affect cooperation and individual payoffs, but not for predicting the effect of punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task. However, the analysis of wealth distributions may offer indirect insights into how structural features (like network heterogeneity) can moderate payoff outcomes in PGGs.

- source: Santos, F. C., Santos, M. D., & Pacheco, J. M. (2008). Social diversity promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods games. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing social diversity via heterogeneous (scale-free) network structures and varying individual participation in multiple public goods games can significantly enhance the emergence and stability of cooperation. Two main models are compared: (1) fixed cost per game (each cooperator pays a cost in every game they play), and (2) fixed cost per individual (each cooperator pays a total cost, divided among all games they play). The latter model, combined with network heterogeneity, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperation, even at lower enhancement factors. The paper also shows that in all-cooperator populations, wealth (fitness) distributions are more unequal on scale-free graphs, but the overall number of 'poor' is reduced under the fixed cost per individual model. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or reputation mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff relative to the cooperative optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it explicitly excludes punishment and focuses on the effects of social diversity and network structure. It is useful for understanding how heterogeneity in group structure and contribution rules can promote cooperation, but does not inform the impact of punishment or sanctions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, especially in heterogeneous network settings.

- source: Huang, K. K., Wang, T., Cheng, Y., & Zheng, X. P. (2015). Effect of Heterogeneous Investments on the Evolution of Cooperation in Spatial Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in cooperative investments (modeled as a uniform distribution around a mean) increases the steady-state fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. This effect is stronger with greater variance in investment. When both strategy and investment coevolve, the promotion of cooperation is weaker because investment heterogeneity is reduced. The main dependent variable is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that heterogeneity in investment can promote cooperation (as measured by the fraction of cooperators), but do not address payoff-based outcomes or the effect of punishment. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Wang, B. K., Chen, X. J., & Wang, L. (2012). Probabilistic interconnection between interdependent networks promotes cooperation in the public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game played on two interdependent networks, the probability of interconnection between corresponding nodes in the two layers has a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: an intermediate interconnection probability maximizes the fraction of cooperators in the system. The coupling effect between layers can promote cooperation in both, even if one starts with only defectors. The study does not include or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency/payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how network structure and interdependence affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based or punishment-related predictions. The absence of punishment, reward, and efficiency outcomes means it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Roca, C. P., & Helbing, D. (2011). Emergence of social cohesion in a model society of greedy, mobile individuals. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game with voluntary contributions and no punishment or reward, cooperation and social cohesion can emerge if individuals have moderate levels of greediness (aspiration). Too little greediness leads to stagnation in noncooperation, while too much greediness destabilizes cooperation and social ties. The model demonstrates that these beneficial levels of greediness can be evolutionarily selected. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, agglomeration, instability), not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include or analyze punishment, and thus provides no direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a PGG environment without punishment or reward, and focuses on the role of individual learning and mobility. While it is useful for understanding alternative mechanisms for cooperation, it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. The game design dimensions are partially covered (e.g., player count, group size, mpcr), but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot be used to predict efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Chen, M. H., Wang, L., Wang, J., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2015). Impact of individual response strategy on the spatial public goods game within mobile agents. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the order in which agents update their strategy and move (strategy update order) can significantly affect the evolution of cooperation and social instability in spatial PGGs with mobile agents. Different aspiration evaluation methods (how agents assess satisfaction based on their own and neighbors' payoffs) have only minor effects on cooperation and agglomeration, but can influence social instability. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how mobility and aspiration-based strategy updating affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based efficiency outcomes or the impact of punishment. It may provide weak contextual information about the role of behavioral updating and mobility, but is not informative for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2009). Topology-independent impact of noise on cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games, increasing the group size (number of players per game) can qualitatively change the relationship between noise (uncertainty in strategy adoption) and the survival of cooperation. On regular graphs with low clustering (no overlapping triangles), pairwise interactions (G=2) show an optimal intermediate noise level for cooperation, but larger groups (G>2) remove this effect and make the system behave more like high-clustering networks. The main outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it provides informative context about how group size and network topology affect cooperation rates in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatial PGGs without sanctions.

- source: Sigmund, K., Hauert, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2001). Reward and punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player public goods minigames with punishment or reward, evolutionary dynamics without reputation lead to asocial equilibria with low efficiency, regardless of the presence of punishment or reward options. When a small chance of reputation is introduced (players can sometimes observe others' punishment/reward behavior), the system can support a stable, high-efficiency equilibrium with cooperation and punishment. This effect is stronger and more robust for punishment than for reward. The model predicts that punishment with reputation is much more effective at promoting group efficiency than reward with reputation, and that without reputation, neither mechanism is effective in the long run.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment alone does not increase efficiency in public goods games unless players have some information about others' punishment behavior (i.e., reputation). In the absence of reputation, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency; the system converges to defection and non-punishment. With even a small probability of reputation, enabling punishment can create a bistable system where high-efficiency outcomes are possible and can be risk-dominant. The effect is parameter-dependent (e.g., punishment cost, effectiveness, benefit/cost of cooperation), but the qualitative result is robust. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of reputation or information about others' actions. If the game design does not allow for reputation, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. If reputation is present, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, more so than reward.

- source: ABREU, D., MILGROM, P., & PEARCE, D. (1991). INFORMATION AND TIMING IN REPEATED PARTNERSHIPS. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated partnership games with imperfect monitoring, the ability to sustain efficient cooperation through punishment is fundamentally limited by the informativeness of the monitoring technology and the timing of information. When monitoring is imperfect, reducing the interest rate (making players more patient) increases the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes, potentially allowing near-first-best payoffs. However, shortening the period of fixed action or reducing the lag with which information is reported can actually decrease efficiency, sometimes eliminating the possibility of cooperation altogether. Delaying information release can, counterintuitively, increase efficiency by reducing the cost of deterring deviations. The paper provides generalizations to n-player symmetric games and shows that these results are robust to more complex action and signal spaces.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of repeated public-goods-like games with punishment under imperfect monitoring. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the informativeness of monitoring, the timing of information, and the patience of players. In particular, enabling punishment does not guarantee high efficiency unless monitoring is sufficiently informative and the timing of information and action periods is chosen carefully. For prediction, the paper suggests that increasing patience (lower interest rate) is reliably associated with higher efficiency, but increasing the frequency of action or information updates can reduce efficiency when monitoring is imperfect. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to estimate efficiency given the relevant game design dimensions.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2018). Punishment and inspection for governing the commons in a feedback-evolving game. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment and inspection mechanisms can promote cooperation and prevent overexploitation in a common-pool resource game, but their ability to improve group efficiency (resource sustainability) depends critically on the intrinsic growth rate of the resource. If the resource renews too slowly, even perfect cooperation enforced by strong punishment leads to resource depletion and low efficiency. At moderate growth rates, strong punishment and inspection can achieve both high cooperation and sustainable resource levels, but weak institutions result in mixed strategy populations and depleted resources. At high growth rates, the resource is always sustainable regardless of punishment, and punishment only affects the fraction of cooperators versus defectors. Thus, the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and can be absent or even negative if resource dynamics are unfavorable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like common-pool resource games is highly contingent on the ecological context, especially the resource's intrinsic growth rate. When predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, it is crucial to consider not only the game design dimensions (such as punishment cost and effectiveness) but also the underlying dynamics of the resource. In settings where the resource cannot recover quickly, punishment may not improve efficiency even if it increases cooperation. In more favorable ecological conditions, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently effective (high detection probability and/or fine). Thus, the paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is not universal and must be interpreted in light of resource dynamics.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Richerson, P. J. (2017). Collective action and the evolution of social norm internalization. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to show that norm internalization evolves more readily and has larger effects on cooperation when peer punishment of free riders is promoted. In public-goods-like ('us-vs.-nature') games, the evolution of norm internalization and punishment increases cooperation and material payoffs (efficiency). In intergroup contest ('us-vs.-them') games, norm internalization and punishment increase cooperation but reduce material payoffs due to overproduction and rent dissipation. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in public-goods-like settings and negative in intergroup contest settings. The model also predicts that group size, punishment cost, and the strength of social norms moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the type of collective action problem. In standard public goods (us-vs.-nature) games, enabling punishment increases efficiency by reducing free riding. In intergroup contest (us-vs.-them) games, enabling punishment can reduce efficiency due to excessive effort and resource dissipation. The model also suggests that group size and punishment cost are important moderators. For prediction, the context of the collective action (public goods vs. contest), group size, and punishment parameters should be considered.

- source: Bowles, S., & Gintis, H. (2004). The evolution of strong reciprocity: cooperation in heterogeneous populations. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The model shows that in groups where some members are willing to incur costs to punish norm violators (strong reciprocators), high levels of cooperation and group efficiency are sustained. Without punishment, groups devolve to all-Selfish agents and low efficiency. The presence of punishment leads to a stable mix of Reciprocators, Cooperators, and Selfish agents, with low average shirking rates and high group payoffs. The model is robust to changes in group size, cost of punishment, and other parameters. The findings support the idea that costly punishment is a key mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling costly punishment in public-goods-game-like environments increases group efficiency and average payoffs, relative to control conditions without punishment. The model is not an empirical experiment but simulates a wide range of parameter values, showing that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to variations in group size, cost of punishment, and other design features. For prediction tasks, the presence of a costly punishment mechanism (even with moderate cost and effectiveness) should be expected to increase efficiency, especially in settings with repeated group interaction and no central authority. The model's structure and outcomes are directly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Sethi, R., & Somanathan, E. (2003). Understanding reciprocity. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment and reciprocity can support efficient outcomes in both repeated and sporadic interactions, but only under certain conditions. In repeated games, strategies that support cooperation (and thus efficiency) can be stable if complexity costs or implementation errors are considered, but many inefficient strategies can also be stable. In one-shot or sporadic interactions, punishment can induce cooperation and improve efficiency, but this stability is fragile: populations of punishers can be invaded by non-punishing cooperators or defectors, and the cost of punishment can erode its effectiveness. In public goods and common pool resource games, the presence of reciprocators willing to punish can lead to higher group payoffs, but only if monitoring and punishment costs are not too high and if group composition allows reciprocators to coordinate. The evolution of mixed populations (reciprocators and defectors) is common, and the efficiency gains from punishment depend on the game structure and population dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines, but only under specific conditions: punishment must be sufficiently effective (high impact per cost), not too costly, and the population must contain enough reciprocators or the ability to assort. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive; in some models, punishment does not prevent the evolution of inefficient outcomes, and the stability of efficient cooperation is sensitive to the cost and observability of punishment, as well as to the possibility of second-order free-riding. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and the ability to observe or assort by type. Control efficiency (without punishment) is often low, but the introduction of punishment can raise efficiency if these moderating conditions are met.

- source: Góis, A. R., Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2019). Reward and punishment in climate change dilemmas. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk dilemmas (threshold public goods games with risk of group failure), both reward and punishment policies improve group achievement (η_G) compared to no policy. Reward is particularly effective at initiating cooperation when the risk of failure is low, while punishment is more effective at sustaining high levels of cooperation, especially when the risk is high or the threshold for group success is stringent. The optimal policy is to use reward when the fraction of cooperators is low and switch to punishment when cooperation is sufficiently high. The model provides explicit formulas for the optimal switching point and shows that combining both policies synergistically yields the highest group achievement. The results are robust across a range of parameter values, including group size, population size, and policy efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (and/or reward) in threshold public goods games will increase group achievement (a proxy for efficiency) compared to a control without such mechanisms. The model allows for direct mapping of game design dimensions such as player count, group size, number of rounds (in evolutionary time), presence and efficiency of punishment and reward, and the structure of the public goods dilemma (threshold, risk). The findings suggest that punishment is most effective at maintaining cooperation once it is established, while reward is more important for initiating cooperation. The optimal policy is a dynamic combination of both. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that adding punishment (especially with sufficient budget and efficiency) will generally increase efficiency, but the effect size and optimality depend on the risk, threshold, and whether reward is also available. The model is theoretical, so empirical calibration may be needed for quantitative predictions.

- source: Sun, W. W., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2021). Combination of institutional incentives for cooperative governance of risky commons. *Iscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk social dilemma games, institutional pure reward strategies (where executors only reward cooperators) are more effective than pure punishment or hybrid strategies in promoting group achievement (i.e., the fraction of groups that reach the collective target). This result holds for both fixed and flexible incentive schemes, and is robust to changes in risk level, mutation rate, and enhancement factor. Local sanctioning schemes are more effective than global ones. The main outcome is group achievement, not efficiency or total group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that, in collective-risk social dilemmas, institutional punishment alone is less effective than reward or hybrid schemes in promoting group achievement (a proxy for cooperation). However, because the main outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, but rather the fraction of groups reaching a behavioral target, the findings should be used with caution for efficiency prediction. The game structure (collective-risk, institutional incentives, evolutionary dynamics) is close but not identical to standard PGGs. The results suggest that institutional punishment may not increase efficiency as much as reward, but do not provide quantitative efficiency estimates.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The coevolution of altruism and punishment: Role of the selfish punisher. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote the evolution of cooperation (and thus higher group payoffs) in both well-mixed and lattice-structured populations, but only under certain conditions. In the score-dependent viability model, punishment is more effective at suppressing defectors and promoting cooperation than in the fertility model. The presence of selfish punishers (defectors who punish other defectors) can help the evolution of altruistic punishers by first eliminating pure defectors, but pure altruists (who cooperate but do not punish) can hinder the evolution of punishment by being exploited. The benefit of cooperation, the fine imposed by punishment, and the cost of punishing all interact to determine evolutionary outcomes. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent and can be positive or negative depending on parameter values and population structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (group payoff) in public-goods-like games depends on the interaction of several design dimensions: population structure (well-mixed vs. lattice), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the benefit of cooperation, and the evolutionary update rule (viability vs. fertility). Punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when the punishment fine is large relative to the cost of punishing, and in spatially structured populations. However, if the cost of punishing is too high or if pure cooperators are common, punishment may not evolve or may even reduce cooperation. The findings suggest that predictions of treatment efficiency from control efficiency should account for these moderators, especially the structure of interactions and the punishment parameters. However, as the paper does not report explicit efficiency values, its support is indirect and based on evolutionary stability of cooperation.

- source: Gardner, A., & West, S. A. (2004). Cooperation and punishment, especially in humans. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote and stabilize cooperation in public-goods-like environments, leading to higher group fitness (efficiency), provided that individuals who punish tend to receive more cooperation. The cost of punishment decreases as cooperation increases, making punishment more sustainable in cooperative populations. Relatedness between individuals is not required for punishment to be favored; instead, the key is a positive association between an individual's punishment and the cooperation she receives. The model predicts that punishment is more easily maintained than initially evolved, and that small group size and effective punishment (where the cost of being punished exceeds the cost of cooperation) facilitate the evolutionary stability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective (i.e., the penalty for being punished is greater than the cost of cooperation) and when there is a mechanism (such as behavioral adjustment) that links punishment to increased cooperation received. The findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing punishment should increase group efficiency, particularly in smaller groups and when punishment is not too costly to the punisher. However, the results are derived from theoretical models rather than empirical data, so quantitative predictions require caution. The paper also highlights that the effect of punishment is not dependent on relatedness or repeated interactions, but on the structure of behavioral associations.

- source: He, N. R., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Central governance based on monitoring and reporting solves the collective-risk social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The introduction of central governance based on monitoring and reporting, with top-down punishment of defectors, can dramatically increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial collective-risk social dilemma. There are threshold effects: small increases in monitoring probability, punishment fine, or decreases in reporting cost can cause a sudden jump from low to high cooperation. The effect is stronger under harsh conditions (low synergy factor). However, the study focuses on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that top-down punishment based on monitoring and reporting can increase cooperation rates in spatial collective-risk dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The findings suggest that, in similar spatial PGG-like environments, enabling punishment via monitoring/reporting is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if contributions are productive, but the paper does not quantify this. The model's details (e.g., spatial structure, overlapping groups, no explicit cost of punishment to individuals) should be considered when generalizing.

  findings: The paper finds that responsible punishment and cooperation can co-evolve and become stable in populations when interactions are non-anonymous and reputation can be built. In anonymous settings, cooperation and responsible punishment do not emerge, and antisocial punishment can persist. The model provides a threshold for the amount of information (reputation) needed for responsible punishment to be adaptive. The results are robust to errors in reputation and extend to public goods games with more than two players, though larger group sizes raise the threshold for reputation needed. The paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the emergence of cooperation and responsible punishment implies higher group welfare than in the absence of these behaviors.

The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by whether players can observe each other's actions and reputations. In anonymous settings, punishment does not promote cooperation or efficiency. The model also suggests that larger group sizes make it harder for punishment to be effective. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes, its value for direct quantitative prediction is limited; it is most useful for understanding the qualitative role of reputation, anonymity, and group size as moderators.

- source: Vasconcelos, V. V., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). A bottom-up institutional approach to cooperative governance of risky commons. *Nature Climate Change*.

  findings: The paper finds that local, group-level sanctioning institutions are much more effective than global, population-level institutions at promoting successful collective action in a threshold public goods game with risk of collective loss. Local institutions increase the fraction of groups that reach the public good threshold (group achievement), especially when the perceived risk of failure is low. Global institutions are unstable and provide only marginal improvements. The effect is stronger in smaller groups. The model does not report efficiency or total payoff, but the results strongly suggest that local punishment institutions increase the likelihood of group success, which is closely related to efficiency in this context.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling local, group-level punishment institutions in threshold public goods games (with risk of collective loss) will increase the rate of successful group outcomes compared to no punishment or global punishment. While the model does not report efficiency or total payoff, the 'group achievement' outcome is a close proxy for efficiency in threshold settings. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive when punishment is local, groups are small, and risk perception is low. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so its value is mainly in qualitative prediction and mechanism understanding.

- source: Lehmann, L., Rousset, F., Roze, D., & Keller, L. (2007). Strong reciprocity or strong ferocity? A population genetic view of the evolution of altruistic punishment. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (costly helping plus costly punishment) can evolve in spatially structured populations if helping and punishment are tightly linked (no recombination) and relatedness is sufficiently high. In this case, strong reciprocity can invade even when rare. When helping and punishment are unlinked (recombination is strong), neither costly helping nor costly punishment can invade from rarity unless punishment yields a direct fitness benefit to the actor. The model shows that kin selection (genetic or cultural) is necessary for the evolution of strong reciprocity. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide direct predictions about group efficiency or payoff, but rather about the evolutionary stability and spread of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the evolutionary preconditions for the emergence of punishment and cooperation in public-goods-like settings, especially the importance of linkage between helping and punishment, population structure (relatedness), and direct fitness effects. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about group efficiency or payoff with and without punishment, nor does it report outcomes directly comparable to experimental public goods games. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting that punishment is unlikely to increase cooperation or efficiency in anonymous, unstructured, or one-shot settings unless there is kinship or direct benefit to punishers. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

It reviews theoretical models showing that the effectiveness of punishment in maintaining cooperation depends on the cost of punishing relative to the cost of being punished, and that social norms enforced by punishment can persist even when the cooperative act is individually costly. The paper also discusses how group-level selection can favor groups with more effective cooperative norms, and how cultural evolution allows for rapid adaptation of cooperation strategies. However, the paper does not provide empirical or quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the mechanisms and evolutionary logic.

It highlights the importance of social norms, cultural learning, and group structure in moderating the effects of punishment. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the magnitude of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. Its value for prediction lies in its mechanistic insights and identification of key moderators (e.g., punishment cost/effectiveness, group size, norm strength), but not in providing direct outcome estimates.

  findings: The paper finds that the reputation consequences of punishment are context-dependent. In forced play games where punishment is possible, a punitive reputation can induce fear and stabilize cooperation, potentially supporting group efficiency. However, when partner choice is available, individuals with a nonpunishing cooperative reputation are typically preferred over punishers, unless punishment is the only available signal of cooperative intent. The efficiency of punishment (fee:fine ratio) also moderates reputational outcomes: 'efficient' punishment (fee:fine > 1) may be viewed as self-serving, while 'inefficient' punishment (fee:fine < 1) is more likely to be seen as genuinely norm-enforcing. The paper synthesizes prior empirical and theoretical work but does not provide new data or direct estimates of efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is most useful for identifying moderators and mechanisms that may affect the impact of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency in public goods games and similar environments. It highlights the importance of partner choice, the efficiency of punishment, and the availability of alternative signals of cooperation. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence on efficiency outcomes, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is primarily in identifying which dimensions are likely to be important moderators (e.g., partner choice, punishment efficiency, information structure).

It reviews evidence that extrinsic incentives (such as punishment) and trust signals independently increase cooperation. Neuroimaging studies show that punishment threats activate cognitive control regions and increase cooperative behavior, particularly among self-regarding or Machiavellian individuals. The review also highlights that individual differences (e.g., social value orientation, Machiavellianism) moderate the impact of punishment and incentives on cooperation. However, the paper does not directly report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and neural responses.

It suggests that the effect of punishment is likely to be more pronounced when extrinsic incentives are salient and among participants with self-regarding preferences. However, because the review does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its value for prediction is primarily in identifying moderators (e.g., individual differences, presence of incentives, trust signals) and in supporting the general mechanism by which punishment increases cooperation. - source: Simpson, B., & Willer, R. (2015). Beyond Altruism: Sociological Foundations of Cooperation and Prosocial Behavior. *Annual Review of Sociology, Vol 41*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent

  findings: The paper reviews sociological mechanisms that promote cooperation in social dilemmas, including public goods games. It finds that both formal (centralized) and informal (peer) punishment can increase cooperation, but the welfare effects are ambiguous due to the costs of sanctioning and potential for over- or under-punishment. Communication and endogenous rule formation improve outcomes. Centralized punishment (e.g., by elected leaders) is more effective and less prone to retaliation than peer punishment. However, the presence of sanctions and extrinsic incentives can undermine intrinsic motivation and trust, leading to lower cooperation when such mechanisms are removed. Reputation and status also promote cooperation, but can introduce ambiguity about motivations. Network structure (e.g., density, centrality, dynamic ties) shapes cooperation, with denser and more dynamic networks generally supporting higher cooperation, but also potentially undermining generalized trust.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides high-level theoretical and empirical synthesis relevant to predicting the effects of punishment in public goods games and similar environments. It supports the general finding that punishment increases cooperation, but cautions that the net effect on efficiency or group welfare depends on the costs and structure of punishment, the presence of communication, and whether enforcement is centralized or peer-based. The review suggests that communication and endogenous institution formation are important moderators, and that centralized punishment is more effective than decentralized peer punishment. However, it also warns that punishment can undermine intrinsic motivation and trust, potentially reducing efficiency in the long run. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent and moderated by game design features such as communication, institution formation, and enforcement structure, but does not provide quantitative estimates or direct efficiency outcomes.

  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that payoff-biased migration—where individuals move from lower-payoff to higher-payoff groups and assimilate—can lead to the spread and predominance of group-beneficial behaviors, resulting in higher average group payoffs (efficiency). This effect is strongest when migration rates are low enough to maintain between-group cultural variation, but not so low as to prevent migration from having an effect. The model is general and can represent public goods, mutualism, and punishment-based norm enforcement. The presence of punishment is discussed as a mechanism that can stabilize group-beneficial equilibria, which are associated with higher group payoffs. The model predicts that, under plausible conditions, group-beneficial (efficient) norms will predominate, but alternative (less efficient) norms may persist in a polymorphic equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms which stabilize group-beneficial behaviors (including punishment) can lead to higher group efficiency, especially in structured populations with migration. The model is not a direct PGG experiment, but its payoff structure and discussion of punishment-based norm enforcement are closely related. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of effective punishment (or other norm-stabilizing mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when migration or group structure allows for between-group variation and selective migration. However, the model is abstract and does not provide quantitative estimates for specific game design parameters such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, so its guidance is qualitative: enabling effective punishment or norm enforcement should increase efficiency, especially when group structure and migration allow for the persistence and spread of efficient norms.

- source: Rankin, D. J., Bargum, K., & Kokko, H. (2007). The tragedy of the commons in evolutionary biology. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews the tragedy of the commons as a framework for understanding how individual-level selfishness can reduce group-level outcomes, including group fitness and productivity, in both human and non-human systems. It highlights that mechanisms such as punishment, policing, kin selection, and ecological feedbacks can prevent or reduce the severity of the tragedy, sometimes restoring group productivity or preventing collapse. The review draws analogies to public goods games and discusses the importance of distinguishing between complete collapse (collapsing tragedy) and partial reduction in group fitness (component tragedy).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that punishment and related mechanisms can improve group outcomes (efficiency) in public-goods-like environments by altering incentives and deterring selfish behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence that would allow direct prediction of efficiency changes from game design dimensions. Its value lies in supporting the general expectation that punishment, when effective and not too costly, can prevent or mitigate efficiency losses due to free-riding, but it does not specify how this effect depends on specific game parameters.

- source: Schlüter, M., Tavoni, A., & Levin, S. (2016). Robustness of norm-driven cooperation in the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that cooperation enforced by social sanctioning is robust under certain conditions but can collapse with increased resource abundance or small increases in resource variability. High resource variability or scarcity can promote cooperation. The effectiveness of sanctioning depends on the proportion of norm followers (social capital). When two groups are ecologically connected, resource leakage from a more cooperative group can destabilize cooperation in the less cooperative group. The model highlights the complex, nonlinear interplay between social and ecological factors in sustaining cooperation. However, the main outcome is the level of cooperation, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (here, non-costly social disapproval) on cooperation in CPR/public goods settings, especially under varying resource conditions. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. The model's focus on non-costly, endogenous social sanctioning differs from standard experimental punishment treatments. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited and mainly contextual/mechanistic.

  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical results on how various mechanisms (iteration, spatial structure, indirect reciprocity, punishment, reward) affect the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in social dilemmas. It notes that spatial structure promotes cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma but can reduce cooperation in the Snowdrift game. Indirect reciprocity, punishment, and reputation can promote cooperation, especially when individuals can condition their behavior on others' reputations. The review also discusses the generalization to public goods games and the impact of group size, voluntary participation, and continuous investment. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the frequency and stability of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how design dimensions such as group size, spatial structure, iteration, and the presence of punishment or reward mechanisms can affect cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment and reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, and that spatial structure can have divergent effects depending on the game. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, it is not directly usable for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its value is mainly in informing mechanistic expectations and highlighting possible moderators, not in providing quantitative effect estimates.

  findings: The paper reviews decades of research on experimental games, including public goods games and social dilemmas. It highlights that cooperation is common even when game theory predicts defection, and that punishment and reward mechanisms can influence cooperation rates. Altruistic punishment is often found to encourage cooperation, but punishment can also have negative side effects such as reducing trust or inducing revenge. The review notes that the structure and framing of games, as well as social context, can significantly affect behavior. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

- source: Wu, B., García, J., Hauert, C., & Traulsen, A. (2013). Extrapolating Weak Selection in Evolutionary Games. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary games with finite populations, the ranking of strategies by abundance (e.g., cooperation, defection, punishment) can change as the intensity of selection increases. This is shown for public goods games with punishment as well as for general multiplayer and multi-strategy games. The main implication is that predictions based on weak selection (where payoffs have little effect on fitness) may not hold for moderate or strong selection. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, but rather focuses on which strategies are most common under different evolutionary conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Instead, it shows that the abundance of strategies (including punishers) can change non-monotonically with selection intensity, and that results from weak selection may not extrapolate to stronger selection. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests caution in using weak selection results to predict efficiency effects of punishment, especially in games with more than two strategies or players. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not analyzed, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency is limited.

- source: Ostrom, E. (2000). Collective action and the evolution of social norms. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost
  findings: Ostrom reviews and synthesizes experimental and field evidence on collective action, focusing on public goods and common-pool resource dilemmas. She identifies that communication and the ability to punish free riders both substantially increase cooperation rates in laboratory public goods games. The paper argues that real-world resource regimes that allow users to design their own rules, monitor each other, and impose graduated sanctions tend to be more sustainable and effective. However, the paper does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report new experimental data. Instead, it provides a conceptual framework for understanding how punishment, communication, and institutional design affect cooperation and the sustainability of collective action.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong conceptual and qualitative support for the idea that enabling punishment and communication increases cooperation in public goods and collective action settings. It suggests that punishment mechanisms, especially when locally designed and enforced, can help sustain cooperation and reduce free riding. However, the paper does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify the effects of particular game design dimensions in a way that would allow direct prediction of treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design features. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the inclusion of punishment and communication as positive moderators of cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence for efficiency outcomes or for the effect sizes associated with specific design parameters.

  findings: The paper reviews the literature on CPR and public goods experiments, highlighting that communication (e.g., face-to-face chat) increases cooperation and average earnings, while costly punishment is used by participants but can reduce net average returns unless chosen voluntarily by the group. The paper emphasizes the importance of micro-situational variables (such as group size, communication, and context) and the need for richer data collection to understand decision-making. It also discusses the crowding-out effect of external regulation and the mixed effects of ecological dynamics on cooperation and punishment. However, the paper does not provide new empirical estimates or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and methodological guidance for interpreting experimental results on punishment and cooperation in CPR and public goods settings. It suggests that communication and voluntary punishment can improve cooperation, but costly punishment may reduce net payoffs unless used sparingly or chosen by the group. The paper highlights the importance of context and micro-situational variables, which should be considered when predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency. However, because it does not report new empirical data or directly analyze efficiency outcomes, its value for quantitative prediction is limited to qualitative insights and literature synthesis.

- source: Raihani, N., & Aitken, D. (2011). Uncertainty, rationality and cooperation in the context of climate change. *Climatic Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in large-scale social dilemmas like climate change can be promoted by mechanisms that increase the net benefit of cooperation relative to free-riding, such as punishment, rewards, communication, and reputation. It highlights that uncertainty and irrational responses (e.g., loss aversion, framing effects) can inhibit cooperation. The authors review evidence that punishment and reward can both increase cooperation in public goods games, but that punishment is costly and may only be effective once cooperation is already established, while rewards may be more effective in early stages. Communication and information about others' actions also increase cooperation. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, but suggests that these mechanisms can indirectly improve collective outcomes by increasing cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and review-based support for the idea that punishment, reward, communication, and framing can increase cooperation in public goods games and climate change contexts. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment (especially when combined with rewards and communication) is likely to increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly addressed. The review highlights that punishment is more effective when cooperation is already high, and that rewards may be more effective in low-cooperation settings. The paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators but does not provide direct empirical input for efficiency prediction.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Mobilia, M., Jiang, L. L., Szczesny, B., Rucklidge, A. M., & Perc, M. (2014). Cyclic dominance in evolutionary games: a review. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
