  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The review highlights that cyclic dominance can emerge in evolutionary games with three or more strategies, including PGG variants with volunteering, punishment, and reward. In structured populations, spatial structure and mobility play crucial roles in maintaining coexistence and generating complex patterns (e.g., spirals, waves). Punishment and reward can stabilize cooperation through cyclic dominance, but the outcomes are typically described in terms of coexistence and oscillatory dynamics rather than efficiency or group payoff. The review also notes that alliances and subsystem solutions can act as effective strategies, further complicating the dynamics. Theoretical and simulation results are emphasized, with limited direct reference to empirical payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative evidence that the introduction of punishment (and reward) in PGG-like environments can promote the coexistence of cooperative strategies through cyclic dominance, especially in structured populations. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this review supports the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation and prevent collapse to all-defection, but it does not inform the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes. The evidence is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., spatial structure, mobility, network topology) rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Uchida, S. (2014). Rewards and the evolution of cooperation in public good games. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public good games, voluntary reward funds can evolve and lead to efficient cooperation, overcoming coordination problems that typically prevent the evolution of cooperation in large, anonymous groups. The model shows that, regardless of initial conditions, rewarders can invade a population of defectors if the reward fund's return is sufficiently high, leading to a cascade where cooperators eventually dominate. The resulting group payoff and average fitness are higher with rewards than without, and the system can reach full cooperation or stable mixed equilibria depending on the risk and threshold parameters. The paper contrasts this with institutional punishment, which is argued to be less effective or more difficult to initiate in large groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing a reward mechanism in threshold public good games will increase group efficiency and average payoff, especially in settings where coordination is otherwise difficult. While it does not directly analyze punishment, it offers indirect evidence that reward mechanisms can be more effective than punishment in some coordination contexts. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling a reward mechanism (with sufficient effectiveness and sustainability) should increase efficiency relative to control. The model's explicit treatment of group size, threshold, risk, and reward parameters can inform how these dimensions moderate the effect of rewards on efficiency.

- source: Foster, K. R. (2004). Diminishing returns in social evolution: the not-so-tragic commons. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper develops and analyzes mathematical models of social evolution in group-structured populations, focusing on how the functional form of returns to investment (linear vs. diminishing) affects group performance (efficiency) in the face of selfish competition. It finds that, under diminishing returns, the negative impact of competition on group efficiency is greatly reduced, and group performance can be close to the optimum even with low relatedness and no explicit enforcement. The paper provides examples from social vertebrates, insects, and microbes, arguing that diminishing returns are common in real biological systems. Explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are not modeled, but enforcement is discussed as one of several mechanisms that can limit the tragedy of the commons.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency impact of selfish competition in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the shape of the returns to investment. In prediction tasks, if the underlying game or biological system features diminishing returns to cooperation or competition, the efficiency loss from selfish behavior (and by extension, the potential efficiency gain from adding punishment) may be much smaller than predicted by linear models. However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the paper does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of enabling punishment in a PGG. It does, however, suggest that in environments with diminishing returns, the baseline efficiency may already be high, potentially reducing the incremental value of punishment.

- source: Iwasa, Y., Uchida, T., & Yokomizo, H. (2007). Nonlinear behavior of the socio-economic dynamics for lake eutrophication control. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the interplay between social pressure (which increases with both the number of cooperators and the level of pollution) and individual cost-benefit decisions can lead to complex group-level outcomes. Depending on parameter values, the system can settle into a high-cooperation/clean-water equilibrium, a low-cooperation/polluted equilibrium, or exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics. The model shows that increasing the effectiveness of pollution-reducing technology can paradoxically worsen water quality if it reduces the perceived need for cooperation. Conservatism (slower behavioral change) can stabilize cooperation and maintain higher group welfare. The model does not include explicit punishment, but social pressure is discussed as a possible proxy for reputational or sanctioning effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how social feedbacks and behavioral dynamics can affect group efficiency in public-goods-like settings. While it does not directly model punishment, it suggests that mechanisms increasing social pressure (which could include punishment, reputation, or social norms) can promote cooperation and improve group outcomes, but may also lead to multiple stable states or complex dynamics. The findings support the idea that the structure and strength of social incentives (including, potentially, punishment) are critical moderators of efficiency. However, the absence of explicit punishment or experimental manipulation limits the direct applicability of the results to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in empirical PGGs.

- source: Panchanathan, K., & Boyd, R. (2004). Indirect reciprocity can stabilize cooperation without the second-order free rider problem. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that linking collective action (public goods contribution) to indirect reciprocity (mutual aid based on reputation) can stabilize cooperation through exclusion of non-contributors from future aid. This mechanism avoids the second-order free rider problem because punishers (Shunners) do not incur a direct cost; instead, they withhold aid from defectors, which is socially sanctioned. The model identifies conditions under which this strategy is evolutionarily stable and shows that both full cooperation (Shunner) and full defection (Defector) can be stable equilibria, depending on initial frequencies and assortment. The model also shows that strategies linking collective action and indirect reciprocity cannot easily invade populations practicing only indirect reciprocity. While the model discusses group payoff and welfare in the context of equilibrium selection, it does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that indirect punishment via exclusion (rather than direct costly punishment) can stabilize cooperation in public goods games, especially when reputation is visible and linked across domains. It suggests that such mechanisms can lead to higher group payoff and welfare, and that equilibrium selection processes (e.g., group selection, random drift) may favor these strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment in a standard PGG. Its relevance is strongest for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion from future benefits rather than direct payoff reduction. The model's insights are most applicable to predicting when and why punishment-like mechanisms (especially exclusion) might be effective, but less so for precise efficiency prediction in standard experimental PGGs with direct costly punishment.

- source: Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2012). Risk-driven migration and the collective-risk social dilemma. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study shows that risk-driven migration, where players move in response to the risk of collective failure, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a collective-risk social dilemma. Cooperation is promoted most effectively at intermediate population densities and when the collective target is high and the risk function is not too flat. The mechanism outperforms random migration, which can actually inhibit cooperation. The results highlight the importance of spatial structure and self-organized mobility for sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding how migration and risk of collective loss affect cooperation in public-goods-like environments, but it does not directly address the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms; instead, it uses a collective-risk loss as a sanction for group underperformance. Therefore, while the findings may inform predictions about the role of endogenous migration and risk in promoting cooperation, they do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency as required by the downstream prediction task. Use this paper for contextual or mechanistic insight, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hauert, C. (2006). Spatial effects in social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that spatial structure can both promote and inhibit cooperation in social dilemmas, depending on the type of game (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, by-product mutualism, coordination) and the microscopic update rule. In strict prisoner's dilemma or public goods settings, spatial structure allows cooperators to survive by forming clusters, but the parameter range for this effect is limited. In snowdrift-type games, spatial structure can actually reduce cooperation. The effect of spatial structure is highly sensitive to the update rule: the death-birth process is most favorable to cooperation, while the replicator rule can lead to reduced cooperation in some parameter regions. The main outcome is the equilibrium fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it is informative about how spatial structure, group size, cost-benefit ratios, and update rules affect the equilibrium level of cooperation in group social dilemmas. If the prediction task involves spatial structure or local interactions, the findings suggest that such structure can have complex, sometimes counterintuitive effects on cooperation, which may indirectly affect efficiency. The absence of punishment as a mechanism means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The main value is in understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatially structured games.

  findings: The review synthesizes evidence that mechanisms such as reputation, reciprocity, and social preferences (including egalitarianism and parochialism) are central to sustaining cooperation in social dilemmas. It highlights that in repeated public goods games, cooperation can be stabilized by making contributions observable and by enabling accountability through punishment or gossip. The review notes that punishment can promote cooperation, but also that gossip may be as effective as punishment and more efficient due to lower costs. The paper discusses the challenges of scaling cooperation to large groups and complex real-world dilemmas, emphasizing the importance of observability, local interactions, and psychological proximity. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This review is useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment, reputation, and observability can promote cooperation in public goods games and related social dilemmas. It provides conceptual support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation, but also cautions that gossip and reputation mechanisms may be equally or more efficient due to lower costs. The review does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects, so it should be used for contextual and mechanistic understanding rather than for parameterizing predictive models of efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the inclusion of observability, reputation, and punishment as important moderators, but does not allow for direct estimation of their effects on efficiency.

The evolutionary dynamics show that risk is a key driver for cooperation: without risk, defection dominates, but with increasing risk, stable coexistence or dominance of cooperation can emerge. The model provides analytical and simulation results for both infinite and finite populations, showing how group size, risk, and threshold interact to determine the likelihood of cooperation and the average payoff. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are modeled.

It provides strong theoretical evidence that group size, risk level, and threshold requirements are key moderators of efficiency and cooperation. However, since punishment is not modeled, the paper does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. Its results are most relevant for understanding baseline efficiency in collective-risk settings and for interpreting the effects of group size, risk, and threshold, but not for the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Bach, L. A., Helvik, T., & Christiansen, F. B. (2006). The evolution of n-player cooperation - threshold games and ESS bifurcations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in n-player threshold public goods games, the existence and stability of cooperative equilibria depend critically on the cost-benefit ratio and the threshold required for the collective benefit. For certain parameter regions, both cooperative and defecting equilibria can coexist, and small changes in parameters can lead to sudden transitions (catastrophes) in group efficiency and cooperation. The model predicts hysteresis: once a population falls into full defection, simply restoring favorable parameters may not be sufficient to recover cooperation. The analysis provides explicit mathematical conditions for these transitions and demonstrates that such complex dynamics are only possible in games with at least three players. No punishment or reward mechanisms are included.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, threshold structure, and cost-benefit parameters affect the efficiency and stability of cooperation in public-goods-like games without punishment. It is directly relevant for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the benefit is all-or-nothing and depends on a minimum number of cooperators. However, it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions, as these mechanisms are not modeled. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for understanding how efficiency varies with game design in the absence of punishment, but provides no evidence on how enabling punishment would change efficiency.

The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on cooperation rates under mutual selection and threshold group formation may be contextually informative for understanding how group composition and selection mechanisms affect cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency outcomes in games with or without punishment.

- source: ELLISON, G. (1994). COOPERATION IN THE PRISONERS-DILEMMA WITH ANONYMOUS RANDOM MATCHING. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that contagious punishment strategies can sustain cooperation and near-efficient payoffs in large, anonymous, randomly-matched repeated prisoner's dilemma games. The equilibrium is robust to small amounts of noise (players making mistakes) and does not require excessive patience except in very large populations or when the gain from defection is very small. The efficiency loss due to noise can be made arbitrarily small as the noise probability decreases. The results hold even without public randomization devices, though the required patience may increase in some parameter regimes. The analysis provides explicit conditions (in terms of discount factor, population size, and gain from defection) under which efficient cooperation is possible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment mechanisms (here, contagious punishment) can enable high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, even under severe informational constraints (anonymity, random matching, no identification, no communication). The results are most directly applicable to repeated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that, for games with similar structure and sufficient patience, enabling punishment can substantially increase efficiency relative to the baseline (no punishment) case. The paper also provides explicit formulas and numerical examples for the minimum patience (discount factor) required for cooperation at different population sizes and temptation levels. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that punishment can enable near-full efficiency in repeated, anonymous, randomly-matched games, provided the population is not too large, the gain from defection is not too high, and players are sufficiently patient. The results are robust to small noise and do not require public randomization, though the patience requirement may increase in those cases.

- source: Lippert, S., & Spagnolo, G. (2011). Networks of relations and Word-of-Mouth Communication. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in networks of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, the ability to transmit information (word-of-mouth) and the use of forgiving, targeted punishment strategies (Multilateral Repentance) allow for cooperation to be sustained under a wider range of conditions (i.e., lower discount factors) than under traditional contagion (Grim Trigger) strategies. This leads to higher efficiency and welfare, especially during punishment phases, as only defectors are punished and cooperation among others continues. The structure of the network (closure, density) and the speed of information transmission further moderate these effects. The results are formalized in terms of equilibrium payoff conditions and sustainability criteria.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling targeted, forgiving punishment (as opposed to all-or-nothing breakdowns) and facilitating information transmission (communication) in networked repeated social dilemmas increases the efficiency of cooperation. The results suggest that, all else equal, punishment regimes that are more targeted and allow for communication will yield higher efficiency than those that rely on contagious, permanent breakdowns. The paper also highlights the importance of network structure (closure, density) and information speed, which may inform how design dimensions like communication, punishment technology, and information feedback affect efficiency. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Hilbe, C., & Sigmund, K. (2010). Incentives and opportunism: from the carrot to the stick. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model demonstrates that negative incentives (punishment) can stabilize cooperation and lead to higher average payoffs (efficiency) in the population, provided that players have enough information about others' reputations. Rewarding (positive incentives) is not stable in the long run but is important for catalyzing the initial emergence of cooperation, especially when information is limited. When both punishment and reward are available, the population transitions from defection to cooperation more quickly, and the stable outcome is a cooperative, high-payoff equilibrium enforced by punishment. The results are robust to population size and mutation structure, and the authors discuss how the findings generalize to n-person public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase efficiency (average group payoff), especially when players can observe or infer others' incentive-providing behavior. The model suggests that the effect of punishment is more pronounced when information about others is available, and that the presence of reward mechanisms can accelerate the transition to cooperation but does not affect the long-run efficiency as much as punishment does. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, two-stage games but are argued to generalize to n-person PGGs. For prediction tasks, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when information/reputation is high, and suggests that the marginal benefit of adding reward is mainly in catalyzing cooperation rather than sustaining higher efficiency in the long run.

- source: Bendor, J., & Swistak, P. (2001). The evolution of norms. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated games of enforceable cooperation, only social norms (strategies that prescribe third-party punishment) can be evolutionarily stable. The presence of effective punishment is necessary for stability. Both efficient and inefficient social norms can be stable, but efficient (cooperative) norms are more robust to invasion and require a lower frequency to stabilize. The model predicts that, over time, groups are more likely to drift toward efficient, cooperative equilibria regulated by social norms, but inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria are also possible and robust. The findings are general for two-person repeated games and extend to sparse networks, provided third-party information is sufficiently available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the necessity of punishment (sanctions) for stable cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. It shows that the presence of punishment mechanisms is a necessary condition for the evolutionary stability of cooperative, efficient outcomes, but does not guarantee efficiency: inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria can also be stable. However, more efficient equilibria are more robust to invasion and require a lower threshold to stabilize. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in a repeated game with enforceable cooperation will generally increase the likelihood of stable cooperation and higher efficiency, but the actual efficiency achieved may depend on the specific norm that evolves. The robustness of efficient equilibria to invasion is higher, so in the long run, efficiency is more likely with punishment enabled, but inefficient outcomes are possible. The results are most directly applicable to two-person repeated games, but the logic extends to larger groups and sparse networks if third-party information is available.

- source: dos Santos, M., Rankin, D. J., & Wedekind, C. (2011). The evolution of punishment through reputation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that punishment, when reputationally visible, can evolve and be stable because it increases the likelihood of receiving help in the future, offsetting its immediate cost. Analytical and simulation results show that punishing discriminators (who punish defectors and are recognized for it) achieve higher payoffs and can dominate, leading to high cooperation and group efficiency. The effect is robust to a range of costs and benefits, but is sensitive to the accuracy of reputation information and the mutation rate. The findings suggest that reputation is a key moderator of punishment's efficiency-enhancing effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment depend critically on whether punishment is reputationally visible and can influence future interactions. In environments where reputation for punishing is tracked and used by others to condition their cooperation, punishment can increase group efficiency and stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of costs and benefits, but efficiency gains are reduced if reputation information is noisy or mutation rates are low. For prediction, the presence of a reputation system that makes punishment visible is a key moderator for positive efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Ali, S. N., & Miller, D. A. (2016). Ostracism and Forgiveness. *American Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated partnership games with private monitoring and evidentiary communication, permanent ostracism (permanent exclusion of deviators) cannot achieve higher efficiency or group payoff than bilateral enforcement, because victims have incentives to conceal deviations rather than report them. This limits the effectiveness of the harshest forms of community punishment. However, if ostracism is temporary and includes forgiveness (eventual readmission of deviators), then higher levels of cooperation and group payoff can be sustained, as the prospect of future cooperation with forgiven players incentivizes truthful reporting. The paper provides formal bounds and equilibrium constructions for these results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the design of punishment—specifically, whether it is permanent or temporary (with forgiveness)—is a critical moderator of the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated partnership games with private monitoring. If punishment is permanent ostracism, efficiency does not improve over bilateral enforcement. If punishment is temporary (forgiveness is possible), efficiency can be strictly higher, especially as patience or group size increases. For prediction tasks, the presence of forgiveness/temporary ostracism should be coded as a key moderator, and the effect of punishment on efficiency should be expected to be positive only when forgiveness is possible. The results are derived in a variable-stakes partnership game, which is adjacent to but not exactly a standard public goods game.

- source: Yan, F., Chen, X. J., Qiu, Z. P., & Szolnoki, A. (2021). Cooperator driven oscillation in a time-delayed feedback-evolving game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment (probabilistic detection and fine for defectors) can stabilize cooperation and maintain the common resource at sustainable levels, but only if the product of detection probability and fine (pβ) is sufficiently large relative to the cost of cooperation or the reinvestment rate. When punishment is strong, the system converges to a stable, efficient state with high cooperation and resource sustainability. When punishment is weak, defectors dominate and the resource is depleted. If cooperator reinvestment is proportional to endowment and there is a time delay in environmental feedback, the system can exhibit persistent oscillations in cooperation and resource levels, especially when punishment is moderate and resource growth is not too fast or too slow. The critical threshold for punishment effectiveness and the impact of time delay are analytically characterized. The model provides explicit conditions under which punishment improves or fails to improve group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how institutional punishment (with known cost and effectiveness) affects group efficiency in a common-pool resource game with feedback dynamics. The results are most relevant for adjacent PGG-like environments with renewable resources and environmental feedback. The model gives explicit formulas for the stationary group payoff/resource level as a function of punishment parameters, player count, resource growth, and reinvestment structure. For prediction, the key insight is that increasing punishment effectiveness (higher pβ) generally increases efficiency up to a threshold, above which full cooperation and sustainability are achieved. However, if punishment is too weak, efficiency remains low. Time delay in feedback can destabilize the system and induce oscillations, but does not always reduce average efficiency. The findings are most transferable to settings with institutional punishment, renewable resources, and feedback between group action and group payoff.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Indirect reciprocity provides only a narrow margin of efficiency for costly punishment. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in models of indirect reciprocity, strategies that use costly punishment (punishing defectors at a cost to oneself) are almost always less efficient than strategies that simply withhold cooperation from defectors. Costly punishment can only be the most efficient equilibrium in a very small region of parameter space, requiring fine-tuned values of benefit, cost, punishment cost, punishment impact, and reputation accuracy. In most cases, the highest group payoff (efficiency) is achieved by not using costly punishment. The results are robust to errors in reputation assignment and action execution, as long as these errors are not too frequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity settings (which are adjacent to public goods games), enabling costly punishment will almost always reduce group efficiency compared to not enabling punishment, except in a very narrow set of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this suggests that unless the game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment impact, benefit/cost ratio, reputation accuracy) are in the narrow region identified, enabling punishment should be expected to lower efficiency. The findings are most directly applicable to indirect reciprocity games with binary reputation and second-order norms, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar structure.

- source: Cai, G. S., & Kock, N. (2009). An evolutionary game theoretic perspective on e-collaboration: The collaboration effort and media relativeness. *European Journal of Operational Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player e-collaboration games, introducing or increasing social punishment for defection increases the likelihood of full cooperation, which is Pareto efficient and maximizes group payoff. When punishment is low, defection or asymmetric cooperation is stable; when punishment exceeds a threshold (typically half the cost of cooperation), full cooperation becomes the unique stable Nash equilibrium. This result holds in both discrete and continuous effort models. The paper also shows that the required punishment to induce cooperation decreases as collaboration efficiency increases. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium characterizations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling or increasing punishment in a two-player, public-goods-like game will increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong relative to the cost of cooperation. The analytic results specify the threshold at which punishment changes the equilibrium from defection to cooperation. While the model is for two players and not a standard PGG, the structure is closely related, and the findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in small-group cooperation games. The results are most applicable when the punishment mechanism is salient, effective, and the cost/benefit structure is similar to the model.

- source: KANDORI, M. (1992). SOCIAL NORMS AND COMMUNITY ENFORCEMENT. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that informal sanctions (punishment by community members) can support efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, provided there is sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized labels/reputation). Under public observability, any efficient and individually rational outcome can be sustained as an equilibrium, regardless of population size or matching rule. With only private experience and no information processing, cooperation (and thus efficiency) can sometimes be sustained via a 'contagious equilibrium,' but this is fragile and breaks down in large populations. Introducing local information processing (labels/reputation) allows for robust, straightforward equilibria that sustain efficiency for any population size and matching rule. The paper provides explicit equilibrium constructions and conditions for when these results hold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the presence of a punishment mechanism, when combined with sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized reputation/labels), enables the group to achieve efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, even with random matching and large populations. For the prediction task, this suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency to the maximum feasible level, provided the game design allows for sufficient information about past actions (e.g., public histories, visible labels, or reputation). If information is very limited (players only know their own history), punishment is less effective, and efficiency gains are limited, especially as group size increases. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes but gives clear qualitative predictions about the moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Wang, S. X., Chen, X. J., Xiao, Z. L., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2023). Optimization of institutional incentives for cooperation in structured populations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both institutional punishment and reward can be tuned to achieve near-full cooperation in structured populations, with the optimal protocol being time-invariant and identical for both types of incentives under a given update rule. The minimal cumulative cost to the institution depends on the initial fraction of cooperators: punishment is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is high, while reward is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is low. The results are supported by analytical derivations and simulations across regular, random, small-world, and scale-free networks. The main outcome is the cumulative cost to the institution, which is a proxy for efficiency from the perspective of implementing cooperation-promoting interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how the cost-effectiveness of punishment (and reward) depends on game parameters such as network structure, update rule, and initial cooperation level. While it does not report group efficiency in the standard sense, its focus on the cumulative cost to achieve high cooperation is closely related and can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in similar structured games. The results suggest that, with optimally tuned punishment, high cooperation (and thus high group efficiency) can be achieved at lower institutional cost when initial cooperation is already substantial. The findings are most directly applicable to structured Prisoner's Dilemma settings with institutional (not peer) punishment or reward, and may be less directly transferable to standard public goods games.

- source: Rand, D. G., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Direct reciprocity with costly punishment: Generous tit-for-tat prevails. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, the addition of a punishment option does not increase cooperation or group payoffs in evolutionary simulations. Even when Nash equilibrium analysis allows for cooperative equilibria using punishment, evolutionary selection in finite populations favors generous tit-for-tat strategies that do not use costly punishment. Costly punishment is rarely used, and when it is, it is associated with lower payoffs. The presence of costly punishment does not allow cooperation to evolve in parameter regions where it would not already be supported by direct reciprocity alone. These findings are robust to variations in game parameters and are supported by experimental data from Dreber et al. (2008).
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling costly punishment in a repeated two-player PD-like environment does not increase efficiency or group payoff, regardless of the punishment cost or effectiveness. Evolutionary dynamics select against strategies that use costly punishment, and the most successful strategies are those that reciprocate cooperation and defect against defectors (generous tit-for-tat), not those that punish. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar repeated games, one should not expect an increase in efficiency or group payoff, and may even expect a negative association between punishment use and payoff. However, the results are specific to repeated two-player PDs and may not generalize to larger group public goods games.

- source: CLUTTONBROCK, T. H., & PARKER, G. A. (1995). PUNISHMENT IN ANIMAL SOCIETIES. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical models show that punishment can be stable and effective in animal societies, especially when dominants can punish subordinates at low cost and with high impact. Punishment can enforce cooperation, deter cheating, and maintain social order. The models identify conditions under which punishment is likely to evolve, such as strong asymmetries in power or fighting ability. Empirical examples illustrate that punishment is widespread in animal societies and can modify behavior, but the paper does not report quantitative effects on group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that punishment can increase the fitness of punishers by reducing the frequency of damaging actions by others, but do not directly address group-level efficiency as in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment can enforce cooperation and deter selfishness, which is relevant to predicting the effects of punishment in PGG-like games. However, the models are dyadic, focus on fitness rather than explicit group payoff, and do not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes. The paper suggests that punishment is more likely to be effective when the cost to the punisher is low and the impact on the punished is high, and when there are clear asymmetries in power. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the plausibility of positive effects of punishment on cooperation and (by analogy) efficiency, but does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence for use in predictive modeling.

- source: Ågren, J. A., Davies, N. G., & Foster, K. R. (2019). Enforcement is central to the evolution of cooperation. *Nature Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enforcement mechanisms—defined as actions that reduce selfishness within cooperative alliances—are predicted by evolutionary models to evolve across a wide range of biological systems, from genomes to human societies. The models show that, in the absence of enforcement, selfishness undermines cooperation and reduces group fitness. When enforcement is possible, it evolves under many parameter regimes and leads to higher cooperation and improved collective outcomes. Empirical examples from animal societies, mutualisms, and cellular systems support the theoretical predictions. The effectiveness of enforcement depends on its cost and the structure of the system, but its presence is generally associated with higher group efficiency or fitness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment or enforcement in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency or collective payoff, relative to control conditions without enforcement. The models are not direct PGGs but are structurally analogous, and the mechanisms (punishment, policing, sanctions) map closely to punishment in PGGs. The effect is generally more positive, but the magnitude depends on the cost of enforcement and system details. The paper also highlights that enforcement is most critical when relatedness is low and that high enforcement costs can limit its effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that adding punishment will increase efficiency, especially when enforcement is not prohibitively costly.

- source: Sánchez, A., & Cuesta, J. A. (2005). Altruism may arise from individual selection. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (altruistic punishment) can arise and persist in agent-based evolutionary models of the Ultimatum Game under individual selection, without the need for group or cultural selection, repeated interactions, or reputation. The key mechanism is the role of fluctuations and finite selection intervals, which allow altruistic punishers to survive and proliferate. The model reproduces the empirical finding that offers and acceptance thresholds are much higher than the self-interested Nash equilibrium. However, the paper does not analyze or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the distribution and evolution of behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the possibility that punishment (in the form of rejection of unfair offers) can evolve and persist under individual selection in one-shot, anonymous games. However, it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding the evolutionary plausibility of punishment behaviors, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited, though it may inform mechanistic expectations about the robustness of punishment behaviors.

- source: Shinada, M., Yamagishi, T., & Ohmura, Y. (2004). False friends are worse than bitter enemies: Altruistic punishment of in-group members. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The bidirectional supervision mechanism, where players can check and avoid corrupt umpires, is effective at suppressing both corruption and defection in a spatial donation game with third-party punishment. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the punishment amount (must be within a certain range), the bribe amount (must not be too high), and the ratio of tax to checking cost (higher tax or lower checking cost is better). The benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation has little effect within reasonable ranges. The main outcomes are the fractions of different strategies (cooperation, defection, corruption, fairness) in the population. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but argues that the mechanism is most beneficial to collective interests when parameters are tuned appropriately.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the design of punishment, bribe, tax, and checking cost parameters can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation and corruption in a donation game with third-party punishment. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as qualitative or mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction of efficiency. The results suggest that punishment is only effective at suppressing defection and corruption if its severity is within a certain range and if corruption incentives (bribes) are not too high. The presence of a mechanism for players to avoid corrupt punishers is critical. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs with third-party punishment and corruption, but less directly transferable to standard public goods games or to quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Promoting cooperation by punishing minority. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a multilayer network with inter-layer feedback and reinforcement learning, cooperation is promoted by lower punishment costs and higher punishment severity. The frequency of cooperation is sensitive to these parameters, with cooperation collapsing when punishment is too costly or too weak. The self-adapting Q-learning mechanism enables agents to learn strategies that support higher cooperation rates, especially when learning rates and discount factors are tuned appropriately. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment cost and severity affect cooperation rates in a spatially structured, multilayer environment with reinforcement learning. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation under certain cost/severity regimes, but excessive punishment cost can undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper's results are only indirectly informative, as it does not quantify group payoff or efficiency. The model is also not a standard public goods game, but rather a spatial prisoner's dilemma with punishment administered by a management layer.

- source: Yang, H. X., Wu, Z. X., Rong, Z. H., & Lai, Y. C. (2015). Peer pressure: Enhancement of cooperation through mutual punishment. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that mutual (symmetric) punishment, where individuals punish neighbors with different strategies, can enhance cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. In well-mixed populations, cooperation can only be sustained if the initial density of cooperators is above a threshold, and if the punishment fine is above a critical value. In structured populations (square lattice), there is often an optimal, moderate punishment fine that maximizes cooperation, especially when the initial density of cooperators is not too low. Too weak or too harsh punishment suppresses cooperation. The main outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform prediction of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with punishment. However, it provides indirect evidence that symmetric, mutual punishment can increase cooperation rates under certain conditions, especially with moderate punishment severity and sufficient initial cooperation. The results are based on the prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and do not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, the paper is only adjacent for the prediction task, and should be used with caution, mainly as qualitative support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation, but not necessarily efficiency.

- source: Wang, Z., Xia, C. Y., Meloni, S., Zhou, C. S., & Moreno, Y. (2013). Impact of Social Punishment on Cooperative Behavior in Complex Networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The introduction of a punisher strategy (who pays a cost to reduce defectors' payoffs) in networked 2-person games (PD and SD) increases the fraction of cooperative strategies (cooperators and punishers) at equilibrium, across a range of network topologies (lattice, ER, scale-free) and punishment severities. The effect is robust: even small punishment fines can substantially increase cooperation. The paper analyzes the spatial and network organization of strategies, showing that punishers can form protective clusters that shield cooperators from defectors. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that social punishment increases the prevalence of cooperative strategies in 2-person networked games, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. The game structure is not a public goods game but a 2-person PD/SD, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Perc, M., Donnay, K., & Helbing, D. (2013). Understanding Recurrent Crime as System-Immanent Collective Behavior. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that increasing punishment or inspection does not always reduce crime; instead, the system exhibits complex, sometimes counterintuitive dynamics, including cyclic dominance and abrupt phase transitions between different equilibria (e.g., dominance of criminals, coexistence of criminals and inspectors, or cyclic coexistence of all three strategies). The effectiveness of punishment depends critically on the temptation to commit crime, the cost and incentive for inspection, and the structure of social interactions. The model demonstrates that crime can be recurrent and robust to increased punishment, especially when spatial structure and local interactions are considered. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to predictions about group welfare or efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on crime (and by analogy, on cooperation in social dilemmas) is highly sensitive to the interplay of temptation, punishment cost, and network structure, and that simple linear predictions (e.g., more punishment always increases efficiency) are likely to fail. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for non-monotonic, phase-transition-like effects and the importance of considering spatial and network structure. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests caution in assuming monotonic or simple effects of punishment, especially in spatially structured or networked environments.

When helping is also available as a signal, the use and signaling value of TPP are reduced, while helping remains a strong signal. The results support a reputational account of TPP, showing that people may punish to signal their character rather than solely to deter selfishness. - source: Pothos, E. M., Perry, G., Corr, P. J., Matthew, M. R., & Busemeyer, J. R. (2011). Understanding cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma game. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that higher punishment severity, higher disguise cost, lower cost-to-benefit ratio, and larger group size all promote the extinction of defectors and the prevalence of cooperation (cooperators and punishers). When these parameters are less favorable, the system remains in a mixed state with both cooperators and defectors. The model shows that the system typically evolves to a state with only two strategies (either cooperators and punishers, or cooperators and defectors), and the boundaries between these regimes are mapped as functions of the key parameters. The results are robust to both deterministic replicator dynamics and stochastic mutation-selection dynamics in finite populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence about how punishment parameters (cost, effectiveness), group size, and cost-to-benefit ratio affect the prevalence of cooperation in an N-person social dilemma with punishment. While the model is for the snowdrift game (not a standard PGG), and the main outcomes are behavioral frequencies rather than efficiency, the results suggest that increasing punishment severity, increasing group size, and increasing the cost for defectors to avoid punishment all make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff ratios, so direct prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible. The findings are most useful for understanding qualitative moderators of punishment effectiveness in related social dilemmas.

- source: Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2015). A double-edged sword: Benefits and pitfalls of heterogeneous punishment in evolutionary inspection games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous punishment (punishers who invest different amounts in sanctioning) can reduce the prevalence of second-order free-riders (ordinary people) compared to uniform punishment, but this does not always translate into lower crime rates. In some parameter regions, uniform punishment is as effective or more effective at reducing crime. The system exhibits complex phase transitions and cyclic dominance, which maintain the coexistence of criminals, punishers, and ordinary people. The main obstacle to eliminating crime is the ability of ordinary people to free-ride on the efforts of punishers. The results are presented as fractions of strategies in the population, not as efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding the role of punishment heterogeneity and second-order free-riding. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict changes in efficiency when punishment is enabled. The findings suggest that the presence of second-order free-riders and the structure of punishment (uniform vs. heterogeneous) can have complex, non-monotonic effects on cooperation and norm enforcement, which may inform qualitative expectations about efficiency in related games. The results highlight the importance of considering strategy diversity and cyclic dominance in predicting the effects of punishment, but quantitative predictions about efficiency require additional evidence.

- source: Bingham, P. M. (1999). Human uniqueness: A general theory. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.

  findings: The central finding is that coalitional enforcement—cooperative, low-cost punishment of defectors—was necessary and sufficient for the evolution of large-scale, stable cooperation among unrelated individuals in humans. The theory predicts that as the efficiency and scale of coalitional enforcement increased (via technological and social monitoring advances), so did the extent and complexity of human cooperation. The paper discusses how this mechanism underlies the emergence of language, culture, large political units, and rapid technological progress. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative findings about efficiency or payoffs in public goods game-like environments; rather, it offers a theoretical framework for understanding the evolutionary roots of cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment (specifically, coalitional or group-based punishment) can enable and stabilize high levels of cooperation among non-kin, by reducing the individual cost of enforcement and making defection unprofitable. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence and efficiency of punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of group cooperation and, by extension, group efficiency. However, because the paper does not report empirical or experimental data on efficiency or payoffs in public goods games, it cannot be used to calibrate quantitative predictions. Its main value is in supporting the general principle that more efficient, lower-cost, and more widely distributed punishment mechanisms should be associated with higher group cooperation and efficiency, all else equal.

  findings: The model shows that the introduction of punishment (policing) as a third strategy in a repeated PD game can lead to stable coexistence or oscillatory dominance cycles among cooperators, defectors, and punishers, depending on ecological and game parameters. The strength of punishment, temptation to defect, and ecological benefits (from 'free space') all modulate which strategies persist. Moderate punishment can suppress defectors and promote cooperation, but high temptation to defect can still undermine cooperation. The model also demonstrates that initial conditions and parameter choices can lead to multistability, with different attractors (e.g., dominance by one strategy, coexistence, or extinction). The focus is on the eco-evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total welfare.

The model is based on a repeated PD game, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation. Therefore, while the findings support the idea that punishment can help sustain cooperation under certain conditions, they do not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency changes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment.   findings: The paper finds that in small-scale, mobile hunter-gatherer societies, both punitive and positive social sanctioning are pervasive and target behaviors relevant to group cooperation, such as sharing, generosity, and suppression of dominance or cheating. Punishment (including ostracism and even capital punishment) is used to control antisocial behaviors, while positive sanctioning (reputation, social approval, preferential partnerships) rewards prosocial behaviors. The author theorizes that these mechanisms have had significant evolutionary effects, shaping both the prevalence of altruism and the development of conscience in humans. However, the findings are based on ethnographic coding and theoretical reasoning, not on experimental or quantitative analysis of efficiency or payoffs.

However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but does not provide data or effect sizes relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

  findings: The paper reviews and synthesizes sociological theories of norms, focusing on why and how people enforce norms (including through punishment and reward), and the implications for social order and inequality. It distinguishes between consequentialist (cooperation-focused), relational (coordination-focused), and agentic (contextual/individual-focused) approaches. The review highlights that sanctions (punishment and reward) can increase norm compliance, but also that enforcement can reinforce inequalities and sometimes undermine collective welfare, depending on the structure of interests and relationships. The paper does not report new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or group payoff in experimental games.

- source: López-Pérez, R. (2008). Aversion to norm-breaking:: A model. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a formal model in which agents internalize social norms (specifically, an 'E-norm' combining efficiency and equality) and experience emotional costs (shame, anger) when violating or observing violations of these norms. The model predicts that people will cooperate and punish norm violators even at a cost to themselves, and that punishment is targeted at those responsible for norm violations. The model is shown to qualitatively account for a range of experimental findings in games such as dictator, ultimatum, trust, centipede, Cournot, and public goods games. In the context of public goods games, the model predicts that the introduction of punishment opportunities increases cooperation by deterring selfish behavior, as selfish agents comply to avoid being punished by principled agents. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical rationale for why punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance in public goods and related games, suggesting that the presence of punishment opportunities should increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment, especially when a sufficient proportion of players are 'principled' and willing to punish norm violations. However, the model is qualitative and does not specify the magnitude of efficiency gains or how they depend on specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR). The model does not provide direct evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game parameters, but supports the general expectation that punishment opportunities will have a more positive effect on efficiency in environments where norm internalization and willingness to punish are present.

- source: Stevens, J. R., Cushman, F. A., & Hauser, M. D. (2005). Evolving the psychological mechanisms for cooperation. *Annual Review of Ecology Evolution and Systematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, although theoretical models (including those based on the Prisoner's Dilemma) predict that punishment can stabilize cooperation, actual evidence for punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in nonhuman animals is rare. Most observed 'punishment' is better characterized as harassment, which yields immediate rather than delayed benefits. The rarity of punishment and reciprocity in animals is attributed to cognitive constraints, particularly the need for individual recognition, low temporal discounting, and sufficient memory to track interactions. In contrast, humans possess cognitive adaptations that enable widespread use of punishment and reciprocity to sustain cooperation. The paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for why punishment may or may not be effective in sustaining cooperation, especially in nonhuman animals. It highlights the importance of cognitive prerequisites (recognition, memory, discounting) for punishment to function as a stabilizer of cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence or data that can be used to predict the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar experimental designs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on whether the game environment allows for individual recognition, repeated interactions, and low discounting, but it does not quantify these effects or relate them to efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Iwasa, Y. (2004). How should we define goodness? Reputation dynamics in indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that only a small subset of reputation assignment rules—those that treat defection against bad individuals as good (i.e., as a form of justified punishment or sanction)—can sustain high efficiency (group payoff close to the maximum possible) in indirect reciprocity games. These 'leading eight' rules enable stable cooperation by allowing individuals to refuse help to bad actors without losing their own good reputation. The model shows that, under these rules, the average payoff in the population approaches the maximum possible (b-c), minus a small term due to errors. The results are robust to error rates and to the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as b > c. The paper provides a formal evolutionary justification for the importance of sanctioning/punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in reputation-based social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms—specifically, the ability to sanction or refuse help to bad actors without reputational cost—are critical for sustaining high efficiency in indirect reciprocity environments. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related: it is a repeated, pairwise helping game with a social dilemma structure. The findings suggest that, in environments where individuals can sanction defectors (and where such sanctioning is not itself punished), group efficiency can approach the maximum possible. For prediction tasks, this implies that enabling punishment or justified sanctioning in a game design is likely to increase efficiency, provided the reputation system is designed so that justified punishment does not harm the punisher's reputation. The results are robust to errors and to variations in the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as cooperation is individually costly but socially beneficial.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2005). Indirect reciprocity, image scoring, and moral hazard. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
