  findings: The paper finds that in models of indirect reciprocity with binary reputation, cooperation can be stably maintained if individuals' knowledge of others' reputations increases over time (e.g., with age or experience). Costly punishment—refusing to help those with a Bad score, even at the risk of one's own reputation—can support a stable equilibrium of cooperation, provided the information environment is sufficiently rich. If information is too scarce, defectors dominate and cooperation collapses. The model demonstrates that the efficiency (total payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is higher when these conditions for stable cooperation are met, and that the presence of costly punishment is essential in the minimal model for resisting defectors. The findings are directly relevant to efficiency and payoff outcomes in social dilemmas with reputation and punishment-like mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment (in the form of costly refusal to help those with a Bad reputation) can increase efficiency in social dilemma environments, but only when the information environment allows for effective reputation tracking. The model is not a literal public goods game, but the mechanisms and payoff logic are closely related. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control only if players can reliably observe or infer others' reputations, and if the cost-to-benefit ratio of helping is not too high. The findings also highlight the importance of the number of rounds (or opportunities for information to accumulate) and the structure of information flow. However, the model does not address group size, communication, or some other public goods game design features directly.

- source: Levine, D. K., & Pesendorfer, W. (2007). The evolution of cooperation through imitation. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models where players can imitate successful strategies and occasionally innovate, the long-run outcome depends on the ability to identify others' strategies. With perfect identification, efficient cooperation enforced by punishment is stable and the group achieves high efficiency. With imperfect or noisy identification, punishment becomes less effective, and the long-run outcome is less efficient, sometimes even less efficient than the static Nash equilibrium. The model shows that punishment strategies that condition on signals about others' types can sustain cooperation, but only if those signals are sufficiently informative. The presence of strategies that can 'masquerade' as cooperators (i.e., avoid detection) can undermine cooperation and efficiency unless masquerading is costly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends on the information structure—specifically, the ability to identify defectors. If the game design allows for perfect or highly informative identification of defectors, punishment can sustain efficient cooperation. If identification is noisy or easily circumvented, punishment is less effective and may not improve efficiency. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, one should pay close attention to the information available to players about others' actions or types, and to the possibility of undetectable defection or masquerading. The model is not an empirical test, but it provides a mechanistic basis for expecting positive, null, or even negative effects of punishment on efficiency depending on these design features.

- source: Quiñones, A. E., van Doorn, G. S., Pen, I., Weissing, F. J., & Taborsky, M. (2016). Negotiation and appeasement can be more effective drivers of sociality than kin selection. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Negotiation and appeasement mechanisms, modeled as responsive strategies where dominants can threaten aggression (punishment) and subordinates can appease by helping, lead to higher group productivity than kin-selected (unconditional) helping. The model shows that when negotiation is possible, even unrelated individuals can achieve high levels of help and group output, with low realized aggression. High relatedness, however, reduces the effectiveness of punishment/enforcement, leading to lower help and lower group productivity. Thus, punishment-like negotiation mechanisms are more effective at driving efficient cooperation than kin selection alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of responsive punishment or enforcement mechanisms (negotiation) increases group efficiency in public-goods-like environments, compared to settings where only kin selection operates. The model is not a literal PGG, but the structure is closely analogous: individuals can help (contribute) or not, dominants can punish (aggression), and group output (productivity) is tracked. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment/negotiation will increase efficiency, especially when relatedness is low. The effect is robust across a range of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment-enabled conditions on efficiency, and highlights that high relatedness may reduce the marginal benefit of punishment.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The leading eight: Social norms that can maintain cooperation by indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations, only eight specific social norms (the 'leading eight') can maintain both evolutionary stability and a high level of cooperation/efficiency, even when errors in action and assessment occur. These norms require players to cooperate with good-reputation individuals, punish defectors, justify punishment, and allow for apology and forgiveness. Under these norms, the average payoff per interaction is close to the theoretical maximum (b-c), and the fraction of cooperative (good) players remains high, with only a small reduction due to errors. The paper provides analytical conditions for when these norms are stable and efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations and one-shot interactions, the structure of the social norm (reputation dynamics) is critical for achieving high efficiency. Specifically, norms that combine justified punishment (refusing to help bad actors and not being penalized for it) and forgiveness (allowing recovery from bad reputation) can maintain efficiency close to the maximum. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms in repeated or reputation-based social dilemmas. The results suggest that, when punishment is structured as justified refusal to cooperate (and not as costly peer punishment), and when forgiveness is possible, efficiency losses due to punishment can be minimized or avoided. The paper does not address group size, communication, or other PGG-specific dimensions directly, but it does show that the design of sanctioning and reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2008). Mutualism, market effects and partner control. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the threat of terminating an interaction (partner control) can suppress cheating and maintain cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on the relative abundance of exploiters and victims (market effects). When victims are rare, they can enforce cooperation and achieve higher payoffs; when they are common, exploiters can cheat more, reducing the efficiency of the interaction. If exploiters gain more control over the duration of interaction, they can extract more benefit, potentially driving the system into parasitism (negative payoffs for the victim). The model provides explicit conditions for when mutualism (positive payoffs) or parasitism (negative payoffs) will occur, and shows that efficiency is maximized when partner control is strong and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like partner control on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the structure of partner choice and market conditions. If the 'punishment' (exit threat) is credible and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters, efficiency is increased. If exploiters can easily find new victims, or if they gain control over the interaction, efficiency can decrease or even become negative for the exploited party. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency will depend on parameters analogous to partner abundance, search costs, and control over interaction duration. The model does not map directly onto standard PGGs, but its logic is relevant for settings where partner choice or exit is possible.

- source: Gintis, H. (2003). The hitchhiker's guide to altruism: Gene-culture coevolution, and the internalization of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that internalized prosocial norms, including altruistic punishment, can become evolutionarily stable through gene-culture coevolution, especially when the fitness benefits of prosocial norms are high, extraparental socialization is strong, and assortative mating is present. Simulations show that groups with internalized altruistic punishment achieve high mean fitness and eliminate antisocial norms. Altruistic punishment is particularly effective at sustaining cooperation and group efficiency because its cost is lower and more concentrated than the cost of direct cooperation. The models predict that, in the presence of punishment, group efficiency and mean fitness are higher than in its absence, especially in large groups or when repeated interaction is infrequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments increases group efficiency and mean payoff, especially when direct cooperation is not stable due to high costs. The models suggest that punishment is a robust mechanism for sustaining cooperation and high group payoff, even in large groups or with high migration, as long as the cost of punishment is low relative to the benefit of cooperation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where internalized norms and cultural transmission play a role, and less so to short-run laboratory games, but the core logic supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like dilemmas.

- source: Lehmann, L., & Keller, L. (2006). The evolution of cooperation and altruism - a general framework and a classification of models. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and altruism can only evolve when at least one of four general conditions is met: (1) direct benefits to the actor, (2) repeated interactions with information about partner behavior, (3) preferential interactions with kin, or (4) genetic linkage between recognition and helping traits (greenbeard effect). Punishment, coercion, and policing are highlighted as mechanisms that can shift the cost-benefit ratio in favor of cooperation, making it more likely to evolve and persist. The paper reviews and classifies a large number of theoretical models, showing that all can be mapped onto these four categories. It emphasizes that punishment is effective in promoting cooperation when it increases the cost of defection relative to cooperation, but notes that the stability of punishment depends on its own cost and on whether it is linked to cooperation genetically or behaviorally.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase the efficiency (fitness, group payoff, or productivity) of cooperative systems by making defection less attractive. It does not provide quantitative predictions for specific game designs, but it clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its ability to alter the cost-benefit ratio and on the presence of at least one of the four general conditions for cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments if it is not prohibitively costly and if it is implemented in a way that aligns with one of the four evolutionary pathways. However, the paper does not address specific design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, chat) in the context of laboratory or field experiments, so its guidance is primarily mechanistic and qualitative.

- source: Okada, I. (2020). A Review of Theoretical Studies on Indirect Reciprocity. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical advances in indirect reciprocity, showing that cooperation can be maintained in social dilemma settings when reputation mechanisms and justified punishment are present. It details how the refinement of assessment functions (e.g., leading eight norms) resolves issues like the scoring and punishment dilemmas, enabling stable cooperation under certain conditions. However, it notes that these models are not directly applicable to public goods games, as selective cooperation (the core of indirect reciprocity) is not possible in standard PGGs. The review also highlights the importance of the benefit-cost ratio, error rates, and the distinction between public and private reputation systems in determining the stability of cooperation. While punishment and reward mechanisms are discussed, the focus is on their role in norm stability and cooperation, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and reputation mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in social dilemmas, but it does not offer direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its models are adjacent to PGGs but differ in key structural ways (e.g., selective cooperation vs. public goods provision). The review is useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and norm refinement can support cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or comparative data on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment depends on norm structure, reputation information, and error rates, but it does not allow for direct mapping from game design dimensions to efficiency outcomes in PGGs.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Bshary, R. (2011). Resolving the iterated prisoner's dilemma: theory and reality. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that while the strict assumptions of the IPD are rarely met in nature, the pay-off structure (temptation to cheat, mutual investment) is relevant to many real-world cooperative interactions. Punishment (negative reciprocity) is identified as a mechanism that can sustain cooperation in IPD-like games, as illustrated by the cleaner fish example, where dominant individuals punish defectors to promote future cooperation. However, the paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral strategies and the conditions under which cooperation is sustained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation in iterated social dilemmas, especially when cooperation is not all-or-nothing and when partner choice or punishment is possible. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically vary game design dimensions. As such, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, but it does support the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in IPD-like settings.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect reciprocity, supported by reputation mechanisms and sometimes by costly punishment (altruistic punishment), can stabilize cooperation in social dilemmas. Theoretical models show that discriminating strategies (which punish defectors by withholding help) can be evolutionarily stable under certain conditions, especially when information about others' behavior is available. The paper reviews experimental evidence that reputation and information about others' past actions increase cooperation, and that costly punishment is observed in public goods and related games. However, the paper does not report direct effects of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games; instead, it focuses on the conditions for the evolution and maintenance of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when information about others' behavior is available. It suggests that punishment (including costly punishment) can help stabilize cooperation, which may lead to higher efficiency, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the importance of information, group size, and the cost/benefit structure, but does not allow direct mapping from control efficiency to treatment efficiency with punishment enabled. Its value is mainly in mechanism and moderator identification rather than direct effect estimation.

- source: Yang, Z. L., Shi, Y. Y., & Li, Y. C. (2018). Analysis of intellectual property cooperation behavior and its simulation under two types of scenarios using evolutionary game theory. *Computers & Industrial Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that under both market and administrative supervision mechanisms, certain policy levers (subsidies, liquidated damages, tax rates, penalties) can increase the probability and stability of cooperation among heterogeneous agents in intellectual property activities. However, the effect is non-monotonic: excessive penalties or taxes can reduce cooperation. The main findings are about the conditions under which the system converges to full cooperation, noncooperation, or mixed states, rather than about efficiency or total group payoff. The results are based on theoretical modeling and numerical simulation, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for how punishment-like mechanisms (liquidated damages, administrative penalties) and incentives (subsidies, tax breaks) affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-agent setting. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured, the results suggest that moderate punishment and incentives can stabilize cooperation, but excessive punishment or taxation can backfire. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the design and magnitude of punishment/reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Boehm, C. (1997). Impact of the human egalitarian syndrome on Darwinian selection mechanics. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that in prehistoric human forager societies, egalitarian social norms—enforced by moralistic punishment and social sanctions—dampened within-group competition and amplified between-group variation. This, in turn, empowered group selection and enabled the evolution and maintenance of altruistic and cooperative behaviors, despite the presence of free riders. The author argues that active punishment and social isolation of noncooperators reduced the free-rider problem, making group-beneficial traits more likely to persist. However, these arguments are theoretical and based on ethnographic and evolutionary reasoning, not on experimental or quantitative data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and social sanctions can promote cooperation and suppress free riding in group contexts, which is relevant to public goods game environments. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify how game design dimensions (such as punishment cost, player count, or MPCR) would moderate these effects. Its value for prediction is limited to supporting the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation, not for estimating efficiency outcomes in specific game designs.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent

  dimensions: chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in commons dilemmas is highly context-dependent and that institutional features such as communication, information about others' actions, and the ability to sanction (punish or reward) are critical for building trust and reciprocity, which in turn support higher cooperation. It reviews evidence that allowing punishment or graduated sanctions in lab and field settings increases cooperation and joint outcomes, but does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff. The chapter also notes that the effect of design dimensions (e.g., group size, repetition, communication, sanctioning) on cooperation is variable and context-specific.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctions in public goods or CPR-like games increases cooperation and can improve group outcomes, especially when combined with communication and information about others' actions. However, it does not provide direct, quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff effects, nor does it systematically analyze the effect of specific game design dimensions on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that punishment mechanisms (especially graduated sanctions) are associated with higher cooperation and likely higher efficiency, but the evidence is indirect and not parameterized for specific game designs.

- source: Riehl, C., & Frederickson, M. E. (2016). Cheating and punishment in cooperative animal societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in animal societies, cheating (adaptive non-cooperation that increases the cheater's fitness at the expense of others) is rare, and when it does occur, punishment is also rare or not clearly evolved as a response to cheating. Most uncooperative individuals have lower, not higher, fitness than cooperators, and variation in cooperation is often due to condition or context rather than strategic cheating. Where punishment or policing is observed (e.g., worker policing in social insects, aggression toward non-helping subordinates), it is often not clearly linked to increased group efficiency or evolved specifically to suppress cheating. The review concludes that cooperation is usually stable because it is directly or indirectly beneficial, not because punishment enforces it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in the context of game design dimensions. It suggests that, in natural animal societies, punishment is not a robust or general mechanism for increasing group efficiency or suppressing cheating, and that cooperation is often stable for other reasons. For prediction tasks, this implies that the presence of punishment mechanisms may not reliably increase efficiency unless cheating is truly adaptive and punishment is both targeted and effective. The review also highlights the importance of context, such as group size, relatedness, and the direct benefits of cooperation, which may moderate the effect of punishment.

- source: Chalub, F. A. C. C., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2006). The evolution of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that, in a two-level selection model with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, simple and unambiguous social norms emerge that maximize group efficiency (average payoff). The dominant norm (10011001) enforces cooperation by rewarding help to good individuals and punishing refusal to help good individuals or helping bad individuals. This norm allows for forgiveness and rapid recovery of reputation, but also implacable punishment for bad actions. Under this norm, cooperative strategies (CO, OR) dominate, and islands reach maximal possible fitness. The model demonstrates that the evolution of such norms is robust across a wide range of benefit parameters (b), and that a small amount of migration between islands is essential for the evolution of efficient norms and strategies. The findings support the idea that reputation-based punishment and norm enforcement can sustain high efficiency in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that reputation-based punishment (loss of reputation for non-cooperation) in repeated social dilemma games can lead to the evolution of norms that maximize group efficiency. The model is not a standard public goods game, but rather a give-and-receive game with binary actions and reputation updating. However, the core mechanism—punishment via reputation loss, indirect reciprocity, and norm enforcement—closely parallels the logic of punishment in public goods games. The findings suggest that, when such mechanisms are present, enabling punishment (via reputation or direct sanctions) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The results are robust across a range of benefit-to-cost ratios and do not depend on fine-tuning of parameters. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that introducing effective punishment or norm enforcement in repeated social dilemma environments will increase efficiency, especially when group structure and reputation are salient.

- source: Takahashi, S. (2010). Community enforcement when players observe partners' past play. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement—whereby third parties punish cheaters based on first-order information about partners' past play—can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with random matching. Under the independent and indifferent equilibrium concept, for sufficiently high discount factors, any symmetric group payoff between 0 (full defection) and 1 (full cooperation) can be achieved. The paper also shows that these results are robust to noise in actions or records, and that the equilibrium construction extends to finite populations. The threshold for sustaining cooperation does not depend on population size under this mechanism, in contrast to contagious equilibria. The paper provides explicit equilibrium constructions and analyzes the stability of cooperation under shocks and noise.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with random matching and first-order information, enabling community enforcement (punishment by third parties based on partners' past play) can increase group efficiency up to the full cooperation level, provided players are sufficiently patient. The results are robust to population size and some forms of noise. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, in analogous public-goods-game-like environments, enabling punishment mechanisms (even with limited information) can substantially increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when the discount factor is high. The paper also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the structure of the stage game (supermodular vs. submodular) and the information available to players.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Santos, F. C., & Lenaerts, T. (2013). Good Agreements Make Good Friends. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that prior commitment strategies, where players agree in advance to cooperate and specify compensation for defection, can achieve higher levels of cooperation and average payoff than traditional costly punishment, provided the arrangement cost is not too high. Sharing the cost of commitment further increases the viability and efficiency of these strategies. The efficiency gains are robust across a range of compensation and arrangement cost parameters, and commitment-based cooperation is less susceptible to antisocial punishment. The findings are derived from analytical risk-dominance conditions and evolutionary simulations, focusing on the stationary distribution of strategies and average payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms analogous to prior commitment with compensation (distinct from standard punishment) can yield higher efficiency than costly punishment, especially when the cost of arranging the agreement is low or shared. While the model is based on the one-shot PD rather than a public goods game, the efficiency results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with similar cost/impact structures. The findings imply that, for games with low punishment/arrangement costs and high punishment/compensation effectiveness, enabling commitment or cost-sharing mechanisms should increase efficiency relative to standard punishment. The paper also highlights the importance of cost-to-impact ratios and the potential for free-riding if arrangement costs are high and not shared.

- source: Chassang, S. (2010). Building Routines: Learning, Cooperation, and the Dynamics of Incomplete Relational Contracts. *American Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, in repeated relationships with incomplete information about how to cooperate, initial stages are characterized by inefficient punishment (exit) due to imperfect monitoring and the need to incentivize information revelation. As players learn and routines are established, the need for punishment disappears and efficiency increases. However, the cost of inducing further information revelation can outweigh the benefits, leading to path dependence and persistent inefficiency: partnerships may settle into suboptimal routines even when more efficient cooperation is possible. The extent of inefficiency depends on the magnitude of incentive conflicts and the cost of aligning incentives. The model provides qualitative predictions about the efficiency costs of punishment and the conditions under which punishment is required or can be avoided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment (or inefficient exit) in repeated games with incomplete information can reduce efficiency, especially in the early stages of a relationship. The efficiency cost of punishment is highest when players are still learning about how to cooperate, and disappears once routines are established and monitoring becomes perfect. The model suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment depends on the information structure and the stage of the relationship: punishment is necessary to induce information revelation but is always inefficient compared to the complete information benchmark. The findings imply that, in environments similar to public goods games with incomplete information and learning, enabling punishment may initially reduce efficiency, but its necessity diminishes as learning occurs. The model also highlights the importance of path dependence and the possibility of persistent inefficiency due to early random events. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent and may be negative or mixed, especially when learning is costly and incomplete.

- source: Hilbe, C., Traulsen, A., & Sigmund, K. (2015). Partners or rivals? Strategies for the iterated prisoner's dilemma. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper provides a comprehensive theoretical analysis of memory-one strategies in the IPD, showing which strategies can enforce mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency), competitive advantage, or linear payoff relationships (ZD strategies). It proves that partner strategies (including Grim and Tit-for-Tat under certain conditions) can enforce mutual cooperation, and that generous ZD strategies can promote cooperation by aligning payoffs. The existence and effectiveness of these strategies depend on the discount factor (patience) and the payoff matrix. The paper does not analyze the effect of enabling or disabling punishment as a separate game feature, but it shows that strategies implementing contingent punishment (e.g., Grim) are critical for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how strategy design in repeated games can enforce efficient outcomes, but it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment as a separate game feature in public goods games. Its results are most relevant for two-player repeated games (IPD) and show that strategies implementing contingent punishment (like Grim or Tit-for-Tat) are necessary for sustaining cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment in PGG-like environments, the paper suggests that the ability to implement credible punishment (via strategy) is essential for efficiency, but it does not address institutional punishment mechanisms or group settings directly. Thus, its value is mainly theoretical and mechanistic, not empirical or directly predictive for PGGs with explicit punishment options.

- source: Lipman, B. L., & Wang, R. Q. (2000). Switching costs in frequently repeated games. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that adding small switching costs to frequently repeated games can overturn standard results from finitely repeated game theory. In the Prisoners' Dilemma, switching costs can enable cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) if the incentive to punish is greater than the incentive to cheat, but can also make cooperation impossible if the reverse is true. In coordination games, switching costs can make commitment to efficient outcomes credible, sometimes leading to unique, efficient equilibria. In some games, switching costs allow for more complex credible threats, which can result in equilibrium payoffs above Stackelberg levels for some players. The overall effect of switching costs on efficiency is thus mixed and highly context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, switching costs that make threats and commitments credible) on efficiency in repeated games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure and the relative size of incentives to cooperate, cheat, or punish. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of enabling punishment (or switching costs) on efficiency cannot be determined solely from the presence of punishment, but must also account for the specific incentive structure of the game. In some cases, enabling punishment (via switching costs) increases efficiency by sustaining cooperation; in others, it has no effect or even reduces efficiency by making punishment non-credible. The results are not directly in public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for settings where punishment credibility and switching costs are relevant.

- source: Bicchieri, C., Duffy, J., & Tolle, G. (2004). Trust among strangers. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in one-shot trust games, strategies that do not trust or reciprocate dominate, resulting in low average payoffs. When the game is repeated for more rounds (increasing the shadow of the future), conditional strategies that punish defection (e.g., grim trigger, hopeful) become viable and eventually dominate the population, leading to the emergence of a norm of trust and reciprocation and higher average payoffs. The transition from non-cooperation to cooperation occurs as the number of rounds increases beyond a threshold (typically r > 2 or 3). The stable outcome is a polymorphic population of conditional strategies supporting trust/reciprocation. The results are robust to initial conditions and to the inclusion of stochasticity in the evolutionary process.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments, the length of the repeated interaction (number of rounds) is a critical moderator of the emergence of cooperation and higher efficiency. When the game is one-shot or very short, efficiency remains low due to dominance of non-cooperative strategies. As the number of rounds increases, conditional strategies that implement punishment by withholding trust/reciprocation after defection can support the emergence of trust and higher efficiency. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, repeated interaction alone can enable the emergence of efficient, cooperative outcomes if conditional strategies are available. The model does not directly manipulate or analyze explicit punishment cost or technology, but the logic of conditional strategies is analogous to endogenous punishment via future play. The results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated trust games as a function of the number of rounds and the available strategy set.

- source: Mani, A., Rahwan, I., & Pentland, A. (2013). Inducing Peer Pressure to Promote Cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing peer pressure mechanisms—where peers are incentivized to exert costly pressure on each other to reduce negative externalities—can lead to higher social surplus (efficiency) than traditional Pigouvian mechanisms, especially in large, sparse networks. Theoretical results show that peer pressure reduces equilibrium action levels (i.e., reduces over-consumption or under-contribution), but not all the way to the social optimum unless subsidized. Social mechanisms that reward peers for their neighbors' good behavior are more cost-effective than direct rewards to individuals. The model predicts that the efficiency gain from peer pressure increases as the cost of exerting pressure decreases and as social ties strengthen. An illustrative experiment on physical activity supports these predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling peer-based sanctions or rewards (peer pressure) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of peer pressure is moderate and the network is sparse. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing peer-based punishment or reward mechanisms should increase efficiency relative to control, and that the effect is stronger when the cost of peer pressure is lower and when social ties are stronger. The model is not an exact PGG, but is closely related and the efficiency results are directly relevant. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates for standard PGG parameters, but the qualitative direction is clear: peer pressure mechanisms are more efficient than no intervention, and often more efficient than centralized punishment/reward, especially at low budgets.

- source: Masuda, N. (2012). Ingroup favoritism and intergroup cooperation under indirect reciprocity based on group reputation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of cooperation in group-structured indirect reciprocity games depends critically on the social norm used for reputation assignment and the frequency of intragroup versus intergroup interactions. Full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency, with payoff b-c per round) is stable only when all observers use a 'serious' social norm (standing or judging) for all types of interactions. If observers use double standards (different norms for ingroup and outgroup interactions), then ingroup favoritism emerges, leading to lower efficiency (payoff reduced by the fraction of intergroup interactions). The key condition for any cooperation to be stable is b*r_in > c, where r_in is the probability of intragroup interaction. The model shows that changing the social norm (e.g., forcing consistency across interaction types) can shift the equilibrium from inefficient ingroup favoritism to efficient full cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but reputation loss/gain serves a similar function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with group structure. It shows that the efficiency outcome (full cooperation, partial, or ingroup favoritism) is determined by the social norm for reputation assignment and the structure of interactions (r_in). In the absence of explicit punishment or reward, but with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, efficiency is maximized only if the social norm is consistent and 'serious' (standing/judging) across all interaction types. If the game design allows or encourages double standards (different norms for ingroup/outgroup), expect lower efficiency due to ingroup favoritism. The model's efficiency results are directly parameterized by the benefit-to-cost ratio and the frequency of intragroup interaction, which can be mapped to MPCR and group structure in PGG-like games. However, the absence of explicit punishment/reward means the results are most applicable to games where reputation is the main enforcement mechanism.

- source: KRAINES, D., & KRAINES, V. (1993). LEARNING TO COOPERATE WITH PAVLOV - AN ADAPTIVE STRATEGY FOR THE ITERATED PRISONERS-DILEMMA WITH NOISE. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that Pavlovian strategies, which adapt their probability of cooperation based on positive and negative payoffs (interpreted as rewards and punishments), can achieve high average payoffs and are robust to noise in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma. Pavlov can exploit overly cooperative opponents (like All-C), is not easily exploited except by always-defect strategies, and can recover mutual cooperation after errors more quickly than Tit-for-Tat (TFT). The optimal learning rate (parameter n) balances exploitability and speed of learning, with n=3 or 4 performing best in simulations. The paper provides detailed payoff tables and Markov chain analyses for various strategy matchups, but does not study group interactions, explicit peer punishment, or public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in public goods games, as it does not study group games, peer punishment, or any of the relevant game design dimensions. However, it is informative about how strategies that adapt to positive and negative payoffs (interpreted as endogenous rewards and punishments) can lead to high efficiency and mutual cooperation in repeated dyadic dilemmas. The findings suggest that adaptive learning can be robust to noise and can outperform simple reciprocity (TFT) in some environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address the effect of enabling punishment mechanisms or any of the design dimensions in public goods games.

- source: Zimmermann, M. G., & Eguíluz, V. M. (2005). Cooperation, social networks, and the emergence of leadership in a prisoner's dilemma with adaptive local interactions - art. no. 056118. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that network adaptation (agents breaking unsatisfactory links and seeking new partners) enables the emergence of highly cooperative, high-efficiency steady states in a networked Prisoner's Dilemma. In these states, most agents cooperate, and the group achieves high average payoffs, even though defectors individually do better than cooperators. The system is robust to a range of parameters, but can be destabilized by noise or by the loss of key cooperative leaders, leading to cascades of defection and efficiency collapse. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms, but the ability to break links with defectors functions as a form of social sanction/ostracism. The results show that such adaptive partner selection can substitute for explicit punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that adaptive partner selection (ostracism, link breaking) in networked social dilemmas can dramatically increase group efficiency, even in the absence of explicit costly punishment or reward. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that enabling mechanisms for social exclusion or network adaptation (even if not labeled as 'punishment') can have similar or greater positive effects on efficiency as explicit punishment in public goods games. However, the model is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and the 'punishment' is indirect (ostracism, not costly reduction of others' payoffs). The results are most relevant for predicting the effects of partner choice, ostracism, or network adaptation, and less directly for classic costly punishment. The paper is highly informative for adjacent mechanisms but should be used with caution for direct predictions about explicit punishment in PGGs.

- source: Bowles, S., & Gintis, H. (2004). Persistent parochialism: trust and exclusion in ethnic networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that parochial networks can achieve higher efficiency (average payoff) than anonymous markets in social dilemma environments, due to better information and informal enforcement (via monitoring and exclusion). However, the relationship between parochialism and efficiency is non-linear: too little parochialism leads to poor information and low trust, while too much parochialism reduces trading opportunities and group size, also lowering efficiency. The model includes a monitoring/inspection strategy (analogous to punishment), which, when information is sufficiently accurate, supports a stable trust equilibrium with higher payoffs. The optimal efficiency is achieved at an intermediate level of parochialism, balancing enforcement and trading opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (monitoring, exclusion) on efficiency in social dilemma games depends on the information structure and group composition. Efficiency is maximized at an intermediate level of parochialism, where information is good enough to support trust and informal enforcement, but group size and trading opportunities are not too restricted. The model suggests that enabling punishment/monitoring can increase efficiency relative to a control (anonymous market) when information is sufficiently accurate and group size is within a viable range. However, excessive exclusion (high parochialism) can reduce efficiency by limiting opportunities. The findings are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment is implemented via monitoring and exclusion, rather than costly direct punishment as in standard PGGs.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2017). The evolution of conditional moral assessment in indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the 'Staying' norm, which prescribes abstaining from moral assessment when a donor interacts with a recipient of bad reputation, is more effective at establishing and maintaining cooperation than the four most studied social norms in indirect reciprocity. Under Staying, discriminators (conditional cooperators) are more likely to invade populations of defectors, and the evolutionary dynamics favor higher average payoffs and more efficient group outcomes. The model demonstrates that the range of initial conditions leading to efficient, cooperative equilibria is wider under Staying, and that the strict application of moral judgment (as in other norms) is less effective for promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity games (a close relative of public goods games), the design of the assessment/punishment norm can have a large effect on group efficiency. Specifically, conditional assessment (Staying) leads to higher efficiency than unconditional assessment (Scoring, etc.), especially when assessment errors are low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the details of the punishment/assessment mechanism (e.g., whether punishment is conditional or unconditional, and how errors are handled) are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. The findings support the idea that more nuanced, context-sensitive punishment/assessment rules can yield higher efficiency than strict, always-on punishment rules.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2002). From parasitism to mutualism: partner control in asymmetric interactions. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The model demonstrates that in asymmetric interactions, the ability of the exploited party to terminate the interaction acts as a deterrent to exploitation, increasing the efficiency (joint payoff) of the system. As the exploiter's power to prolong the interaction increases, exploitation rises, the victim's payoff falls (eventually becoming negative), and the system shifts from mutualism to parasitism. The temptation to cheat (benefit/cost ratio) and the rate at which mutualistic benefits diminish also modulate these outcomes. The model is supported qualitatively by empirical data from cleaner-fish systems, where larger clients (with more to gain from cleaning) experience less exploitation and longer interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing the exploited party to control the duration of interaction (analogous to exit, partner choice, or punishment in PGG-like games) can increase efficiency by deterring exploitation. While not a PGG or direct punishment experiment, the model is relevant for predicting that increasing the ability of group members to sanction or exit (or otherwise control exploiters) will generally increase group efficiency, especially when the temptation to cheat is not overwhelming. The model also suggests that efficiency gains from punishment or partner control are greatest when the benefits of cooperation are high and the costs of cheating are high relative to the exploiter's gain. However, the model does not address repeated group interactions, explicit punishment costs, or the full range of PGG design dimensions, so its predictive value is indirect.

- source: Bird, R. B., & Power, E. A. (2015). Prosocial signaling and cooperation among Martu hunters. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In populations of SLA agents without memory, those with low penalty sensitivity dominate, leading to high defection rates and low average payoffs. (2) SLA strategies are not stable against invasion by TFT or ALLD; TFT can dominate and lead to higher population payoffs. (3) Adding memory to SLA agents enables them to learn to cooperate with TFT, increasing cooperation and average payoffs, but this effect is limited to interactions with TFT; among themselves, SLA agents still defect. (4) The penalty parameter (analogous to punishment sensitivity) is a key moderator: higher penalty sensitivity leads to more cooperation and higher payoffs, but is outcompeted by less sensitive types in evolutionary dynamics. (5) The model predicts that, absent memory or external cooperative strategies, populations of simple learning agents will evolve toward defection and low efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments without explicit punishment mechanisms, agents with low sensitivity to penalties (i.e., low punishment cost or low punishment effectiveness) evolve toward defection and low efficiency. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, the presence and effectiveness of punishment (or penalty sensitivity) are critical for sustaining cooperation and high efficiency. However, the model does not include explicit peer punishment as a game design feature, so direct quantitative predictions for the effect of enabling punishment in public goods games are limited. The results do support the qualitative prediction that increasing the effectiveness or salience of punishment (or penalty) responses can increase efficiency, but such strategies may be evolutionarily unstable without additional mechanisms (e.g., memory, reputation, or external enforcement).

- source: MacLeod, W. B. (2007). Reputations, relationships, and contract enforcement. *Journal of Economic Literature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that informal enforcement mechanisms—such as reputational punishment for breach—can support efficient outcomes in repeated interactions, especially when formal legal enforcement is costly or unavailable. The efficiency of punishment depends on contract form: warranty and bonus contracts can achieve first-best efficiency under certain conditions, while simple fixed-price contracts relying on reputation alone are often inefficient due to risk of reputation loss even when performance is adequate. The paper predicts that warranty contracts are more efficient for 'normal' goods (where bad outcomes are rare), while bonus contracts are more efficient for 'innovative' goods (where good outcomes are rare). The efficiency of punishment/reward mechanisms is moderated by the observability of breach, the frequency of trade, the quality of law, and the allocation of reputational capital. The paper also discusses how formal and informal enforcement can be substitutes or complements, and how the choice of contract form and enforcement mechanism affects overall surplus and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment and reward mechanisms in repeated games and contract-like environments. It suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the contract form (e.g., whether punishment is triggered by observable breach, whether rewards are available), the type of good or task (frequency and likelihood of good/bad outcomes), and the quality of formal enforcement. For public-goods-game-like environments, the results imply that informal punishment (reputation loss) can improve efficiency, but only under certain conditions—especially when breach is easily observed and when contract terms (such as warranties or bonuses) are used to align incentives. The paper also highlights that the efficiency gains from punishment may be limited or negative if punishment is triggered by events outside the agent's control, or if the risk of reputation loss is high even for compliant agents. Thus, for the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms and the information structure are key moderators of efficiency effects.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2006). Unifying the theories of inclusive fitness and reciprocal altruism. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that the evolution of cooperation (altruism) in both kin and non-kin contexts can be predicted by a generalized Hamilton's rule that incorporates the covariance between genotypes and the cooperative behaviors of others, as well as nonadditive (synergistic) effects. In iterated prisoner's dilemma models, conditional strategies like tit-for-tat can increase in frequency when the benefit received from others' cooperation exceeds the cost, as predicted by Queller's rule. The same logic applies to mutualistic interactions between species. The paper shows that synergy (nonadditivity) and conditional behavior can both facilitate the evolution of cooperation, even in the absence of kinship or repeated interactions. Punishment is discussed as a possible mechanism for positive assortment but is not modeled. The findings are theoretical and do not provide empirical estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical background for understanding how mechanisms like punishment, synergy, and conditional behavior can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency, but it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It suggests that any mechanism (including punishment) that increases positive assortment or synergy can increase the likelihood that cooperation evolves, but does not specify the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes in experimental settings. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is useful for mechanistic context but not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Nowak, M. A. (2006). Five rules for the evolution of cooperation. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr
  findings: The paper derives and summarizes simple mathematical rules for when cooperation can evolve and be maintained in populations, based on five mechanisms: kin selection, direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, network reciprocity, and group selection. For each, a threshold condition is given (e.g., benefit-to-cost ratio exceeding a critical value) that determines whether cooperation can increase average fitness or group payoff. The paper notes that, without such mechanisms, natural selection favors defection and reduces average fitness. Punishment is discussed as a factor that can enhance cooperation within these frameworks, but is not itself a primary mechanism for the evolution of cooperation. The paper does not provide direct predictions or data on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical background on the conditions under which cooperation can increase group efficiency or average payoff, but does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment in public goods games or the impact of specific game design dimensions. It is useful for understanding the broader theoretical context and for mapping how punishment may interact with other mechanisms (e.g., indirect reciprocity, group selection), but does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or specific design features. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as high-level theoretical context rather than as a source of empirical effect sizes or moderator effects.

- source: Lotem, A., Fishman, M. A., & Stone, L. (2003). From reciprocity to unconditional altruism through signalling benefits. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated cooperation games with individual variation in quality, the addition of signalling benefits to helping can lead to stable unconditional altruism among high-quality individuals. This occurs when the signalling benefit (S) is greater than the cost of helping (C) for high-quality individuals but not for low-quality individuals. Under these conditions, high-quality individuals cooperate unconditionally, while low-quality individuals defect or play tit-for-tat. The result is a higher average payoff (efficiency) for high-quality cooperators, and the overall level of cooperation and efficiency in the population is increased compared to models without signalling benefits. The model also shows that if signalling benefits become too cheap (affordable to all), the correlation between quality and cooperation disappears, undermining the signalling function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that adding signalling benefits to cooperation games can increase efficiency, especially among high-quality individuals. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in environments where helping can serve as a costly signal (and where the signalling benefit exceeds the cost for some but not all), enabling such signalling will increase the efficiency of cooperation relative to control. The model is adjacent to public goods games and punishment, but the payoff logic and ESS analysis are directly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes when game design allows for signalling or reputation effects. However, the model does not directly manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters, so its predictive value is strongest for settings where signalling is a plausible mechanism.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Santos, F. C., & Chalub, F. A. C. C. (2006). Stern-judging: A simple, successful norm which promotes cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that, in a multilevel evolutionary model of indirect reciprocity, the stern-judging norm—where helping good individuals or refusing help to bad individuals is rewarded, and the opposite is punished—emerges as the dominant and most robust social norm. This norm leads to maximized average payoffs for groups, indicating high efficiency in sustaining cooperation. The result is robust to different forms of group selection and inter-tribe conflict. The model suggests that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms are sufficient to promote and maintain high levels of cooperation and group welfare, even in the presence of errors and migration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms (like stern-judging) can robustly increase group efficiency and cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is adjacent: it involves costly helping, reputation, and punishment via social norms. The findings suggest that, in environments where indirect reciprocity and reputation are salient, enabling punishment (in the form of social norm enforcement) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines. However, the model is abstract and does not manipulate most game design dimensions directly, so its predictive value is strongest for settings with similar indirect reciprocity structure and less for standard PGGs.

- source: Rand, D. G., Tarnita, C. E., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Evolution of fairness in the one-shot anonymous Ultimatum Game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Theoretical and simulation results show that, in finite populations with stochastic evolutionary dynamics, fairness (both higher offers and nonzero rejection thresholds) can be favored by natural selection in the Ultimatum Game, even without reputation or repeated interaction. The favored strategies depend on mutation rate and selection strength. The behavioral experiment supports the prediction that greater uncertainty about others' success and less behavioral consistency are associated with higher offers and/or demands. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total welfare, or surplus, and the Ultimatum Game structure is only adjacent to public goods games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the evolution of costly punishment and fairness in strategic interactions, but its findings are not directly transferable to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The Ultimatum Game is a two-player bargaining game with a binary accept/reject punishment mechanism, not a multi-player public goods game with explicit punishment options. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer and demand), not group efficiency. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that costly punishment (rejection) can be favored and lead to fairer outcomes, it does not provide quantitative evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper for mechanism insight, not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2015). Apology and forgiveness evolve to resolve failures in cooperative agreements. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated social dilemmas modeled as noisy iterated Prisoner's Dilemma, the ability to make commitments and to retaliate (revenge) after commitment breakdown increases the prevalence of cooperative strategies. When apology and forgiveness are added, cooperation is further boosted, but only if apology is sufficiently costly (sincere). If apology is too cheap, fake committers exploit the system, reducing cooperation. The model shows that the optimal apology cost is above the cost of cooperation but below the penalty for breaking commitment. The results are robust to noise and repeated interaction length. While the model is not a public goods game, it is adjacent, and the mechanisms studied (commitment, retaliation, apology, forgiveness) are relevant to understanding how punishment-like mechanisms affect cooperation and group payoff in repeated social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in repeated, noisy social dilemmas, mechanisms analogous to punishment (revenge/retaliation) and costly apology/forgiveness can increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoff/efficiency, especially when the cost and effectiveness of these mechanisms are well-calibrated. The findings suggest that enabling punishment-like responses after norm violations (commitment breakdown) and allowing for costly apology/forgiveness can improve efficiency, but only if the costs are set appropriately. The results are most transferable to repeated games with commitment and noise, and less so to one-shot or standard PGGs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency, but supports the idea that punishment and compensation mechanisms can be beneficial for group outcomes under certain conditions.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Reputation Effects in Public and Private Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperative strategies in indirect reciprocity depends critically on the observability of private actions (q), the frequency of public interactions (p), and the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c). Honest cooperation (leading to full group efficiency) is only stable when private actions are sufficiently observable and b/c is high. When private actions are rarely observed or b/c is low, only partial cooperation or defection is stable, resulting in lower group efficiency. The model provides explicit analytical thresholds for these transitions. The presence of public observation increases the incentive for cooperation, but if private actions are unobserved, individuals may defect in private, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how observability (analogous to information feedback or monitoring in public goods games) and the benefit-to-cost ratio moderate the effect of reputation-based incentives on group efficiency. While not a direct PGG or punishment study, its results suggest that increasing the probability that actions are observed (or reputationally consequential) increases the likelihood of stable, high-efficiency cooperation. In prediction tasks, similar mechanisms (e.g., transparency, monitoring, or reputation updating) should be expected to increase the efficiency gains from reputation or sanctioning systems, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high. However, if observability is low, the efficiency impact of such systems will be limited.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2004). The logic of reprobation: assessment and action rules for indirect reciprocation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that indirect reciprocity models with assessment rules that distinguish justified from unjustified defections (STANDING) are more effective at sustaining cooperation and suppressing defectors than simple scoring rules, especially in the presence of errors. However, even simple scoring can support cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and players have enough interactions. The model demonstrates that the structure of the population, the number of observed interactions, and the error rates in action and perception all influence the stability of cooperation. Analytical results show that, under certain assumptions about the distribution of the number of rounds, stable mixtures of discriminating and non-discriminating altruists can exist, leading to high average payoffs. The findings suggest that costly punishment (refusing to help) can sustain cooperation, but is less robust than non-costly forms (STANDING).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus efficiency) depends on whether punishment is costly, the benefit-to-cost ratio, error rates, and the number of interactions. While the model is not a standard PGG, the parallels are strong: refusing to help is analogous to punishing defectors, and the group payoff is analogous to efficiency. The results imply that costly punishment can increase efficiency, but is less robust than non-costly punishment, and that the details of the assessment and action rules, as well as the game structure (e.g., number of rounds, observation probability), are important moderators. For prediction, the paper supports the view that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and errors are not too frequent, but the effect is sensitive to the costliness and implementation of punishment.
