- source: Aktipis, C. A. (2004). Know when to walk away: contingent movement and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that a simple contingent movement rule ('Walk Away') enables cooperation to evolve and persist in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, outperforming more cognitively complex strategies like TFT and PAVLOV under many conditions. Walk Away is robust to invasion, can invade populations of defectors at lower initial frequencies than other strategies, and is most successful when movement costs, error rates, and agent density are low. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and outcomes are measured by the evolutionary success of strategies (frequency in the population), not by direct group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that contingent movement (exit) can substitute for more complex memory-based or punishment-based strategies in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment, but shows that contingent movement (exit after defection) can promote cooperation and evolutionary success in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing agents to avoid defectors (analogous to exit or partner choice) can be highly effective, even without explicit punishment or memory. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, and does not manipulate punishment cost or technology, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding the role of partner choice, mobility, and spatial structure as alternatives or complements to punishment.

- source: Wardil, L., & da Silva, J. K. L. (2009). Adoption of simultaneous different strategies against different opponents enhances cooperation. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to adopt different strategies against different opponents, combined with a 'replace the worst confrontation' update rule (model A), leads to the dominance or persistence of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on both square lattices and rings. This mechanism acts as a form of punishment by neutralizing exploiters, but does not impose a cost on the punisher. The fraction of cooperation remains high for a wide range of defection temptations and is robust to some misjudgment. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its relevance is limited to the insight that certain update rules that allow targeted responses to defectors can sustain high cooperation rates, even without explicit costly punishment. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG), and the outcome is cooperation rate (not efficiency or payoff), its transfer value to the prediction task is limited and indirect. The paper does not report or analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so it cannot be used to calibrate efficiency predictions for PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Li, X. P., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2019). Reputation-based adaptive adjustment of link weight among individuals promotes the cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to adaptively increase or decrease based on reputation (interpreted as a form of social reward/punishment) leads to a substantial increase in the frequency of cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games. The effect is stronger as the range of possible link weights (controlled by ε) increases, which increases heterogeneity and supports the formation of cooperative clusters. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency outcomes is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that dynamic, reputation-based social reward and punishment mechanisms (via link weight adjustment) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a simulation of spatial PDG/SDG rather than a public goods game, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited and should be considered only as suggestive, not directly predictive.

- source: Fu, F., Chen, X. J., Liu, L. G., & Wang, L. (2007). Promotion of cooperation induced by the interplay between structure and game dynamics. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to adapt their social ties by breaking links with the most frequent defectors (after a multi-round PDG) and rewiring to others can promote the emergence and dominance of cooperation in the population. The interplay between network structure adaptation and strategy evolution is key: there is a critical rate of network adaptation above which cooperators can dominate. The model explores how parameters such as temptation to defect, number of rounds, average connectivity, and selection intensity affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and network properties, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its model is adjacent: it studies a networked PDG with social tie breaking as a form of sanction, but does not include explicit costly punishment or measure efficiency/welfare. The findings suggest that adaptive social structure (link breaking as a response to defection) can promote cooperation, but the results are not directly transferable to standard PGGs with explicit punishment and efficiency outcomes. Use this paper as weak, indirect support for the idea that social sanctions (even non-costly ones) can promote cooperation, but do not use it for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Huang, K. K., Zheng, X. P., Li, Z. J., & Yang, Y. Q. (2015). Understanding Cooperative Behavior Based on the Coevolution of Game Strategy and Link Weight. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to coevolve with strategies in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can significantly enhance the fraction of cooperators, especially at intermediate values of link weight amplitude. The mechanism works by reinforcing links to successful neighbors and weakening links to less successful ones, which leads to the formation and expansion of cooperator clusters. The effect is non-monotonic: too little or too much link weight adaptation is less effective than an intermediate level. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods or punishment games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG) and studies the coevolution of strategies and link weights (not explicit punishment or sanctions). The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings may be contextually informative for understanding how dynamic network structures or adaptive relationships can promote cooperation, which could be relevant for models where network adaptation or endogenous partner selection is a feature. For the core prediction task, this paper offers only adjacent, indirect support.

- source: BIEL, A., & GARLING, T. (1995). THE ROLE OF UNCERTAINTY IN RESOURCE DILEMMAS. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The main finding is that a reputation mechanism that rewards/punishes based on the number of consecutive rounds a player has cooperated or defected can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. Higher reputation factors and higher upper bounds on consistent strategy times both further promote cooperation. The mechanism is more effective and leads to faster stabilization of cooperation than traditional reputation mechanisms. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment/reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG environments, and that the strength and structure of the reputation mechanism matter. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its relevance is further limited by the use of a spatial PDG rather than a PGG or direct payoff-based analysis. It may be useful for mechanism design intuition, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gou, Z. Q., & Li, Y. (2023). Prisoner's dilemma game model Based on historical strategy information. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that increasing the length of historical memory (M) and the weight of memory information (β) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to higher cooperation rates and more stable cooperative clusters. The effect saturates at high memory lengths, and excessive memory does not further promote cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rate and strategy stability, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It models a spatial prisoner's dilemma with memory-based strategy updating, focusing on how memory and its weight affect cooperation rates. There is no manipulation or analysis of punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Thus, its relevance to the downstream prediction task is limited to providing indirect, adjacent evidence that memory and historical information can promote cooperation, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Danku, Z., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Knowing the past improves cooperation in the future. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when agents in spatial social dilemma games (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, stag-hunt) update their strategies based on a combination of current and past payoffs, cooperation is strongly promoted compared to the standard model where only current payoffs matter. This effect is robust to the details of how the past is incorporated (weighted average or single past payoff with decaying probability). The mechanism works by slowing down the invasion of defectors into cooperative clusters, allowing cooperation to persist and spread. The effect is most pronounced when the memory window is sufficiently long or the selected past payoff is not too far in the past. The results are shown for the stationary fraction of cooperators, not for efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or closely related environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are about how memory of past payoffs (a strategy-neutral mechanism) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. While the results may be conceptually relevant for understanding how information and history affect cooperation, they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, except perhaps as very weak, indirect context for the role of history or memory in cooperation.

- source: Page, K. M., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2000). The spatial ultimatum game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that spatial structure in the ultimatum game leads to the evolution of fairer offers and higher acceptance thresholds compared to non-spatial (random encounter) settings. In well-mixed populations, average offers and acceptance levels are low, approaching zero as mutation error decreases. In spatially structured populations (1D and 2D lattices), average offers and acceptance levels are much higher, approaching a fair split in some cases. The paper interprets rejection of low offers as a punishment mechanism that, when combined with spatial structure, supports the evolution of fairness. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that spatial structure and local competition can support the evolution of fairer, more cooperative behavior in bargaining games where punishment is endogenous (via rejection). However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of spatial structure and local interactions for the evolution of fairness and the effectiveness of endogenous punishment mechanisms.

- source: Sinatra, R., Iranzo, J., Gómez-Gardeñes, J., Floría, L. M., Latora, V., & Moreno, Y. (2009). The Ultimatum Game in complex networks. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that network topology and the type of evolutionary update rule strongly influence the distribution of offers and acceptance thresholds in the Ultimatum Game. Under natural selection, strategies converge toward fair offers (around 50%) in both homogeneous and heterogeneous networks, but scale-free networks allow for a broader range of surviving strategies due to the influence of hubs. Under the social penalty rule, which penalizes the lowest-payoff player and its neighbors, scale-free networks promote polarization: hubs can afford to be highly generous, while low-degree nodes may be more selfish. When players can independently choose offers and thresholds, the population tends to self-organize into pragmatic (role-ignoring) strategies, especially under social penalty. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the implications for efficiency are not directly addressed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies the Ultimatum Game and focuses on the evolution of behavioral strategies rather than payoff or efficiency outcomes. The 'social penalty' mechanism is a form of network-level selection rather than direct costly punishment. While the findings may be informative about how network structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation and fairness-related behaviors, they do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. Therefore, this paper should be considered only as weak, adjacent evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Wang, Q., He, N. R., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Replicator dynamics for public goods game with resource allocation in large populations. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the coevolutionary dynamics of cooperation and environmental state are more diverse than in well-mixed populations. Depending on payoff parameters and the number of neighbors, the system can exhibit persistent oscillations, bistability (with both depleted and replete environmental states as attractors), coexistence of oscillation and dominance, or stable coexistence of cooperation and defection. The number of neighbors (degree) can either expand or shrink the basin of attraction for favorable environmental states, depending on the ratio of enhancement/degradation rates or payoff parameter ratios. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or model costly punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The model is adjacent to PGGs in that it studies cooperation and defection in a structured population with environmental feedback, but the 'punishment' parameter is the matrix payoff for mutual defection, not a sanctioning institution. The findings may inform how structural parameters (e.g., number of neighbors) and payoff ratios affect the maintenance of cooperation and environmental quality, but do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency.

- source: Powers, S. T., van Schaik, C. P., & Lehmann, L. (2016). How institutions shaped the last major evolutionary transition to large-scale human societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that the ability to create and enforce institutional rules, including sanctioning mechanisms, is a key driver of large-scale human cooperation and division of labor. Institutions can transform the default equilibrium of social dilemmas (such as public goods games) to more cooperative, higher-payoff outcomes by enabling coordination, information sharing, and self-enforcing sanctioning. Theoretical arguments and historical/anthropological examples are used to support the claim that institutions can increase group efficiency and welfare, but the paper does not provide new empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms within an institutional framework can increase efficiency and group payoffs in public-goods-like environments, especially as group size increases. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes as a function of game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its value for prediction is in supporting the plausibility and mechanism of positive effects of institutions (including punishment) on efficiency, but it does not inform the magnitude or specific moderators of these effects.

- source: Gardner, A., West, S. A., & Barton, N. H. (2007). The relation between multilocus population genetics and social evolution theory. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that multilocus population genetics provides a general framework for modeling the evolution of social behaviors, including cooperation and punishment. In a simple two-locus model (one for cooperation, one for punishment), it finds that cooperation and punishment cannot invade unless cooperation is already favored by relatedness (i.e., punishment does not expand the parameter space for cooperation). Moreover, increased relatedness directly selects against punishment, as punishment is mutually costly. The only benefit to punishment comes from its association with cooperation, but this is insufficient to overcome the direct costs. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on evolutionary stability and gene frequency change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the evolutionary logic of cooperation and punishment, and suggest that costly punishment may not increase cooperation or group benefit except under restrictive conditions. However, because it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it should not be used as a primary source for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context, especially regarding the evolutionary logic of punishment and cooperation.

- source: Falk, A., & Fischbacher, U. (2006). A theory of reciprocity. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper develops a psychological game-theoretic model of reciprocity, where individuals' utility depends on both material payoffs and the perceived kindness or unkindness of others' actions, which in turn depends on both outcomes and intentions. The model explains why people sometimes punish defectors or reward cooperators, even at a cost to themselves, and why the same material outcome can elicit different responses depending on the perceived intention behind it. The theory is shown to be consistent with a range of experimental findings, such as higher rejection rates for unfair offers in the ultimatum game, positive wage-effort relationships in gift-exchange, and conditional cooperation in public goods games. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical framework for understanding when and why individuals will punish or reward others in social dilemmas, including public goods games. It highlights the importance of both outcomes and intentions in driving reciprocal behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper offers conceptual guidance on the psychological mechanisms underlying punishment, but does not supply direct evidence or parameter values for efficiency outcomes. Its value is mainly in informing the types of behavioral responses to expect under different game structures, especially regarding the role of intentions and available alternatives.

- source: Giardini, F., & Conte, R. (2012). Gossip for social control in natural and artificial societies. *Simulation-transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that gossip, understood as the spread of reputation (meta-evaluations with hidden sources), enables more efficient social control by reducing the costs and risks (especially retaliation) associated with punishing or excluding cheaters. Ethnographic evidence shows that gossip is structured to avoid direct responsibility and retaliation, while agent-based simulations demonstrate that reputation-based information exchange can maintain higher product quality and more robust partner selection even with many cheaters. However, these findings are in the context of market-like artificial societies and do not directly address efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that decentralized, reputation-based information exchange (gossip) can improve the effectiveness and reduce the costs of social control mechanisms, such as punishment or exclusion, in environments where cheating is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The agent-based simulation results suggest that reputation mechanisms can sustain higher system performance (e.g., product quality) under high cheating rates, but these are not directly translatable to PGG efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform mechanistic reasoning about the role of information and retaliation risk in punishment systems, but does not provide parameter-level evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Taylor, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Transforming the dilemma. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper provides a unified framework for understanding how different mechanisms (direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, kin selection, group selection, network reciprocity) transform the payoff structure of the Prisoner's Dilemma to allow for the evolution of cooperation. For each mechanism, it derives the threshold conditions under which cooperation can be stable or invade. The analysis is mathematical and does not include empirical or simulation results. Punishment is discussed only as an extension, not as a primary focus, and is said to be subsumed under direct or indirect reciprocity. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical synthesis of mechanisms for cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma, with only a brief, conceptual discussion of punishment. The paper does not analyze or model the effect of punishment on group payoff, efficiency, or related outcomes, and does not provide parameterized results for game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task. Its main value is in clarifying that punishment is not a separate mechanism but an extension of reciprocity, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are not directly addressed.

- source: West, S. A., Griffin, A. S., & Gardner, A. (2007). Social semantics: altruism, cooperation, mutualism, strong reciprocity and group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that much confusion in the literature on cooperation, altruism, and punishment arises from inconsistent or ambiguous use of key terms. It clarifies that in many models (including public goods games), behaviors labeled as 'altruistic' or 'strong reciprocity' may actually be mutually beneficial, providing direct fitness benefits to the actor as well as to the group. Punishment, often called 'altruistic punishment,' can also be selfish or spiteful, depending on the context. The paper argues that clear definitions and careful distinction between proximate and ultimate explanations are essential for progress in the field. It also notes that the group selection and kin selection frameworks are mathematically equivalent in most cases relevant to cooperation and public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers important theoretical context: it cautions that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on the underlying fitness consequences and the structure of the game. The paper suggests that punishment can increase cooperation and group benefit if it provides a direct or indirect fitness advantage, but the terminology used in the literature may obscure whether the effect is truly altruistic or mutually beneficial. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the need for precise definitions of efficiency and careful mapping of model assumptions to experimental designs. It also highlights that the effect of punishment may depend on whether it is costly, whether it increases group survival or productivity, and how benefits are distributed.

- source: Jagers, S. C., Harring, N., Löfgren, Sjöstedt, M., Alpizar, F., Brülde, B., Langlet, D., Nilsson, A., Almroth, B. C., Dupont, S., & Steffen, W. (2020). On the preconditions for large-scale collective action. *Ambio*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

- source: MOLM, L. D. (1994). DEPENDENCE AND RISK - TRANSFORMING AND STRUCTURE OF SOCIAL-EXCHANGE. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that the risk inherent in social exchange relations (mutual dependence) can be reduced by structuring transactions as negotiated (joint decision, binding agreements) or as serially dependent (allowing for contingent action and trust development). Negotiated transactions most directly transform dependence into interdependence, reducing risk and making outcomes more predictable. Serially dependent transactions (such as repeated reciprocal exchanges) also reduce risk, but less so, and rely more on emergent trust and norms. Generalized exchange (indirect reciprocity) is riskier and less amenable to these risk-reducing mechanisms. The paper suggests that these structural features influence the likelihood of stable cooperation, trust, and solidarity, but does not provide empirical evidence or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how structural features of social exchange (e.g., repeated interaction, negotiation, direct vs. generalized exchange) may moderate the risk of defection and the emergence of cooperation. While it does not address public goods games or punishment directly, it suggests that mechanisms which increase interdependence (such as repeated play or enforceable agreements) should improve group outcomes and stability. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game designs with more interdependence, repeated interaction, or enforceable agreements are likely to see higher efficiency or more stable cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence. The relevance to punishment is indirect: punishment is mentioned as a possible influence tactic in negotiation, but not as a central mechanism.

- source: Capraro, V., & Perc, M. (2021). Mathematical foundations of moral preferences. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that a wide range of unselfish behaviors in one-shot, anonymous economic games are better explained by a 'moral preference hypothesis'—that is, people have preferences for following their own personal norms about what is right or wrong, beyond the monetary consequences of their actions. This framework organizes and explains empirical deviations from standard social preference models in games such as the dictator game, ultimatum game (altruistic punishment), Prisoner's Dilemma (cooperation), trust game (trustworthiness), sender-receiver game (truth-telling), and trade-off games (equality vs. efficiency). The review also discusses the practical implications of targeting personal norms (rather than social or injunctive norms) in interventions to promote pro-sociality, and proposes a formal utility function incorporating personal norms. However, the paper does not provide new data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions in multi-player, repeated, or public goods games. Its main contribution is theoretical: it suggests that personal moral norms are a key driver of unselfish behavior (including punishment) in one-shot, anonymous games, and that interventions targeting personal norms may be more effective than those targeting social norms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only indirect, mechanistic context about why people might punish or cooperate, but not quantitative or design-specific evidence about efficiency effects.

  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation at large scales is best explained by cultural group selection, where rapid cultural adaptation leads to persistent differences between groups. These differences, when linked to group-beneficial norms (such as those enforced by punishment and reward systems), can spread through group competition, imitation of successful groups, and selective migration. The authors posit that moral systems enforced by sanctions and rewards increase group welfare and the prevalence of prosocial motives. However, the paper is theoretical and does not present empirical data or experimental results on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for why punishment and reward systems may support the evolution and maintenance of cooperation and group-beneficial norms, potentially increasing group welfare. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Its value for prediction is mainly in supporting the plausibility of positive effects of sanctions on group-level outcomes, but it does not inform how specific game design dimensions or control efficiency moderate these effects.

  findings: The paper finds that reciprocity and spite are rare or absent in non-human animals due to both ultimate (evolutionary) and proximate (mechanistic) constraints. In contrast, humans evolved the necessary cognitive integration and social structures (such as large groups of unrelated individuals) that favor the emergence of reciprocity and punishment. Punishment is argued to be essential for maintaining cooperation and enforcing social norms in humans, and psychological spite is seen as a byproduct of selection for punishment. The paper reviews animal experiments (e.g., with jays, tamarins, chimpanzees) and human studies (including children), showing that only humans reliably exhibit the full suite of behaviors required for sustained reciprocity and norm-enforcing punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for why punishment and reciprocity are effective in humans but not in most non-human animals. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding the evolutionary and cognitive prerequisites for punishment and cooperation, but does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in specific game designs. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of group size, repeated interactions, norm enforcement, and cognitive integration for the emergence of punishment and cooperation.

  findings: The paper argues that Ostrom's core design principles, including monitoring and graduated sanctions (punishment), are critical for the efficacy of groups managing common-pool resources and can be generalized to other group contexts such as education and neighborhoods. It provides theoretical justification for why these principles suppress within-group conflict and promote cooperation, referencing evolutionary and game theory. The paper discusses case studies (drawn from other literature) where the implementation of these principles led to improved group functioning, but does not report new experimental or quantitative results on efficiency or payoffs. The main finding is that the presence of monitoring and sanctioning mechanisms is associated with more effective group outcomes, but this is discussed in terms of group efficacy and cooperation rather than explicit efficiency or payoff metrics.

Its value for prediction is mainly in reinforcing the importance of including monitoring and sanctioning mechanisms in models of group cooperation, and in highlighting that the effectiveness of such mechanisms depends on their implementation and the broader institutional context. For direct prediction of efficiency outcomes, empirical studies with quantitative results are needed.

It draws on evolutionary game theory and references experimental evidence (e.g., Fehr and Gächter 2002) showing that punishment can enhance the evolution of beneficial social norms. - source: Clavien, C., & Chapuisat, M. (2013). Altruism across disciplines: one word, multiple meanings. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the term 'altruism' is used inconsistently across disciplines, leading to confusion and overstated claims, especially when transferring findings between fields like experimental economics and evolutionary biology. It argues for a clear taxonomy distinguishing psychological, reproductive, behavioural, and preference altruism. The authors show that much of the experimental economics literature (including public goods games with punishment) is best understood in terms of behavioural or preference altruism, not reproductive altruism. The paper cautions against conflating outcome-based and motivation-based definitions, and against assuming that findings about punishment or cooperation in economic games directly inform evolutionary or moral questions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it is useful for clarifying that many studies in experimental economics (including those on punishment in public goods games) are about behavioural or preference altruism, and that their outcomes (such as increased cooperation or punishment behavior) should not be conflated with broader evolutionary or moral claims. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that careful attention should be paid to the definitions and outcome measures used in empirical studies, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are distinct from measures of cooperation or punishment frequency.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Direct reciprocity on graphs. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of cooperation via direct reciprocity is affected by the structure of the population (graph degree k) and the update rule. For birth-death and pairwise comparison updating, structured populations make it harder for cooperation to be evolutionarily stable, but easier for cooperators to be advantageous. For death-birth and imitation updating, structured populations always facilitate cooperation. The critical benefit-to-cost ratio for cooperation to be favored depends on the probability of repeated interaction (w) and the number of neighbors (k). Errors in strategy execution, which increase with the number of neighbors, make cooperation harder to sustain unless the network is sparse (small k). The paper does not consider punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it provides theoretical results on how network structure and repeated interaction affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoffs in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means it cannot inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Its results may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated dyadic games on networks, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Competition and partnership between conformity and payoff-based imitations in social dilemmas. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the coevolution of payoff-based and conformity-based learning protocols in spatial social dilemma games can dramatically increase the parameter space where full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) is achieved, especially in the snowdrift and stag-hunt game regions. In the snowdrift game, the coevolutionary model allows for full cooperation at higher temptation and sucker's payoff values than the classic payoff-driven model. In the stag-hunt game, alliances between different types of cooperators can reverse the direction of evolution, leading to full cooperation in regions where defection would otherwise dominate. In the prisoner's dilemma, the coevolutionary model does not improve cooperation. The main outcome is that diversity in learning protocols can enhance collective payoff and efficiency, but this is achieved without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is highly informative about how alternative mechanisms (specifically, the coevolution of learning protocols) can affect efficiency and cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms other than punishment (such as conformity-based imitation) can also expand the region of high efficiency, but it does not inform the effect of punishment per se. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding the broader context of cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

In structured populations, higher network degree (more neighbors) makes cooperation harder to sustain. The analysis provides explicit formulas for the critical benefit-to-cost ratio as a function of group size, thresholds, and network structure. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

However, it provides detailed theoretical results on how group size, thresholds, and network structure affect the baseline efficiency and cooperation in a closely related multi-player game. These results may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Hilbe, C. (2011). Local Replicator Dynamics: A Simple Link Between Deterministic and Stochastic Models of Evolutionary Game Theory. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals interact and update strategies in finite groups (local replicator dynamics), the evolutionary outcome can be captured by a replicator equation with a modified payoff matrix. This approach allows the use of deterministic replicator dynamics to predict outcomes in finite populations, including the emergence of cooperation, risk aversion, and even the dominance of strategies that are not optimal in the classical sense. The model shows that group size has a significant effect: smaller groups can lead to the spread of spiteful or dominated strategies, while larger groups approximate the classical infinite-population results. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or public goods games directly, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant for understanding how group size and local updating affect efficiency and the evolution of cooperation in general evolutionary games, but it does not include punishment mechanisms or analyze public goods games with or without punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. However, its theoretical insights on group size and local updating may be contextually informative for understanding the broader dynamics of efficiency in group-structured games.

- source: Lewis, H. M., Vinicius, L., Strods, J., Mace, R., & Migliano, A. B. (2014). High mobility explains demand sharing and enforced cooperation in egalitarian hunter-gatherers. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The simulation demonstrates that enforced demand sharing (forced equal sharing of food among all camp members) combined with high mobility enables populations to survive in unpredictable environments, maintain high group efficiency (measured by survival, energy surplus, and life expectancy), and buffer against the presence of free riders. In contrast, loner (non-sharing) populations and sedentary (non-mobile) demand-sharing populations are much more likely to collapse, especially under harsh environmental conditions. The model explicitly excludes punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, showing that high efficiency and cooperation can be maintained without punishment, as long as mobility is high. The findings are directly relevant to understanding the efficiency of enforced cooperation in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the effect of adding punishment to a public-goods-like environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as punishment is not present in the model. Instead, it shows that enforced cooperation (demand sharing) combined with high mobility can maintain high efficiency and group survival without the need for punishment. For the downstream prediction task—predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency—this paper suggests that, in some environments, enforced cooperation and mobility may substitute for punishment, but it does not provide direct evidence on the marginal effect of punishment. The model is adjacent to PGGs, but the absence of a punishment condition means it cannot be used to predict the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that evolutionary game dynamics on regular graphs can be described by a replicator equation with a transformed payoff matrix, which incorporates local competition effects. For the Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games, spatial structure (graph topology) can promote or inhibit cooperation depending on the update rule and game parameters, thereby affecting the equilibrium group payoff and efficiency. The framework is general and applies to any n-strategy game, but does not address punishment, reward, or explicit public goods games. The results are most relevant for understanding how population structure alters the efficiency and stability of cooperative outcomes in evolutionary games.

However, it does not address punishment, reward, or public goods games directly, nor does it provide empirical estimates. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The model demonstrates that in spatially structured populations where survival requires occasional cooperation, increasing the cost of living or the cost of unreciprocated cooperation can promote the long-term survival and dominance of cooperators. This occurs because defectors, while initially advantaged, eventually die out when they cannot find enough cooperators to exploit, allowing cooperator clusters to recover and expand. However, if costs become too high, the population may collapse entirely. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the frequency of cooperators, not explicit group efficiency or payoff.

Its findings are relevant to understanding how environmental adversity and the cost structure of cooperation can affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatially structured populations, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency outcomes or the impact of punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions involving punishment.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2006). Evolutionary games on cycles. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary games on cycles, the update rule strongly affects whether cooperation can evolve. For the Prisoner's Dilemma, birth-death updating never favors cooperation, but death-birth and imitation updating can favor cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold (b/c > 2 for death-birth, b/c > 4 for imitation, for large N). In coordination games, death-birth and imitation updating can favor Pareto efficient outcomes over risk-dominant ones, unlike birth-death updating. The results are analytic and apply to two-strategy games on cycles, not to public goods games or punishment. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how spatial structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation in two-strategy games (such as the Prisoner's Dilemma), and discusses payoff-based concepts like risk dominance and Pareto efficiency in coordination games. However, it does not analyze punishment, sanctions, or public goods games, nor does it report group efficiency or welfare outcomes. Its findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how local interactions and update mechanisms can support cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects in public goods games.

- source: Wang, L., Jia, D. Y., Zhang, L., Zhu, P. C., Perc, M., Shi, L., & Wang, Z. (2022). Levy noise promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game with reinforcement learning. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game with reinforcement learning, introducing Lévy noise into the payoff matrix increases the fraction of cooperators in the population. The effect is stronger for Lévy noise (lower stability index α) than for Gaussian noise (α=2) or no noise. The mechanism is that noise accumulates in the Q-value updates, making cooperation more attractive over time. The results are robust to initial conditions. However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes, and the game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with Q-learning and noise. The findings may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how stochastic payoff fluctuations affect cooperation rates, but not for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

  findings: The main finding is that success-driven migration, when combined with imitation, enables the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when noise (random strategy mutation and random relocation) is present. This mechanism can lead to a sudden outbreak of predominant cooperation, even from initial conditions dominated by defectors. The results are robust across a range of payoff parameters and noise types. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

There is an optimal intermediate level of heterogeneity that maximizes cooperation, while too little or too much heterogeneity is less effective. No punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency/welfare outcomes are studied.

  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial ultimatum game, the accuracy of strategy imitation (i.e., the number of discrete choices for offers and acceptance thresholds) strongly affects the evolution of fairness. - source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2004). Strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that, contrary to previous consensus, strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups if groups persist for multiple generations. This is due to increased positive assortment (measured by the regression coefficient between actors and recipients) that arises after one or more generations within groups, even without kin selection, homogeneous groups, or population growth. The models show that smaller group size, higher benefit-to-cost ratio, and intermediate numbers of generations within groups favor the evolution of altruism. Stochasticity in group formation and population regulation can further affect the likelihood of altruism evolving. However, the models do not include punishment, reward, or explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes, and thus do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its relevance is limited to providing theoretical context about the evolution of cooperation/altruism in group-structured populations, especially regarding the effects of group persistence, assortment, and stochasticity. It does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper demonstrates that stable social groups and polarization can emerge in agent-based models where individuals imitate others who are similar to themselves (homophily), even in the absence of any explicit payoffs, rewards, or punishments. The stability of group structures depends on the rate of introspective (idiosyncratic) changes and the strength of homophilous imitation. The models are not payoff-based and do not include any public goods, cooperation, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not model or analyze any payoff, efficiency, or sanctioning mechanism, and thus provides no direct or indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes. Its findings are not transferable to the prediction task as defined.
